]
by adding to the M
0
-brane action in its spinor moving frame formulation (which then describes the center of energy sector of mM
0
) an action for matrix fields with interacting terms involving center of energy variables. These matrix fields, which are precisely the fields of 10D SU(
N
) SYM theory dimensionally reduced to
\text{d}=1

[
103
]
, where the mD
0
-brane system at very low energies is described by a maximally supersymmetric matrix model, namely the dimensional reduction of 10D SYM to
\text{d}=1
for D
0
-branes.

The resulting action
[
126

was interpreted as describing the dynamical system of multiple interacting M-waves (multiple M
0
-branes or mM
0
). Its advantages over the bosonic Myers-type 11D action in
[

,
116

are that it involves fermions and the action
[
126
]
is invariant under two types of supersymmetry: the global 11D target space supersymmetry and the local worldline supersymmetry. The latter is especially important in the framework of String/M-theory since it guarantees that the ground state of the system preserves a part (one half) of the target space supersymmetry. This, in its turn, guarantees that the ground state is a
1/2
BPS state which saturates the BPS bound, ensuring its stability and underscores their importance in the non-perturbative regime of String/M-theory.

An attempt to derive an action for the 10D mD

[

[

Another significant limitation is that both the 11D mM

The quantization of the 3D nAmW model was carried out in

Therefore, constructing and quantizing the

28
28

28

Moreover, the 4D nAmW system provides a conventional toy model for addressing an important open problem: the generalization of the 11D mM

.

2
Spinor moving frame formalism

No question; language can free us of feeling, or almost.

Maybe that’s one of its functions.

So we can understand the world without

becoming entirely overwhelmed by it.

Contact

by Carl Sagan.

The
moving frame
and
spinor moving frame
approach employed throughout this thesis involves the use of some auxiliary variables known as moving frame vectors and their spinor moving frame companions. In this section we will introduce this formalism starting from the first order form of 4D version of the Brink-Schwarz action. Notice that it is also relevant to the so-called
superembedding approach

[
136
,
137
]
, a geometrical description of the dynamics of supersymmetric point-like and extended objects in String Theory (see
[
138
,
139
,
140
]
for reviews and references).

Here we present this formalism starting for the case of
\text{D}=4

\mathcal{N}=1
massless superparticle which serves as a prelude of the chapter
3
, where the 4D counterpart of the 11-dimensional system of multiple M
0
-branes is presented and described. Notice that the action for this latter system is known only in spinor moving frame formulation.

2.1

Massless superparticle in different formulations

In this section we study the action of
\text{D}=4

\mathcal{N}=1
massless superparticle (a counterpart of the 11D single M
0
-brane
[
94
]
, also known as M-wave) in the framework of spinor moving frame formulation.

2.1.1

Massless superparticle in D=4
\mathcal{N}
=1 superspace

The first order form of the Brink-Schwarz action for
\text{D}=4

\mathcal{N}=1
massless superparticle
[
141
]
reads

S_{\text{BS}}=\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\left(p_{\mu}\hat{E}^{\mu}-\frac{e}{2}p_{\mu}p^{\mu}\right)=\int\text{d}\tau\left(p_{\mu}\hat{E}^{\mu}_{\tau}-\frac{e}{2}p_{\mu}p^{\mu}\right)\penalty\ ,

(2.1)

where
\mu=0,1,2,3
is the vector index of the Lorentz group SO
(1,3)
and
e=e(\tau)
is an auxiliary einbein field whose variation imposes the mass shell condition
p_{\mu}p^{\mu}=0
for the auxiliary momentum variable
p_{\mu}=p_{\mu}(\tau)
and

\hat{E}^{\mu}=\hat{E}^{\mu}(\hat{z})=\text{d}\tau\hat{E}^{\mu}_{\tau}=\text{d}\hat{z}^{M}\hat{E}_{M}^{\mu}(\hat{z})\penalty\ ,

(2.2)

is the pull-back of the bosonic supervielbein of the 4D target superspace to the worldline parametrised by proper time
\tau
. In this thesis, we restrict ourselves by the case of flat target superspace; the bosonic supervielbein in this case (also known as Volkov-Akulov (VA) 1-form
[
49
,
50
]
) has the form

E^{\mu}=\Pi^{\mu}=\text{d}x^{\mu}-i\text{d}\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}+i\theta\sigma^{\mu}\text{d}\bar{\theta}=\text{d}x^{\mu}-i\text{d}\theta^{\alpha}\sigma_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}^{\mu}\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}+i\theta^{\alpha}\sigma_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}^{\mu}\text{d}\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}\penalty\ ,

(2.3)

where
z^{M}=(x^{\mu},\theta^{\alpha},\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}})
are
\text{D}=4

\mathcal{N}=1
superspace coordinates carrying vector and Weyl spinor indices
\alpha=1,2
and
\dot{\alpha}=1,2
. Here and below we use the relativistic Pauli matrices

\sigma_{\mu}=\sigma_{\mu\alpha\dot{\beta}}=(\mathbb{1},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3})_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}=\tilde{\sigma}^{\mu\dot{\alpha}\beta}:=\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\epsilon^{\beta\alpha}\sigma_{\nu\alpha\dot{\beta}}\eta^{\nu\mu}\penalty\ ,

which obey

(\sigma_{\mu}\tilde{\sigma}_{\nu}+\sigma_{\nu}\tilde{\sigma}_{\mu})_{\alpha}^{\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \beta}=2\eta_{\mu\nu}\delta_{\alpha}^{\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \beta}\penalty\ ,\qquad(\tilde{\sigma}_{\mu}\sigma_{\nu}+\tilde{\sigma}_{\nu}\sigma_{\mu})^{\dot{\alpha}}_{\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \dot{\beta}}=2\eta_{\mu\nu}\delta^{\dot{\alpha}}_{\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \dot{\beta}}\penalty\ ,

(2.4)

\sigma_{\mu\penalty\ \alpha\dot{\beta}}\tilde{\sigma}^{\mu\penalty\ \beta\dot{\alpha}}=2\delta_{\alpha}^{\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \beta}\delta^{\dot{\alpha}}_{\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \dot{\beta}}\penalty\ .

(2.5)

The spinorial indices are raised and lowered by the unit antisymmetric tensor

\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}=-\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}=-\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}=\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\penalty\

“acting from the left”, i.e.
\theta^{\alpha}=\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\theta_{\beta}
,
\theta_{\alpha}=\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\theta^{\beta}
and
\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}=\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\bar{\theta}_{\dot{\beta}}
,
\bar{\theta}_{\dot{\alpha}}=\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\beta}}
.

The pull-back of the Volkov-Akulov (VA) 1-form to the worldline is proportional to differential of proper time
\tau
:

\hat{\Pi}^{\mu}=\text{d}\tau\hat{\Pi}^{\mu}_{\tau}\penalty\ ,\qquad\hat{\Pi}_{\tau}^{\mu}=\hat{\Pi}_{\tau}^{\mu}(\hat{z})=\partial_{\tau}\hat{x}^{\mu}-i\partial_{\tau}\hat{\theta}\sigma^{\mu}\hat{\bar{\theta}}+i\hat{\theta}\sigma^{\mu}\partial_{\tau}\hat{\bar{\theta}}\penalty\ .

(2.6)

It is obtained from (
2.3
) by replacing the coordinates
x^{\mu}
and
\theta^{\alpha}
,
\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}
by bosonic and fermionic coordinate functions
\hat{x}^{\mu}(\tau)
and
\hat{\theta}^{\alpha}(\tau)
,
\hat{\bar{\theta}}^{\dot{\alpha}}(\tau)
, respectively,

z^{M}=(x^{\mu},\theta^{\alpha},\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}})\quad\mapsto\quad\hat{z}^{M}(\tau)=(\hat{x}^{\mu}(\tau),\hat{\theta}^{\alpha}(\tau),\hat{\bar{\theta}}^{\dot{\alpha}}(\tau))\penalty\ .

(2.7)

The coordinates functions are used to define parametrically the embedding of the superparticle worldline
\mathcal{W}^{1}
into
\text{D}=4

\mathcal{N}=1
superspace
\Sigma^{(4|4)}
,

\mathcal{W}^{1}\subset\Sigma^{(4|4)}:\qquad z^{M}=\hat{z}^{M}(\tau)\penalty\ .

(2.8)

Below we will omit the hat symbol
\textasciicircum
from the coordinate functions and the pull-back of the forms in order to simplify equations. Moreover, we prefer to hide
d
\tau
inside of differential form, in particular to define the Lagrangian 1-form
\mathcal{L}=\text{d}\tau\mathcal{L}_{\tau}
, and write our actions as integral of this 1-form over the worldline
\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\mathcal{L}
rather than as an integral over
\text{d}\tau
of a Lagrangian,
\int\text{d}\tau\mathcal{L}_{\tau}
.

2.1.2

Massless superparticle in twistor-like formulation

The variation of worldline einbein
e(\tau)
produces the mass shell constraint
p^{\mu}p_{\mu}=0
. Exploiting this fact it is possible to write the action (
2.1
) in an equivalent form by solving this algebraic equation and substituting the solution back.

Let us introduce the bosonic spinor fields
\lambda
and its c.c.
\bar{\lambda}

\lambda^{\alpha}=\lambda^{\alpha}(\tau)\penalty\ ,\qquad\bar{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}=\bar{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}(\tau)\penalty\ .

(2.9)

The mass shell constraint
p^{\mu}p_{\mu}=0
can be easily solved using these bosonic spinors
30
30

30

Indeed, as bosonic spinors
\lambda^{\alpha}\lambda^{\beta}=\lambda^{\beta}\lambda^{\alpha}\penalty\ ,
we find

\lambda^{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}=\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\lambda^{\alpha}\lambda^{\beta}=\epsilon_{[\alpha\beta]}\lambda^{\left(\alpha\right.}\lambda^{\left.\beta\right)}\equiv 0\penalty\

and then

p_{\mu}p^{\mu}=\lambda^{\alpha}\bar{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}\lambda^{\beta}\bar{\lambda}^{\dot{\beta}}\sigma_{\mu\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\sigma^{\mu}_{\beta\dot{\beta}}=\lambda^{\alpha}\bar{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}\lambda^{\beta}\bar{\lambda}^{\dot{\beta}}(2\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}})=2\epsilon_{[\alpha\beta]}\lambda^{\left(\alpha\right.}\lambda^{\left.\beta\right)}\epsilon_{[\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}]}\bar{\lambda}^{\left(\dot{\alpha}\right.}\bar{\lambda}^{\dot{\beta})}\equiv 0\penalty\ .

p_{\mu}=\lambda^{\alpha}\sigma_{\mu\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}\penalty\ .

(2.10)

Substituting this solution back into (
2.1
), we arrive at a new equivalent form of the superparticle action

S_{\text{FS}}=\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\lambda^{\alpha}\sigma_{\mu\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}\Pi^{\mu}\penalty\ ,

(2.11)

which is known as twistor-like or Ferber-Shirafuji formulation
[
142
,
143
]
of the massless superparticle action.

2.1.3

Moving frame formulation of the massless superparticle

In this section we present the action of the same system in a one more equivalent form. This new form is quiet close to the Ferber-Shirafuji formulation but it provides a much convenient basis for the study of the multiple branes systems, the main subject of this thesis.

Let us begin by considering a solution of the mass shell condition
p_{\mu}p^{\mu}=0
in a special reference frame,

P_{(a)}=(1,0,0,-1)\rho\penalty\ .

(2.12)

Clearly the vector field
P_{(a)}=P_{(a)}(\tau)
is light-like and
\rho=\rho(\tau)
describes the energy of the massless particle. The solution of the mass shell condition in an arbitrary frame can be obtained from (
2.12
) by making a Lorentz transformation

p_{\mu}:=U_{\mu}^{(a)}P_{(a)}=U_{\mu}^{(a)}(1,0,0,-1)\rho=:u_{\mu}^{=}\rho^{\#}\penalty\ .

(2.13)

Here

U_{\mu}^{(a)}:=\left(\frac{u^{\#}_{\mu}+u^{=}_{\mu}}{2},U^{i}_{\mu},\frac{u^{\#}_{\mu}-u^{=}_{\mu}}{2}\right)\in\text{SO}(1,3)

(2.14)

is a Lorentz group valued matrix field whose elements are known as
moving frame variables
(also referred to as
Lorentz harmonics

[
144
]
; see section
2.1.4
for more details on the connection between these terms) and
i=1,2
. Eq. (
2.14
) implies that the elements of
U_{\mu}^{(a)}=U_{\mu}^{(a)}(\tau)
obey the constraints
[
146
,
147
]

U_{\mu}^{(a)}U^{\mu(b)}=\eta^{ab}=\text{diag}(1,-1,-1,-1)\penalty\ ,

(2.15)

which can be split into

\begin{array}[]{rcrcr}u^{=}_{\mu}u^{\mu=}=0\penalty\ ,&&u^{\#}_{\mu}u^{\mu\#}=0\penalty\ ,&&u^{=}_{\mu}u^{\mu\#}=2\penalty\ ,\\
\penalty\ \penalty\ u^{=}_{\mu}U^{\mu i}=0\penalty\ ,&&\penalty\ u^{\#}_{\mu}U^{\mu i}=0\penalty\ ,&&U^{i}_{\mu}U^{\mu j}=-\delta^{ij}\penalty\ .\end{array}

(2.16)

The relations (
2.16
) are invariant under
\text{SO}(\text{D}-2)=\text{SO}(2)
symmetry action on
i,j
indices and SO
(1,1)
symmetry action on light-like vectors as indicated by their sign indices
u_{\mu}^{=}=u_{\mu}^{--}
,
u_{\mu}^{\#}=u_{\mu}^{++}
. In (
2.13
) we have supplied the energy variable
\rho
of (
2.12
) with
\#
index, indicating its transformations with respect to SO
(1,1)
symmetry defined in such a way that (
2.13
) remains invariant. Let us stress that, as
p_{\mu}=p_{\mu}(\tau)
was a dynamical variable in the superparticle action (
2.1
), such is the Lorentz group matrix valued
U_{\mu}^{(a)}=U_{\mu}^{(a)}(\tau)
in
(
2.13
)
.

Substituting
(
2.13
)
, we can rewrite the Brink-Schwarz action (
2.1
) in the following equivalent form

S_{\text{M}0}=\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\rho^{\#}u_{\mu}^{=}\Pi^{\mu}=\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\rho^{\#}\text{E}^{=}\penalty\ ,

(2.17)

which corresponds to the action of massless superparticle in
moving frame formalism
. In the second form of (
2.17
) we have introduced the notation

\text{E}^{=}=u_{\mu}^{=}\Pi^{\mu}

(2.18)

which will be useful below.

For our purposes it is interesting to go one step further: the above analysis shows that the superparticle action in this moving frame formalism “hides” a more useful
spinor moving frame formulation
.

2.1.4

Moving frame and spinor moving frame for massless superparticle in D=4

In
\text{D}=4
the spinor moving frame is constructed from a pair of nonvanishing complex spinors
v^{\pm}=(\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{\mp})^{*}
that satisfy the normalization conditions

v^{\alpha-}v_{\alpha}^{+}=1\;,\qquad\bar{v}^{\dot{\alpha}-}\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{+}=1\;.

(2.19)

These constraints ensure that the matrix formed by the columns
v_{\alpha}^{+}
,
v_{\alpha}^{-}
(and its c.c. formed by
\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{+}
,
\bar{v}_{\bar{\alpha}}^{-}
) is unimodular and thus an element of the SL
(2,{\mathbb{C}})
group
31
31

31

The spinor frame variables are complexification of the
harmonic variables of
{\cal N}=2
harmonic superspace approach to the off-shell description of the

{\cal N}=2
SYM, matter and supergravity in terms of unconstrained superfields
[
152
,
153
,
154
]
. Hence the name of Lorentz harmonics used in
[
144
]
.

\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}v_{\alpha}^{+}v_{\beta}^{-}=1\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad V_{\alpha}^{(\beta)}:=(v_{\alpha}^{+},v_{\alpha}^{-})\;\in\;\text{SL}(2,{\mathbb{C}})\;.

(2.20)

This matrix is referred to as the
spinor (moving) frame matrix
, and it can be regarded as a kind of “square root” of a Lorentz vector frame, described by the Lorentz group valued
moving frame matrix
(
2.14
)

U_{\mu}^{(a)}:=\left(\frac{u^{\#}_{\mu}+u^{=}_{\mu}}{2},U^{i}_{\mu},\frac{u^{\#}_{\mu}-u^{=}_{\mu}}{2}\right)\in\text{SO}(1,3)

in the following sense. The real light-like vectors
u^{=}_{\mu}
and
u^{\#}_{\mu}
of (
2.14
) can be constructed from
v_{\alpha}^{-},\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{-}
and
v_{\alpha}^{+},\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{+}
, respectively, by the Cartan-Penrose representation similar to (
2.10
),

u^{=}_{\mu}=v^{-}\sigma_{\mu}\bar{v}^{-}\;,\qquad u^{\#}_{\mu}=v^{+}\sigma_{\mu}\bar{v}^{+}\;.

(2.21)

Then, to proceed further, it is convenient to express also orthogonal and normalized space-like vectors
U^{(i)}_{\mu}=(U^{1}_{\mu},U^{2}_{\mu})
in (
2.14
) in terms of complex light-like vectors

u_{\mu}^{+-}=\frac{1}{2}(U^{1}_{\mu}+iU^{2}_{\mu})\penalty\ ,\qquad u_{\mu}^{-+}=\frac{1}{2}(U^{1}_{\mu}-iU^{2}_{\mu})=(u_{\mu}^{+-})^{*}\penalty\ .

(2.22)

For these, the relation with spinor moving frame variables are given by

\begin{array}[]{ccc}u^{+-}_{\mu}=v^{\alpha+}\sigma_{\mu\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\bar{v}^{\dot{\alpha}-}\penalty\ ,&&u^{-+}_{\mu}=v^{\alpha-}\sigma_{\mu\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\bar{v}^{\dot{\alpha}+}=(u_{\mu}^{+-})^{*}\penalty\ .\end{array}

(2.23)

It is easy to check that the vectors (
2.21
) and (
2.22
) are light-like

u_{\mu}^{=}u^{\mu=}=0\penalty\ ,\qquad u_{\mu}^{\#}u^{\mu\#}=0\penalty\ ,\qquad u_{\mu}^{-+}u^{\mu-+}=0\penalty\ ,\qquad u_{\mu}^{+-}u^{\mu+-}=0\penalty\

(2.24)

and, as a result of (
2.19
), are normalized by

u^{=}_{\mu}u^{\mu\#}=2\;,\qquad u^{+-}_{\mu}u^{\mu\,-+}=-2\;.

(2.25)

All other contraction of these vectors vanish, so that (
2.15
) and hence (
2.14
) hold

\begin{array}[]{c}U_{\mu}^{(a)}\in\text{SO}(1,3)\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad U_{\mu}^{(a)}U^{\mu(b)}=\eta^{ab}=\rm{diag}(+1,-1,-1,-1)\;,\\
\\
u^{\#}_{\mu}=U_{\mu}^{(0)}-U_{\mu}^{(3)}\;,\qquad u^{=}_{\mu}=U_{\mu}^{(0)}+U_{\mu}^{(3)}\;,\qquad u^{\mp\pm}_{\mu}=-U_{\mu}^{1}\pm iU_{\mu}^{2}\;.\end{array}

(2.26)

Actually, the light-like moving frame vectors form a complete light-like tetrad of the Newman-Penrose formalism
[
155
]
(see also
[
156
]
).

It is important that (
2.21
) and (
2.22
) can be written in an equivalent matrix form relations (see (
2.5
))

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\penalty\ u_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}^{=}=u^{=}_{\mu}{\sigma}^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}=2v_{\alpha}^{-}\bar{v}_{\dot{\beta}}^{-}\penalty\ ,&&\penalty\ \penalty\ u_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}^{\#}=u^{\#}_{\mu}{\sigma}^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}=2v_{\alpha}^{+}\bar{v}_{\dot{\beta}}^{+}\penalty\ ,\\
\\
u_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}^{-+}=u^{-+}_{\mu}{\sigma}^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}=2v_{\alpha}^{-}\bar{v}_{\dot{\beta}}^{+}\penalty\ ,&&\penalty\ u_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}^{+-}=u^{+-}_{\mu}{\sigma}^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}=2v_{\alpha}^{+}\bar{v}_{\dot{\beta}}^{-}=(u_{\beta\dot{\alpha}}^{-+})^{*}\penalty\ .\end{array}

(2.27)

Substituting the expression from (
2.21
) for
u_{\mu}^{=}
in (
2.17
), we arrive at the massless superparticle action in its spinor moving frame formulation
[
144
]

S_{\text{M}0}=\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\rho^{\#}u_{\mu}^{=}\Pi^{\mu}=\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\rho^{\#}v^{\alpha-}\sigma_{\mu\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\bar{v}^{\dot{\alpha}-}\Pi^{\mu}\penalty\ .

(2.28)

It is not difficult to see that a simple change of bosonic spinor variables

\lambda_{\alpha}=\sqrt{\rho^{\#}}v_{\alpha}^{-}\penalty\ ,\qquad\bar{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}=\sqrt{\rho^{\#}}\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{-}\penalty\

(2.29)

converts (
2.28
) into the Ferber-Shirafuji action (
2.11
),

S_{\text{FS}}=\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\lambda^{\alpha}\sigma_{\mu\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}\Pi^{\mu}\penalty\ .

At first glance, this reformulation of the Ferber-Schirafuji action (
2.11
) might appear to be trivial: the only difference seems to be the inclusion of the Stückelberg field
\rho^{\#}
, which introduces a scale gauge symmetry. However, the spinor moving frame approach goes far beyond this. It is rather a method to deal with Ferber-Schirafuji-like formulation of superparticle, allowing for systematic generalization to higher dimensions, to extended objects such as
p
-branes, and for the construction of complete doubly supersymmetric actions for systems like the mM
0
and mD
0
.

The group-theoretical structure beyond the spinor moving frame variables, in the present case including
v_{\alpha}^{-}
and its partner
v_{\alpha}^{+}
obeying
v^{\alpha-}v_{\alpha}^{+}=1
, is essential to this generalizations, and the gauge invariance under SO
(1,1)
subgroup of SO
(1,\text{D}-1)=\text{SO}(1,3)
implemented in (
2.28
) is essential to treat these as generalized homogeneous coordinates of celestial sphere
\mathbb{S}^{\text{D}-2}=\mathbb{S}^{2}
.

Notice also the possibility to supplement the action (
2.28
) by a kind of bosonic Wess-Zumino term constructed from the forms
v^{\alpha-}\text{d}v_{\alpha}^{+}
and
\bar{v}^{\dot{\alpha}-}\text{d}\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{+}
which have the properties of connection under SO
(1,1)\otimes\text{SO}(2)
gauge transformation
[
144
]
. These described the (super)helicity of massless superparticle at the (quasi-)classical level
[
144
,
145
]
.

2.1.5

Local fermionic
\kappa
-symmetry

To derive the equation of motions as well as to analyse the symmetries of a dynamical system, one studies the variation of the action with respect to all independent variables
[
148
]
. While we will not delve into the general details here, we are particularly interested in a specific local fermionic symmetry characteristic for supersymmetric particles and extended objects. Let us consider the variations
\delta_{\kappa}
obeying
\iota_{\delta}\Pi^{\mu}=\delta x^{\mu}-i\delta\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}+i\theta\sigma^{\mu}\delta\bar{\theta}=0
,
32
32

32

\iota_{\delta}\Pi^{\mu}
corresponds to (
2.3
) but with replacement
\text{d}\mapsto\delta
.
which leads to the following transformation of the bosonic coordinate function in terms of the fermionic ones,

\iota_{\kappa}\Pi^{\mu}\equiv\iota_{\delta_{\kappa}}\Pi^{\mu}=0\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\delta_{\kappa}x^{\mu}=i\delta_{\kappa}\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}-i\theta\sigma^{\mu}\delta_{\kappa}\bar{\theta}\penalty\ .

(2.30)

Furthermore, imposing
\iota_{\kappa}\Pi^{\mu}=0
in the variation of the action and requiring vanishing of this latter determines the complete set of
\delta_{\kappa}
transformations for the remaining variables. The local fermionic gauge symmetry thus obtained is known as
\kappa
-symmetry
33
33

33

The
\kappa
-symmetry was discovered in
[
135
]
and
[
149
]
, and was shown to be equivalent with worldline supersymmetry in
[
132
]
.
.

Such a variation (with
\iota_{\kappa}\Pi^{\mu}=0
) of 4D Brink-Schwarz action (
2.1
) gives

\delta_{\kappa}S_{\text{BS}}=\int\text{d}\tau\left[\delta_{\kappa}p_{\mu}\left(\Pi_{\tau}^{\mu}-ep^{\mu}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\kappa}ep_{\mu}p^{\mu}-2ip_{\mu}\partial_{\tau}\theta\sigma^{\mu}\delta_{\kappa}\bar{\theta}+2ip_{\mu}\delta_{\kappa}\theta\sigma^{\mu}\partial_{\tau}\bar{\theta}\right]\penalty\ .

(2.31)

Studying (
2.31
) one observes that, for
\delta_{\kappa}\theta^{\alpha}=\bar{\kappa}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\sigma}^{\nu\dot{\alpha}\alpha}p_{\nu}=\bar{\kappa}\bar{\sigma}^{\nu}p_{\nu}
, the contribution from variation of coordinates functions can be compensated by some
\delta_{\kappa}e(\tau)
. This implies that (
2.1
) is invariant under followed local fermionic
\kappa
-symmetry transformations

\begin{array}[]{lclcr}\delta_{\kappa}x^{\mu}=i\delta_{\kappa}\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}-i\theta\sigma^{\mu}\delta_{\kappa}\bar{\theta}\penalty\ ,&&\delta_{\kappa}\theta^{\alpha}=\bar{\kappa}\bar{\sigma}^{\nu}p_{\nu}\penalty\ ,&&\delta_{\kappa}\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}=\kappa\sigma^{\nu}p_{\nu}\penalty\ ,\\
\delta_{\kappa}e=4i(\bar{\kappa}\text{d}\bar{\theta}-\text{d}\theta\kappa)\penalty\ ,&&\delta_{\kappa}p_{\mu}=0\penalty\ .\end{array}

(2.32)

This symmetry is important because it guarantees that the ground state of the system is invariant under
1/2
of spacetime supersymmetry (see
[
150
,
151
]
and refs. therein) and a stable so-called BPS state. Moreover, the
\kappa
-symmetry can be used to remove half of the components of
\theta
(and its c.c.
\bar{\theta}
)
[
135
]
which equates the number of bosonic and fermionic degrees of freedom
34
34

34

The equality of degrees of freedom is a necessary condition for linearly realized supersymmetry in
d>1
, so that massless superparticle action can be written in (super)space of any (bosonic) dimension
D
. This balance however is necessary for superstring and higher
p
-branes.
.

To see this, one should observe that
\kappa
-symmetry of Brink-Schwarz action is
infinitely reducible
. Indeed, with
p_{\mu}p^{\mu}=0
one can check that
\kappa_{\alpha}
and
\kappa_{\alpha}+p_{\mu}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\kappa}^{(1)\dot{\alpha}}
(with an arbitrary
\bar{\kappa}^{(1){\dot{\alpha}}}=\bar{\kappa}^{(1)\dot{\alpha}}(\tau)
) generate the same
\kappa
-symmetry transformations (
2.32
). This is usually expressed by saying that
\delta_{\kappa}
has a null vector
\bar{\kappa}^{(1)\dot{\alpha}}
and hence that
\kappa
-symmetry is
reducible
. But moreover, it can be easily observed that
\bar{\kappa}^{(1)\dot{\alpha}}
and
\bar{\kappa}^{(1)\dot{\alpha}}+p_{\mu}\bar{\sigma}^{a\dot{\alpha}\beta}\kappa^{(2)}_{\beta}
(with an arbitrary
\kappa_{\beta}^{(2)}=\kappa_{\beta}^{(2)}(\tau)
) lead to the same variation of the parameter
\kappa_{\alpha}
, indicating a second order of reducibility for this
\kappa
-symmetry (existence of null-vector for null-vector). Furthermore, this process can be continued infinitely many times, the fact which is referred to as
infinitely reducible
nature of the
\kappa
-symmetry of the Brink-Schwarz action for 4D massless superparticle.

As we mentioned above,
\kappa
-symmetry can be used to removed fermionic degrees of freedom. This happens exactly because of
\infty
-reducibility because the number of the components of
\theta^{\alpha}
,
\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}
, and of the
\kappa
-symmetry parameter is the same. The same number of components have null-vector, null-vector for null-vector, and so on. So that, in this case, the number of the removed degrees of freedom is calculated as the infinite sum

4-4+4-\ldots=4\cdot(1-1+1-\ldots)=4\cdot\lim_{q\to 1}(1-q+q^{2}-\ldots)=4\cdot\lim_{q\to 1}\frac{1}{1+q}=4\cdot\dfrac{1}{2}=2\penalty\ ,

(2.33)

illustrating how only two of four fermionic degrees of freedom of massless superparticle are physically relevant.

In contrast, the massless superparticle action in spinor moving frame formalism
35
35

35

The conclusions taking the Ferber-Shirafuji representation of (
2.11
) are similar.
(
2.17
) has a manifestly
irreducible
form of
\kappa
-symmetry

\begin{array}[]{lclcl}\delta_{\kappa}x^{\mu}=i\delta_{\kappa}\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}-i\theta\sigma^{\mu}\delta_{\kappa}\bar{\theta}\penalty\ ,&&\delta_{\kappa}\theta^{\alpha}=\epsilon^{+}v^{{\alpha}-}\penalty\ ,&&\delta_{\kappa}\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}=\bar{\epsilon}^{+}\bar{v}^{\dot{\alpha}-}\penalty\ ,\\
\delta_{\kappa}v^{\alpha\mp}=0\penalty\ ,&&\delta_{\kappa}\rho^{\#}=0\penalty\ ,\end{array}

(2.34)

parametrised by
2
fermionic parameters
\epsilon^{+}=\epsilon^{+}(\tau)
and
\bar{\epsilon}^{+}=\bar{\epsilon}^{+}(\tau)
. Then each of these
\epsilon^{+}
,
\bar{\epsilon}^{+}
can be used to reduce one half of the number of degrees of freedom of fermionic coordinate functions
\theta^{\alpha}(\tau)
and
\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}(\tau)
. This latter fact does not surprise us since the actions in Eqs. (
2.1
) and (
2.28
) are classically equivalent. In particular, it is straightforward to find the relation between parameters of
\bar{\kappa}^{\dot{\alpha}}
of infinitely reducible
\kappa
-symmetry (
2.32
) and
\epsilon^{+}
irreducible
\kappa
-symmetry of the spinor moving frame formulation. Substituting (
2.13
) into (
2.32
) we find (
2.34
) with

\epsilon^{+}=2\rho^{\#}\bar{v}^{\dot{\alpha}-}\bar{\kappa}_{\dot{\alpha}}\penalty\ .

(2.35)

This discussion demonstrates one of the advantages of spinor moving frame (or twistor-like) formalism: in its framework the
\kappa
-symmetry of superparticles and super-
p
-branes acquire an irreducible form
[
157
,
158
,
159
,
160
]
. This irreducibility makes it more suitable for quantization and reinforces the identification of
\kappa
-symmetry with local worldline/worldsheet/worldvolume supersymmetry of superembedding approach
[
136
,
138
,
139
,
140
]
through the generalized action approach of
[
161
]
.

Let us stress that this relation, as well as the transformation rules of the irreducible
\kappa
-symmetry (
3.11
), necessarily involves the constrained spinors
v^{\alpha-}
and
\bar{v}^{\dot{\alpha}-}
. Thus the irreducible form of the
\kappa
-symmetry is a characteristic property of the spinor moving frame and other “twistor-like” approaches to superparticle dynamics.

In our case, the spinor moving frame variables are strictly necessary ingredients of the complete multiple D
0
-brane and mM
0
actions which are not known in other formulations.

3
4D counterpart of multiple M-wave system

Journey before destination… It cannot be a journey if

it doesn’t have a beginning.

The Stormlight Archive III: Oathbringer

by Brandon Sanderson.

In this chapter we construct the action of
\text{D}=4

\mathcal{N}=1
non-Abelian multiwave (nAmW) system in flat superspace and prove its invariance under local worldline supersymmetry, which generalizes the so-called
\kappa
-symmetry of the massless superparticle. This
\text{D}=4

\mathcal{N}=1
nAmW system, a lower dimensional counterpart of 11D multiple M
0
-brane (multiple M-wave or mM
0
) system, provides a toy model for developing the methods applicable to the study of its more complex higher-dimensional counterpart.

3.1

D
=4
\mathcal{N}
=1 massless superparticle

In this section we begin by coming back to the spinor moving frame formulation of the massless superparticle in
\text{D}=4

\mathcal{N}=1
superspace since its action will be used to describe the center of energy sector of the complete nAmW system.

Using (
2.5
), we can rewrite this action as

S^{4\text{D}}_{0}=\int_{{\cal W}^{1}}\rho^{\#}v_{\alpha}^{-}\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{-}\Pi^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=\int\text{d}\tau\rho^{\#}(\tau)v_{\alpha}^{-}(\tau)\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{-}(\tau)\Pi_{\tau}^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\;,

(3.1)

where

\Pi^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=\text{d}\tau\Pi_{\tau}^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\;,\qquad\Pi_{\tau}^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=\partial_{\tau}x^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}(\tau)-2i\partial_{\tau}\theta^{\alpha}(\tau)\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}(\tau)+2i\theta^{\alpha}(\tau)\partial_{\tau}\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}(\tau)\;\qquad

(3.2)

is the pull-back to the superparticle worldline of the VA 1-form

\Pi^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}:=\Pi^{\mu}\tilde{\sigma}_{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}\;=\text{d}x^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}(\tau)-2i\text{d}\theta^{\alpha}(\tau)\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}(\tau)+2i\theta^{\alpha}(\tau)\text{d}\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}(\tau)\penalty\ ,

(3.3)

x^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}(\tau)=x^{\mu}(\tau)\tilde{\sigma}_{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}
and

\theta^{\alpha}(\tau)=(\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}(\tau))^{*}
are bosonic vector and fermionic spinor coordinate functions of proper time
\tau
which define embedding of the superparticle worldline
{\cal W}^{1}
in
\text{D}=4

\mathcal{N}=1
superspace
\Sigma^{(4|4)}
,

{\cal W}^{1}\in\Sigma^{(4|4)}\;:\qquad x^{\mu}=x^{\mu}(\tau)\;,\qquad\theta^{\alpha}=\theta^{\alpha}(\tau)\;,\qquad\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}=\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}(\tau)\penalty\ .

(3.4)

To simplify the notation, we use the same symbols for coordinate functions and coordinates (e.g.
x^{\mu}(\tau)
and
x^{\mu}
) as well as for the pull-backs of differential forms to the worldline
{\cal W}^{1}
and the forms on target superspace
\Sigma^{(4|4)}
(see e.g. (
3.2
) and (
3.3
)).

The bosonic spinor fields
v_{\alpha}^{-}
in (
3.1
) are constrained by (
2.19
) and define a spinor moving frame attached to the worldline.
Using (
2.21
) and (
2.27
), we can rewrite (
3.1
) in the following equivalent forms

S_{0}^{4\text{D}}=\int_{{\cal W}^{1}}\rho^{\#}\frac{1}{2}u_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}^{=}\Pi^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=\int_{{\cal W}^{1}}\rho^{\#}u_{\mu}^{=}\Pi^{\mu}=\int_{{\cal W}^{1}}\rho^{\#}\text{E}^{=}\;,

(3.5)

where, at the last stage, we have introduced one of the pull-backs of the 1-forms of supervielbein adapted to the embedding of worldline in superspace, bosonic

\text{E}^{=}=\Pi^{\mu}u_{\mu}^{=}=\frac{1}{2}u_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}^{=}\Pi^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=\Pi^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}v_{\alpha}^{-}\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{-}\penalty\ ,

(3.6)

\text{E}^{\#}=\Pi^{\mu}u_{\mu}^{\#}=\frac{1}{2}u_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}^{\#}\Pi^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=\Pi^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}v_{\alpha}^{+}\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{+}\penalty\ ,

(3.7)

\text{E}^{-+}=\Pi^{\mu}u_{\mu}^{-+}=\frac{1}{2}u_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}^{-+}\Pi^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=\Pi^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}v_{\alpha}^{-}\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{+}=(\text{E}^{-+})^{*}\,,

(3.8)

and fermionic

\text{E}^{\mp}=\text{d}\theta^{\alpha}v_{\alpha}^{\mp}\;,\qquad\bar{\text{E}}^{\mp}=\text{d}\bar{\theta}{}^{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{v}{}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{\mp}\;.

(3.9)

The first two real forms,
\text{E}^{=}
and
\text{E}^{\#}
, will be used below to write the 4D nAmW action.

The action (
3.1
) is manifestly invariant under
\text{D}=4

{\cal N}=1
spacetime supersymmetry which leaves the spinor moving frame variables inert and acts on bosonic and fermionic coordinates functions by

\delta_{\varepsilon}x^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=2i\theta^{\alpha}\bar{\varepsilon}^{\dot{\alpha}}-2i\varepsilon^{\alpha}\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}\penalty\ ,\qquad\delta_{\varepsilon}\theta^{\alpha}=\varepsilon^{\alpha}\penalty\ ,\qquad\delta_{\varepsilon}\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}=\bar{\varepsilon}^{\dot{\alpha}}\penalty\ ,

(3.10)

with constant fermionic spinor parameter
\varepsilon^{\alpha}

=

(\bar{\varepsilon}^{\dot{\alpha}})^{*}
. It also possesses the local fermionic
\kappa
-symmetry
in its irreducible form (as it is described in section
2.1.5
)

\begin{array}[]{lclcl}\delta_{\kappa}x^{\mu}=i\delta_{\kappa}\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}-i\theta\sigma^{\mu}\delta_{\kappa}\bar{\theta}\penalty\ ,&&\delta_{\kappa}\theta^{\alpha}=\kappa^{+}v^{{\alpha}-}\penalty\ ,&&\delta_{\kappa}\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}=\bar{\kappa}^{+}\bar{v}^{\dot{\alpha}-}\penalty\ ,\\
\delta_{\kappa}v_{\alpha}^{\mp}=0\penalty\ ,&&\delta_{\kappa}\rho^{\#}=0\penalty\ .\end{array}

(3.11)

3.1.1

Admissible variations of spinor moving frame variables and Cartan forms

To vary the action and to clarify the structure of equations of motion, one must also vary and differentiate the moving frame and spinor moving frame variables. Although these variables obey algebraic constraints, the underlying group theoretic structure beyond them makes their differential calculus and variational problem quite simple. In fact, all differentials and variations of these fields can be expressed in terms of the SO
(1,3)
valued Cartan forms and their variational counterparts.

As spinor frame variables are constrained by (
2.19
), it is convenient to search for “admissible” variations which preserve these conditions. The same applies to the derivatives of spinor moving frame variables. The admissible derivatives preserving (
2.19
) can be expressed through the so-called Cartan forms of SO
(1,3)
group. Since there are four complex variables in
v_{\alpha}^{\pm}
and one complex condition in (
2.19
), we can expect that there are three independent complex derivatives of spinor moving frame. These are related to three complex Cartan forms and their conjugates

\Omega^{--}=v^{\alpha-}\text{d}v_{\alpha}^{-}\;,\qquad\bar{\Omega}{}^{--}=\bar{v}^{\dot{\alpha}-}\text{d}\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{-}\;,

(3.12)

\Omega^{++}=v^{\alpha+}\text{d}v_{\alpha}^{+}\;,\qquad\bar{\Omega}{}^{++}=\bar{v}^{\dot{\alpha}+}\text{d}\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{+}\;,

(3.13)

\penalty\ \omega^{(0)}=v^{\alpha-}\text{d}v_{\alpha}^{+}\;,\penalty\ \penalty\ \qquad\bar{\omega}{}^{(0)}=\bar{v}^{\dot{\alpha}-}\text{d}\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{+}\;.

(3.14)

Indeed, using the consequences

\delta_{\alpha}^{\penalty\ \penalty\ \beta}=v_{\alpha}^{+}v^{\beta-}-v_{\alpha}^{-}v^{\beta+}\penalty\ ,\qquad\delta_{\dot{\alpha}}^{\penalty\ \penalty\ \dot{\beta}}=\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{+}\bar{v}^{\dot{\beta}-}-\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{-}\bar{v}^{\dot{\beta}+}\penalty\ ,

(3.15)

of the constraints (
2.19
), we can express the derivatives of spinor frame variables i.e.
\text{d}v_{\alpha}^{(\beta)}
and
\text{d}\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{(\dot{\beta})}
in terms of Cartan forms by

\text{d}v_{\alpha}^{-}=-\penalty\ \omega^{(0)}v_{\alpha}^{-}+\Omega^{--}v_{\alpha}^{+}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\text{D}v_{\alpha}^{-}:=\text{d}v_{\alpha}^{-}+\omega^{(0)}v_{\alpha}^{-}=\Omega^{--}v_{\alpha}^{+}\;,

(3.16)

\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{d}v_{\alpha}^{+}=\omega^{(0)}v_{\alpha}^{+}-\Omega^{++}v_{\alpha}^{-}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\text{D}v_{\alpha}^{+}:=\text{d}v_{\alpha}^{+}-\omega^{(0)}v_{\alpha}^{+}=-\Omega^{++}v_{\alpha}^{-}\;,

(3.17)

and their c.c. relations.

The expression for admissible variations

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\delta v_{\alpha}^{-}=-\iota_{\delta}\omega^{(0)}v_{\alpha}^{-}+\iota_{\delta}\Omega^{--}v_{\alpha}^{+}&\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow&\iota_{\delta}\text{D}v_{\alpha}^{-}:=\delta v_{\alpha}^{-}+\iota_{\delta}\omega^{(0)}v_{\alpha}^{-}=\iota_{\delta}\Omega^{--}v_{\alpha}^{+}\;,\end{array}

(3.18)

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\delta v_{\alpha}^{+}=\iota_{\delta}\omega^{(0)}v_{\alpha}^{+}-\iota_{\delta}\Omega^{++}v_{\alpha}^{-}&\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow&\iota_{\delta}\text{D}v_{\alpha}^{+}:=\delta v_{\alpha}^{+}-\iota_{\delta}\omega^{(0)}v_{\alpha}^{+}=-\iota_{\delta}\Omega^{++}v_{\alpha}^{-}\;,\end{array}

(3.19)

can be obtained from (
3.16
) and (
3.17
) by formal contraction with variation symbol
\iota_{\delta}
as it is described in section
1.2
. Note that in the case of 1-forms this is essentially the substitution
\text{d}\mapsto\delta
.

In Eqs. (
3.16
) and (
3.17
) we also define covariant derivatives
D
in which the 1-form
\omega^{(0)}
and its c.c.
\bar{\omega}^{(0)}
play the role of a
\text{SO}(1,1)\otimes\text{U}(1)
connection. These gauge transformations can be parametrized by
\iota_{\delta}\omega^{(0)}
and its complex conjugate
\iota_{\delta}\bar{\omega}{}^{(0)}
, as they appear in the expressions for spinor frame variations (
3.18
) and (
3.19
). These symmetries correspond to the gauge invariance of the massless superparticle action (
3.1
) (or (
3.5
)) provided it is supplemented by the following
\text{SO}(1,1)
scaling of the Lagrange multiplier
\rho^{\#}
,

\delta\rho^{\#}=(\iota_{\delta}\omega^{(0)}+\iota_{\delta}\bar{\omega}{}^{(0)})\rho^{\#}\;.

(3.20)

The derivatives
D
in (
3.16
) and (
3.17
) are covariant derivatives with respect to this gauge symmetry. Moreover, one can observe that the action (
3.5
) (or (
3.1
)) involves only
u_{\mu}^{=}
(or
v_{{}_{\alpha}}^{-}
and
\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{-}
) variables. Noting that the parameters
\iota_{\delta}\Omega^{++}
and
\iota_{\delta}\bar{\Omega}{}^{++}
, present in (
3.19
), do not enter into the variation of
u_{\mu}^{=}
nor
v_{{}_{\alpha}}^{-}
, we conclude that there is an additional gauge symmetry of the system: the so-called
\mathbb{K}_{2}
-symmetry

\displaystyle{\mathbb{K}}_{2}\,:\qquad

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathbb{K}_{2}}v_{\alpha}^{-}=0\;,\qquad\delta_{\mathbb{K}_{2}}v_{\alpha}^{+}=-\iota_{\delta}\Omega^{++}v_{\alpha}^{-}\;,

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathbb{K}_{2}}\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{-}=0\;,\qquad\delta_{\mathbb{K}_{2}}\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{+}=-\iota_{\delta}\bar{\Omega}{}^{++}\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{-}\;.

Together the above listed gauge symmetries
[\text{SO}(1,1)\otimes\text{U}(1)]\subset\!\!\!\!\!\!\times{\mathbb{K}}_{2}
, can be used as identification relations on the set of constrained pair
\{v_{\alpha}^{-},v_{\alpha}^{+}\}
which thus can be considered as a kind of homogeneous coordinate for the coset of the
\text{Spin}(1,3)=\text{SL}(2,{\mathbb{C}})
group, which is isomorphic to the
{\mathbb{S}}^{2}
sphere
[
163
,
164
]

\{v_{\alpha}^{\mp}\}\;=\;\frac{\text{SL}(2,{\mathbb{C}})}{[\text{SO}(1,1)\otimes\text{U}(1)]\subset\!\!\!\!\!\!\times{\mathbb{K}}_{2}}={\mathbb{S}}^{2}\;.

(3.21)

This can be recognized as the celestial sphere of an observer in a 4-dimensional spacetime. Hence the introduction of the Lagrange multiplier
\rho^{\#}
, which has a Stückelberg-type transformation (
3.20
) under the
\text{SO}(1,1)
gauge symmetry (i.e. it can be gauged to unity), makes the constrained bosonic spinors a kind of homogeneous coordinates on the celestial 2-sphere.

3.1.2

Equations of motion and induced supergravity on the worldline

The equations of motion derived from the action (
3.1
) include (see Eqs. (
A.11
)-(
A.18
) in Appendix
A
for the complete list of equations)

\text{E}^{=}=0\penalty\ ,\qquad\text{E}^{+-}=\text{E}^{-+}=0\;,\qquad\text{E}^{-}=\bar{\text{E}}^{-}=0\;.\qquad

(3.22)

Thus, only the pull-backs of one bosonic and two fermionic projections of the target superspace supervielbein remain nonzero on the mass shell. These forms

\text{E}^{\#}=\Pi^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}v_{\alpha}^{+}\bar{v}{}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{+}=\text{d}\tau\text{E}_{\tau}^{\#}\;,\qquad\text{E}^{+}=\text{d}\theta^{\alpha}v_{\alpha}^{+}=\text{d}\tau\text{E}_{\tau}^{+}\;,\qquad\bar{\text{E}}^{+}=\text{d}\bar{\theta}{}^{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{v}{}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{+}=\text{d}\tau\bar{\text{E}}_{\tau}^{+}\;\;\qquad

(3.23)

describe 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
supergravity induced on the worldline by its embedding into
\text{D}=4

{\cal N}=1
target superspace. Indeed, under the local fermionic
\kappa
-symmetry (
3.11
) these 1-forms transform as

\delta_{\kappa}\text{E}^{\#}=-4i(\text{E}^{+}\bar{\kappa}{}^{+}-\kappa^{+}\bar{\text{E}}{}^{+})\;,\qquad\delta_{\kappa}\text{E}^{+}=\text{D}\kappa^{+}\;,\qquad\delta_{\kappa}\bar{\text{E}}{}^{+}=\text{D}\bar{\kappa}{}^{+}\,,\qquad

(3.24)

and its
\tau
-components by

\delta_{\kappa}\text{E}_{\tau}^{\#}=-4i(\text{E}^{+}\bar{\kappa}{}^{+}-\kappa^{+}\bar{\text{E}}{}^{+})\;,\qquad\delta_{\kappa}\text{E}_{\tau}^{+}=\text{D}_{\tau}\kappa^{+}\;,\qquad\delta_{\kappa}\bar{\text{E}}{}_{\tau}^{+}=\text{D}_{\tau}\bar{\kappa}{}^{+}\,,\qquad

(3.25)

which are precisely the transformation rules of a
\text{1d}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{N}=2
supergravity supermultiplet. We refer to this as supergravity induced by embedding, because these supergravity 1-forms are built from the embedding coordinates
x^{\mu}(\tau)
,
\theta^{\alpha}(\tau)
,
\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}(\tau)
and spinor moving frame variables.

This induced supergravity provides the natural tool to couple a 1-dimensional supersymmetric matter multiplets to massless superparticle while preserving its local fermionic
\kappa
-symmetry. In particular, the action for 4D nAmW system, the counterpart of 11D mM
0
action from
[
126
]
, is constructed by coupling of 3D
\mathcal{N}=2

\text{SU}(N)
SYM multiplet dimensionally reduced to
\text{d}=1
to this induced 1d supergravity. We shall present that action and exhibit its full local worldline supersymmetry in the next section.

3.2

D
=4
{\cal N}
=1 non-Abelian multiwave action and its worldline supersymmetry

The action of 4D non-Abelian multiwave (nAmW) system, a lower dimensional counterpart of the 11D mM
0
action, could in principle can be obtained from this latter by dimensional reduction. In practice, however, the complicated structure of the 11D moving frame variables makes more easy to construct it directly using symmetry principles (see
[
126
]
for
\text{D}=11
and
[
133
]
for
\text{D}=3
). On this way we arrive at

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}S^{4\text{D}}_{\rm nAmW}&=\int_{{\cal W}^{1}}\rho^{\#}\text{E}^{=}+{1\over\mu^{6}}\int_{{\cal W}^{1}}(\rho^{\#})^{3}\text{tr}\left(\bar{\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}}}{\rm D}{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}+{\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}}}{\rm D}\bar{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}-{i\over 8}{\rm D}{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}\,\bar{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}+{i\over 8}{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}{\rm D}\bar{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}\right)+\\
&\penalty\ \\
&+{1\over\mu^{6}}\int_{{\cal W}^{1}}(\rho^{\#})^{3}\left[\text{E}^{\#}\widetilde{{\cal H}}+i\text{E}^{+}\text{tr}(\bar{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}\bar{\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}}}+{\widetilde{\Psi}}[{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}},\bar{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}])+i\bar{\text{E}}^{+}\text{tr}({{\widetilde{\Psi}}}{\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}}}+\bar{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}[{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}},\bar{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}])\right],\end{split}\end{array}

(3.26)

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\cal H}}=\text{tr}\left({\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}}}\bar{\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}}}+[{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}},\bar{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}]^{2}-{i\over 2}{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}+{i\over 2}\bar{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}\bar{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}\bar{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}\right)\;.\qquad

(3.27)

The first term of (
3.26
) is exactly the
\text{D}=4
massless superparticle action (
3.5
), now interpreted as the center of energy motion of the interacting multiwave system. The remaining part of the nAmW action, proportional to the dimensional parameter
1/{\mu^{6}}

36
36

36

Notice that
\mu
has dimension of mass in our physical system of units with
c=\hbar=1
. Then
[\mu^{-6}]=M^{-6}=L^{6}
.
contains bosonic and fermionic matrix fields. These are traceless
N\times N
bosonic

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}=\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}_{0|\#}:=\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}_{0|+2}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\bar{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}=\bar{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}_{\#|0}:=\bar{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}_{+2|0}=({\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}})^{\dagger}\,,\qquad

(3.28)

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}}=\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}}_{+|\#+}:=\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}}_{+|+3}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\bar{\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}}}=\bar{\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}}}_{\#+|+}:=\bar{\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}}}_{+3|+}=(\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}})^{\dagger}\,,\qquad

(3.29)

and fermionic

\displaystyle{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}={\widetilde{\Psi}}_{\#|+}:={\widetilde{\Psi}}_{+2|+}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\bar{{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}}=\bar{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}_{+|\#}:=\bar{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}_{+|+2}=({\widetilde{\Psi}})^{\dagger}\,

(3.30)

matrix fields
37
37

37

The matrix field symbols in the nAmW model are covered with tildes here to distinguish them from the matrix fields of the 3D mD

\text{SO}(1,1)
but carry
\text{U}(1)

The reason is that such a redefinition would introduce derivatives of the Stückelberg field directly into the action, resulting in a formulation that we find less convenient for our purposes.
all transforming under the gauge group SU
(N)
. Here the sign subindices (equivalent to the opposite sign superindices, e.g.
{\widetilde{\Psi}}_{\#|+}={\widetilde{\Psi}}^{=|-}
) indicate the transformation properties of the matrix fields under the
\text{GL}(1,{\mathbb{C}})=\text{SO}(1,1)\otimes\text{U}(1)

v_{\alpha}^{\mp}=v_{\alpha}^{(\mp|0)}
and
\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\mp}=\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{(0|\mp)}
as described in sec.

above. These latter enter, besides the first term, also in the remaining part of the action (

) through the induced supergravity 1-forms: 1d supervielbein
\text{E}^{\#}

\text{E}^{+},\bar{\text{E}}^{+}
(

\omega^{(0)}

{\rm D}{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}={\rm d}{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}+2\bar{\omega}{}^{(0)}{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}+[\,{\mathbb{A}}\,,\,{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}\,]\;,\qquad{\rm D}\bar{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}={\rm d}\bar{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}+2{\omega}{}^{(0)}\bar{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}+[\,{\mathbb{A}}\,,\,\bar{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}\,]\;,

(3.31)

{\rm D}{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}={\rm d}{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}+(2\omega^{(0)}+\bar{\omega}{}^{(0)}){{\widetilde{\Psi}}}+[\,{\mathbb{A}}\,,\,{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}\,]\;,\qquad{\rm D}\bar{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}={\rm d}\bar{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}+(\omega^{(0)}+2\bar{\omega}{}^{(0)})\bar{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}+[\,{\mathbb{A}}\,,\,\bar{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}\,]

(3.32)

as connection for the
\text{GL}(1,{\mathbb{C}})=\text{SO}(1,1)\otimes\text{U}(1)
transformations. The
\text{SU}(N)
connection 1-form
{\mathbb{A}}={\rm d}\tau{\mathbb{A}}_{\tau}
, with the traceless antihermitian
N\times N
matrix field
{\mathbb{A}}_{\tau}
, enters into the action (
3.26
) inside the covariant derivatives (
3.31
) and (
3.32
).

The dimension of the matrix matter fields are

{}[{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}]=M=[\bar{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}]\;,\qquad{}[\bar{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}}]=M^{2}=[{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}}]\;,\qquad[{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}]=M^{3/2}=[\bar{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}]\;.

(3.33)

The choice of such non-canonical dimension allows us to reduce the dependence on the dimensional parameter
\mu
, with dimension of mass
[\mu]=M
, to an overall multiplier in front of the part of the action including the matrix fields.

The action (
3.26
) is invariant under the local worldline supersymmetry transformations which act on the center of energy fields as a bit deformed version of the
\kappa
-symmetry of massless superparticle

\begin{array}[]{l}\delta_{\epsilon}x^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=2i\delta_{\epsilon}\theta^{\alpha}\bar{\theta}{}^{\dot{\alpha}}-2i\theta^{\alpha}\delta_{\epsilon}\bar{\theta}{}^{\dot{\alpha}}+v^{\alpha+}\bar{v}{}^{\dot{\alpha}+}\iota_{\epsilon}\text{E}^{=}\;,\\
\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \delta_{\epsilon}\theta^{\alpha}=\epsilon^{+}v^{\alpha-}\;,\qquad\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \delta_{\epsilon}\bar{\theta}{}^{\dot{\alpha}}=\bar{\epsilon}^{+}\bar{v}{}^{\dot{\alpha}-}\;,\\
\penalty\ \penalty\ \delta_{\epsilon}v_{\alpha}^{\mp}=0\;,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \qquad\qquad\delta_{\epsilon}\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{\mp}=0\;,\qquad\delta_{\epsilon}\rho^{\#}=0\;,\end{array}

(3.34)

where

\iota_{\epsilon}\text{E}^{=}=\dfrac{3i}{2\mu^{6}}(\rho^{\#})^{2}\,{\rm tr}\left(\epsilon^{+}\bar{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}\bar{{\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}}}}+\bar{\epsilon}{}^{+}{\widetilde{\Psi}}{\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}}}-(\epsilon^{+}{\widetilde{\Psi}}+\bar{\epsilon}{}^{+}\bar{{\widetilde{\Psi}}})[{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}},\bar{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}]\right)\;.

(3.35)

The matrix fields themselves are transformed by

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\delta_{\epsilon}{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}=-i\epsilon^{+}\bar{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}\;,&&\penalty\ \penalty\ \delta_{\epsilon}{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}=-i\epsilon^{+}\bar{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}\;,\end{array}

(3.36)

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \delta_{\epsilon}{\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}}}=i\epsilon^{+}[{{\widetilde{\Psi}}},{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}]+i\bar{\epsilon}{}^{+}[\bar{{\widetilde{\Psi}}},{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}]\;,&&\penalty\ \penalty\ \delta_{\epsilon}\bar{\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}}}=-i\epsilon^{+}[{{\widetilde{\Psi}}},\bar{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}]-i\bar{\epsilon}{}^{+}[\bar{{\widetilde{\Psi}}},\bar{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}]\;,\end{array}

(3.37)

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\delta_{\epsilon}{\widetilde{\Psi}}=4\epsilon^{+}\bar{\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}}}+4\bar{\epsilon}{}^{+}[{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}},\bar{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}]\;,&&\delta_{\epsilon}\bar{{\widetilde{\Psi}}}=4\bar{\epsilon}{}^{+}{\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}}}+4{\epsilon}{}^{+}[{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}},\bar{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}]\;,\end{array}

(3.38)

\delta_{\epsilon}{\mathbb{A}}=i\text{E}^{\#}(\epsilon^{+}{\widetilde{\Psi}}-\bar{\epsilon}{}^{+}\bar{{\widetilde{\Psi}}})+8i\text{E}^{+}\epsilon^{+}\bar{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}-8i\bar{\text{E}}{}^{+}\bar{\epsilon}{}^{+}\;{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}\;.

(3.39)

In the next chapter, we perform the dimensional reduction of the 4D nAmW action to
\text{D}=3
. The resulting theory provides a candidate for the 3-dimensional counterpart of the 10D multiple D
0
-brane system; henceforth referred to as the 3D mD
0
-brane.

4
Nonlinear 3D mD0 action by dimensional reduction of the 4D nAmW system

Always something new, always something I didn’t expect,

and sometimes it isn’t horrible.

The Wheel of Time II: The Great Hunt

by Robert Jordan.

In this section, we perform the dimensional reduction of
\text{D}=4
nAmW action, which serves as a counterpart of the 11D mM
0
-brane system, down to
\text{D}=3
and obtain in this way an action with the properties expected for 3D counterpart of 10D mD
0
system. Furthermore, we will show that actually this procedure produces just one representative of a family of 3D actions possessing these properties. All members of the family contain in their actions some positive definite function
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H})
, whose meaning will be explained later. The dimensional reduction produces the action with particular
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H})
given in Eq. (
4.71
) below.

To proceed, it is convenient to first derive the action for a massive
\text{D}=3

\mathcal{N}=2
superparticle by performing the dimensional reduction of the
\text{D}=4
massless superparticle action (
3.5
).

4.1

Notation and conventions from 4D to 3D

As it was already stated,
\text{D}=4
Weyl spinor indices are denoted by dotted and undotted symbols taken from the beginning of the Greek alphabet (see section
1.2
). Symbols from the middle of the Greek alphabet are used for 4-vector indices when they are untilded, and for 3-vector indices when they carry a tilde.

Thus, the bosonic vector and fermionic spinor coordinates of
\text{D}=4

{\mathcal{N}}=1
superspace are denoted by
z^{M}
(see Eq. (
1.33
)),
while the coordinates of the
\text{D}=3

{\mathcal{N}}=2
superspace are

z^{\tilde{M}}=(x^{\tilde{\mu}},\theta^{\alpha},\bar{\theta}{}^{\alpha})\penalty\ ,\qquad\tilde{\mu}=0,1,2\penalty\ ,\qquad\alpha=1,2\penalty\ .

(4.1)

The Weyl spinor indices are raised and lowered using Levi-Civita symbols

\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}=i\sigma_{2}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&1\cr-1&0\end{matrix}\right)=-\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}=\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}{\dot{\beta}}}=-\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}{\dot{\beta}}}\penalty\ .

(4.2)

For instance,

\theta^{\alpha}=\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\theta_{\beta}\;,\qquad\theta_{\alpha}=\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\theta^{\beta}\;,\qquad

(4.3)

which similarly applies to
\text{D}=3
spinors, and

\bar{\theta}{}^{\dot{\alpha}}=\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\bar{\theta}{}_{\dot{\beta}}\;,\qquad\bar{\theta}{}_{\dot{\alpha}}=\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\bar{\theta}{}^{\dot{\beta}}\;.\qquad

(4.4)

We use the following representation for the relativistic Pauli matrices (rPMs)

\begin{array}[]{l}\sigma_{\mu\,\alpha\dot{\beta}}=\left\{{\mathbb{1}},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}\right\}=\left\{\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\cr 0&1\end{matrix}\right),\left(\begin{matrix}0&1\cr 1&0\end{matrix}\right),\left(\begin{matrix}0&-i\cr i&\;0\end{matrix}\right),\left(\begin{matrix}1&\;0\cr 0&-1\end{matrix}\right)\right\}=\\
\\
\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ =\tilde{\sigma}^{\mu\,\dot{\alpha}{\beta}}:=\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\epsilon^{{\beta}{\alpha}}{\sigma}_{\nu\,{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\eta^{\nu\mu}\penalty\ ,\end{array}

(4.5)

which obey Eqs. (
2.4
) and (
2.5
).
Among the
\text{D}=4
rPMs (
4.5
), only
\sigma_{2}
is complex and antisymmetric. This allows us to identify the
\text{D}=3
gamma matrices, which admit a real symmetric representation (after raising or lowering indices using the charge conjugation matrix) with the rPMs carrying indices
0
,
1
, and
3

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mu\,\alpha\dot{\beta}}=\left(\sigma_{\tilde{\mu}\,\alpha\dot{\beta}},\sigma_{2\,\alpha\dot{\beta}}\right)\;,\qquad\tilde{\sigma}_{\mu}^{\dot{\beta}\alpha}=\left(\tilde{\sigma}_{\tilde{\mu}}^{\dot{\beta}\alpha},\tilde{\sigma}_{2}^{\dot{\beta}\alpha}\right)\,,

(4.6)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\tilde{\mu}\,\alpha{\beta}}=\sigma_{\tilde{\mu}\,\alpha\dot{\beta}}=\left\{{\mathbb{1}},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{3}\right\}=\left\{\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\cr 0&1\end{matrix}\right),\left(\begin{matrix}0&1\cr 1&0\end{matrix}\right),\left(\begin{matrix}1&\;0\cr 0&-1\end{matrix}\right)\right\}\;,

(4.7)

\displaystyle\tilde{\gamma}_{\tilde{\mu}}^{{\beta}\alpha}=\tilde{\sigma}_{\tilde{\mu}}^{\dot{\beta}\alpha}=({\mathbb{1}},-\sigma_{1},-\sigma_{3})=\epsilon^{{\alpha}\gamma}\epsilon^{{\beta}\delta}\gamma_{\tilde{\mu}\,\gamma{\delta}}\;.\qquad

(4.8)

4.2

Massive 3D counterpart of D0-brane from massless 4D superparticle

As a warm-up exercise, let us discuss the dimensional reduction of the
\text{D}=4

\mathcal{N}=1
massless superparticle and obtain in this manner the action for a massive
\text{D}=3

\mathcal{N}=2
superparticle, the 3D counterpart of the 10D action for a single D
0
-brane. This action will later serve as the center of mass sector of the system of multiple D
0
-branes.

4.2.1

Reduction of Brink-Schwarz action

Let us begin with the previously presented
\text{D}=4
Brink-Schwarz superparticle action (
2.1
),

S_{\rm BS}^{4\text{D}}=\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}p_{\mu}\Pi^{\mu}+\dfrac{e}{2}p_{\mu}p^{\mu}\;.

(4.9)

Let us choose
x^{2}
as the direction of reduction,

x^{\mu}=(x^{\tilde{\mu}},x^{2})\;,\qquad p_{\mu}=(p_{\tilde{\mu}},p_{2})\;,\qquad{\tilde{\mu}}=0,1,3\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\mu=0,1,2,3\;,

(4.10)

which is convenient because it aligns with the representation of the
\text{D}=4
rPMs (
4.5
) and their relation with
\text{D}=3
gamma matrices (
4.7
). This corresponds to the split of the 4D VA 1-form into

\Pi^{2}={\rm d}x^{2}-i{\rm d}\theta\sigma^{2}\bar{\theta}+i\theta\sigma^{2}{\rm d}\bar{\theta}={\rm d}x^{2}-\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}({\rm d}\theta^{\alpha}\bar{\theta}^{\beta}-\theta^{\alpha}{\rm d}\bar{\theta}^{\beta})\;

(4.11)

and the 3D VA 1-form

\Pi^{\tilde{\mu}}={\rm d}x^{\tilde{\mu}}-i{\rm d}\theta{\gamma}^{\tilde{\mu}}\bar{\theta}+i\theta{\gamma}^{\tilde{\mu}}{\rm d}\bar{\theta}=\frac{1}{2}\Pi^{\alpha\beta}{\gamma}^{\tilde{\mu}}_{\alpha\beta}\;.

(4.12)

Here

\text{d}\theta\gamma^{\tilde{\mu}}\bar{\theta}=\text{d}\theta{}^{\alpha}\gamma^{\tilde{\mu}}_{\alpha\beta}\bar{\theta}{}^{\beta},

(4.13)

with symmetric 2
\times
2 matrices

\gamma^{\tilde{\mu}}_{\alpha\beta}=-i\gamma^{\tilde{\mu}}_{\alpha}{}^{\sigma}\epsilon_{\sigma\beta}\;,\qquad\tilde{\gamma}{}^{\tilde{\mu}\alpha\beta}=i\epsilon^{\alpha\sigma}\gamma^{\tilde{\mu}}{}_{\sigma}{}^{\beta}\,\qquad

(4.14)

constructed from the 3D Dirac
\gamma^{\tilde{\mu}\beta}_{\alpha}
and
\epsilon
-symbol matrices. These are imaginary in our mostly-minus metric convention and satisfy

\gamma^{\tilde{\mu}}\gamma^{\tilde{\nu}}=\eta^{\tilde{\mu}\tilde{\nu}}+i\epsilon^{\tilde{\mu}\tilde{\nu}\tilde{\sigma}}\gamma_{\tilde{\sigma}}\;,\qquad\eta^{\tilde{\mu}\tilde{\nu}}=\text{diag}(+1,-1,-1)\;.

(4.15)

Eq. (
4.12
) can be represented as a symmetric spin-tensor 1-form

\Pi^{\alpha\beta}=\Pi^{\tilde{\mu}}\tilde{\gamma}_{\tilde{\mu}}^{\alpha\beta}={\rm d}x^{\alpha\beta}-2i{\rm d}\theta^{(\alpha}\bar{\theta}{}^{\beta)}+2i\theta^{(\alpha}{\rm d}\bar{\theta}{}^{\beta)}\;.\qquad

(4.16)

The dimensional reduction can be performed by using equations of motion for the
x^{2}
coordinate which reads
\text{d}p_{2}=0
implying that
p_{2}
is a constant,

{\rm d}p_{2}=0\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad p_{2}=m={\rm const}\;.

(4.17)

Substituting the solution (
4.17
) back into the action (
4.9
), using (
4.11
) and omitting the total derivative term, we find

S_{\rm dAL}^{3\text{D}}=\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\left(p_{\tilde{\mu}}\Pi^{\tilde{\mu}}+\dfrac{e}{2}(p_{\tilde{\mu}}p^{\tilde{\mu}}-m^{2})\right)+m\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}({\rm d}\theta^{\alpha}\bar{\theta}_{\alpha}-\theta^{\alpha}{\rm d}\bar{\theta}_{\alpha})\;.

(4.18)

This is the
\text{D}=3
counterpart of the De Azcárraga-Lukierski
{\mathcal{N}}=2
massive superparticle action
[
149
,
165
]
. This action possesses
\kappa
-symmetry, and let us note that
\kappa
-symmetry was first discovered in this massive example
[
149
]
, slightly earlier than in the case of the massless superparticle
[
135
]
.

From a modern perspective, (
4.18
) is the
\text{D}=3
counterpart of super-D
0
-brane action
[
94
]
or, simplifying terminology, the 3D D
0
-brane action. The second term in (
4.18
) is the Wess-Zumino term of the D
0
-brane, which serves as the prototype for the Wess-Zumino term of the superstring
[
87
]
and of higher super-
p
-branes (see
[
99
,
100
,
92
,
93
,
94
,
104
]
,
[
88
,
166
,
167
]
and refs. therein).

4.2.2

A suggestive ansatz for dimensional reduction of (spinor) moving frame formalism

Our task now is to perform dimensional reduction of the spinor moving frame action for
\text{D}=4
massless superparticle in a way that reproduces the 3D D
0
-brane action. (For a warm-up see Appendix
B
, where we dimensionally reduce to the 3D massless superparticle.). To this end it is sufficient to employ the following ansatz (
cf.
(
B.4
))

\begin{cases}v_{\alpha}^{-}={\rm v}_{\alpha}^{-}-i{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{+}\mu^{=}\;,\qquad\cr v_{\alpha}^{+}={\rm v}_{\alpha}^{+}\;,\end{cases}\qquad\begin{cases}\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{-}={\rm v}_{\alpha}^{-}+i{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{+}\mu^{=}\;,\qquad\cr\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{+}={\rm v}_{\alpha}^{+}\;,\end{cases}

(4.19)

where
{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{\pm}
are real spinors satisfying

{\rm v}^{-\alpha}{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{+}=1\;,\qquad({\rm v}_{\alpha}^{\pm})^{*}={\rm v}_{\alpha}^{\pm}\;,

(4.20)

and
\mu^{=}=\mu^{=}(\tau)
is a real 1d scalar field.

This ansatz, although useful for the present illustrative purpose, will not be appropriate for the dimensional reduction of the nAmW system, for reasons to be explained later. Nevertheless, it provides valuable intuition regarding dimensional reduction in the spinor moving frame formalism.

Using (
4.19
), we can easily write that

u_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}^{=}=2{\rm v}^{-}_{\alpha}{\rm v}^{-}_{\beta}+2{\rm v}^{+}_{\alpha}{\rm v}^{+}_{\beta}(\mu^{=})^{2}+2i\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\mu^{=}\quad(\;=(u_{\beta\dot{\alpha}}^{=})^{*}\;)\;.\qquad

(4.21)

Substituting into the original action (
3.5
) splits it into parts depending only on the 3D VA 1-forms (
4.12
) and a separate element involving
\Pi^{2}
(
4.11
)

S^{4\text{D}}_{0}|_{\eqref{v-=v-+}}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\rho^{\#}\left[{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{-}{\rm v}_{\beta}^{-}+(\mu^{=})^{2}{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{+}{\rm v}_{\beta}^{+}\right]\Pi^{\alpha\beta}-\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\rho^{\#}\mu^{=}\left[{\rm d}x^{2}-\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}({\rm d}\theta^{\alpha}\bar{\theta}^{\beta}-\theta^{\alpha}{\rm d}\bar{\theta}^{\beta})\right]\;.

(4.22)

Now let us use the equation of motion for
x^{2}
coordinate,

{\rm d}(\rho^{\#}\mu^{=})=0\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\rho^{\#}\mu^{=}=m={\rm const}\;.

(4.23)

Substituting its solution into (
4.22
) we find

\displaystyle S^{4\text{D}}_{0}|_{\eqref{v-=v-+},\penalty\ \eqref{rmu=m}}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\rho^{\#}\left({\rm v}_{\alpha}^{-}{\rm v}_{\beta}^{-}+\frac{m^{2}}{(\rho^{\#})^{2}}{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{+}{\rm v}_{\beta}^{+}\right)\Pi^{\alpha\beta}+m\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}({\rm d}\theta^{\alpha}\bar{\theta}^{\beta}-\theta^{\alpha}{\rm d}\bar{\theta}^{\beta})\;.\qquad

(4.24)

The term with
x^{2}
has disappeared as a total derivative. We have thus obtained a 3D superparticle action, where the second term in (
4.24
) is the Wess-Zumino term of the D
0
-brane, the same as in (
4.18
).

Now let us redefine the spinor frame variables as

{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{2}=\sqrt{\frac{\rho^{\#}}{m}}{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{-}\;,\qquad{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{1}=\sqrt{\frac{m}{\rho^{\#}}}{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{+}\penalty\ .

(4.25)

Then
\rho^{\#}
is just removed from the first kinetic term.

The new spinors satisfy
38
38

38

This constraint implies that
2\times 2
matrix
(v_{\alpha}^{1},v_{\alpha}^{2})
belongs to double covering SL
(2,{\mathbb{R}})
of the 3D Lorentz group SO
(1,2)
. Hence the second name of Lorentz harmonics; see below, in particular footnote
53
, for more details and references.

{\rm v}^{\alpha 2}{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{1}=1\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad{\rm v}^{\alpha p}{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{q}=-\epsilon^{pq}\;,\qquad q=1,2\;

(4.26)

and hence form a
\text{SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
valued matrix

{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{q}=({\rm v}_{\alpha}^{1},{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{2})\;\in\;\text{SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})\;.

(4.27)

Substituting the solutions of (
4.25
) for
v_{\alpha}^{\pm}
in the action (
4.24
) obtained by dimensional reduction of the 4D massless superparticle action (
3.5
), we arrive at

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}S_{\text{D}0}^{3\text{D}}&=\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{D}0}^{3\text{D}}=\frac{1}{2}m\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}{\rm v}^{q}_{\alpha}{\rm v}^{q}_{\beta}\Pi^{\alpha\beta}+m\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}({\rm d}\theta^{\alpha}\bar{\theta}^{\beta}-\theta^{\alpha}{\rm d}\bar{\theta}^{\beta})=\\
&\penalty\ \\
&=\frac{1}{2}m\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}{\rm u}^{0}_{\alpha\beta}\Pi^{\alpha\beta}+m\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}({\rm d}\theta^{\alpha}\bar{\theta}^{\beta}-\theta^{\alpha}{\rm d}\bar{\theta}^{\beta})\penalty\ ,\end{split}\end{array}

(4.28)

which is the 3D D
0
-brane action in spinor moving frame formulation
[
130
,
168
]
.

In the second line of (
4.28
) we have introduced the matrix
{\rm u}^{0}_{\alpha\beta}={\rm v}^{q}_{\alpha}{\rm v}^{q}_{\beta}
representing timelike normalized vector from the 3D moving frame attached to the worldline,

\displaystyle{\rm u}^{0}_{\alpha\beta}={\rm u}^{0\tilde{\mu}}{\gamma}_{\tilde{\mu}\alpha\beta}={\rm v}_{\alpha}^{1}{\rm v}_{\beta}^{1}+{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{2}{\rm v}_{\beta}^{2}\;,\qquad

(4.29)

\displaystyle{\rm u}^{1}_{\alpha\beta}={\rm u}^{1\tilde{\mu}}{\gamma}_{\tilde{\mu}\alpha\beta}=2{\rm v}_{(\alpha}^{1}{\rm v}_{\beta)}^{2}\;,\qquad{\rm u}^{2}_{\alpha\beta}={\rm u}^{2\tilde{\mu}}{\gamma}_{\tilde{\mu}\alpha\beta}={\rm v}_{\alpha}^{1}{\rm v}_{\beta}^{1}-{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{2}{\rm v}_{\beta}^{2}\penalty\ \;.\qquad

(4.30)

These vectors obey the orthogonality and normalization conditions

{\rm u}^{0}_{\tilde{\mu}}{\rm u}^{0\tilde{\mu}}=1\;,\qquad{\rm u}^{0}_{\tilde{\mu}}{\rm u}^{I\tilde{\mu}}=0\;,\qquad{\rm u}^{I}_{\tilde{\mu}}{\rm u}^{J\tilde{\mu}}=-\delta^{IJ}\;,\qquad I,J=1,2\;

(4.31)

which imply that they form
\text{SO}(1,2)
valued 3D moving frame matrix

{\rm u}_{\tilde{\mu}}{}^{\tilde{a}}=\left({\rm u}_{\tilde{\mu}}{}^{0},{\rm u}_{\tilde{\mu}}{}^{I}\right)\;\in\;\text{SO}(1,2)\qquad\text{with}\qquad\tilde{a}=0,1,2\penalty\ .

(4.32)

4.2.3

Irreducible
\kappa
-symmetry of the 3D super-D0-brane

To analyse the local symmetries of the 3D D
0
-brane action (
4.28
), we compute the exterior derivative of the Lagrangian form of the action (
4.28
). This reads

\displaystyle{\rm d}{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{D}0}^{3\text{D}}=-2im({\cal E}^{1}+i{\cal E}^{2})\wedge(\bar{{\cal E}}^{1}-i\bar{{\cal E}}^{2})+\frac{1}{2}m\Pi^{\alpha\beta}\wedge{\rm d}{\rm u}^{0}_{\alpha\beta}\;,\qquad

(4.33)

where
{\cal E}^{q}
and
\bar{{\cal E}}^{q}
are fermionic 1-forms of the pull-back of 3D supervielbein adapted to the embedding

{\cal E}^{q}=({\cal E}^{1},{\cal E}^{2})={\rm d}\theta^{\alpha}{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{q}\;,\qquad\bar{{\cal E}}{}^{q}=(\bar{{\cal E}}{}^{1},\bar{{\cal E}}{}^{2})={\rm d}\bar{\theta}^{\alpha}{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{q}\;.\qquad

(4.34)

We do not focus now on the derivatives of the spinor moving frame variables which will be discussed later.

Below we will also need one of three bosonic 1-forms of this supervielbein

\displaystyle{\rm E}^{0}=\Pi^{\tilde{\mu}}{\rm u}_{\tilde{\mu}}^{0}=\frac{1}{2}\Pi^{\alpha\beta}{\rm u}_{\alpha\beta}^{0}=\frac{1}{2}\Pi^{\alpha\beta}({\rm v}_{\alpha}^{1}{\rm v}_{\beta}^{1}+{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{2}{\rm v}_{\beta}^{2})\;,\qquad{\rm E}^{I}=\Pi^{\tilde{\mu}}{\rm u}_{\tilde{\mu}}^{I}=({\rm E}^{1},{\rm E}^{2})\;.\qquad

(4.35)

From (
4.33
) (and using the formalism presented in section
1.2
), it follows that the action is invariant under the following irreducible
\kappa
-symmetry transformations

\begin{array}[]{l}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \iota_{\kappa}{\cal E}^{1}=\kappa\;,\qquad\iota_{\kappa}{\cal E}^{2}=i\kappa\;,\qquad\iota_{\kappa}\bar{{\cal E}}^{1}=\bar{\kappa}\;,\qquad\iota_{\kappa}\bar{{\cal E}}^{2}=-i\bar{\kappa}\;,\\
\\
\iota_{\kappa}\Pi^{\alpha\beta}=0\;,\qquad\delta_{\kappa}{\rm v}^{q}_{\alpha}=0\qquad(\Longrightarrow\qquad\delta_{\kappa}{\rm u}^{0}_{\alpha\beta}=0)\;,\end{array}

(4.36)

or, equivalently,

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\delta_{\kappa}\theta^{\alpha}=\kappa({\rm v}^{\alpha 2}-i{\rm v}^{\alpha 1})\;,&&\delta_{\kappa}\bar{\theta}{}^{\alpha}=\bar{\kappa}({\rm v}^{\alpha 2}+i{\rm v}^{\alpha 1})\;,\\
\\
\delta_{\kappa}x^{\alpha\beta}=2i(\delta_{\kappa}\theta^{(\alpha}\,\bar{\theta}{}{}^{\beta)}-\theta^{(\alpha}\delta_{\kappa}\bar{\theta}{}^{\beta)})\;,&&\delta_{\kappa}{\rm v}^{q}_{\alpha}=0\;.\end{array}

(4.37)

Let us also observe that the action (
4.28
) is invariant under the local
\text{SO}(2)
symmetry rotating the spinor frame components (
4.27
). This acts as a local
\text{SO}(2)
rotation of the spacelike moving frame vectors
{\rm u}_{\tilde{\mu}}^{I}
and identifies the set of spinor moving frame fields
{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{q}
as homogeneous coordinates of the coset

\frac{\text{SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})}{\text{SO}(2)}\simeq\frac{\text{SO}(1,2)}{\text{SO}(2)}\;,

(4.38)

which is suitable for the description of the 3D massive particle since
\text{SO}(2)
is the small group of 3D timelike momentum.

Another observation is that this
\text{SO}(2)
symmetry is not manifest in the original ansatz (
4.19
) for 4D spinor frame variables. It appears in the final action as a kind of emergent symmetry. However, if we try to use this ansatz for dimensional reduction of a more complicated nAmW system, the symmetry does not emerge automatically. This would imply that the spinor frame variables in this 3D action parametrize the
\text{SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
group rather than coset (
4.38
) and hence carry one extra degree of freedom which actually is not wanted. Thus a refined dimensional reduction ansatz must be used.

4.3

Revising the 4D M0 reduction to 3D D0. Properties of 3D spinor frame

The dimensional reduction described above was based on the ansatz (
4.19
) for the spinor moving frame variables, which explicitly breaks the
\text{U}(1)
subgroup of the
\text{GL}(1,{\mathbb{C}})
gauge symmetry of the 4D spinor moving frame formalism. Moreover, this ansatz restores an important
\text{SO}(2)
gauge symmetry of the
\text{D}=3
Lorentz harmonic approach only at the final stage, as a symmetry of the single super-D
0
-brane action (
4.28
).

4.3.1

\text{SO}(2)
=
\text{U}(1)
invariant reduction of
D
spinor frame formalism

In this section, we describe a more complicated
\text{SO}(2)\simeq\text{U}(1)
invariant ansatz for the reduction of the
\text{D}=4
spinor moving frame formalism to
\text{D}=3
. This ansatz is characterized by the following expressions for the reduced lightlike vectors of the 4D moving frame

\displaystyle\rho^{\#}u_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}^{=}={\cal M}{\rm u}^{0}_{\alpha\beta}+i{\cal M}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\;,\qquad

(4.39)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho^{\#}}u_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}^{\#}=\dfrac{1}{\cal M}{\rm u}^{0}_{\alpha\beta}-\dfrac{i}{\cal M}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\;,\penalty\ \qquad

(4.40)

where
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{M}}(\tau)
is a new real field on the worldline. Since only these moving frame vectors appear explicitly in the nAmW action, and their reduction does not involve the vectors
{\rm u}_{\alpha\beta}^{I}=({\rm u}_{\alpha\beta}^{1},{\rm u}_{\alpha\beta}^{2})
, we can expect that the reduced action will be invariant under
\text{SO}(2)
rotation mixing these vectors.

The explicit form of the
\text{SO}(2)\simeq\text{U}(1)
invariant ansatz, expressing the
\text{D}=4
spinor moving frame variables (
2.20
) in terms of 3D Lorentz harmonics
{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{q}=({\rm v}_{\alpha}^{1},{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{2})
defined in (
4.27
), and which reproduces (
4.39
) and (
4.40
), is given by

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\sqrt{\rho^{\#}}v_{\alpha}^{-}=\dfrac{\sqrt{{\cal M}}}{\sqrt{2}}\,\left({\rm v}_{\alpha}^{2}-i{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{1}\right)\;,&&\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\rho^{\#}}}v_{\alpha}^{+}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\,\left({\rm v}_{\alpha}^{1}-i{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{2}\right)\;,\\
\sqrt{\rho^{\#}}\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{-}=\dfrac{\sqrt{{\cal M}}}{\sqrt{2}}\,\left({\rm v}_{\alpha}^{2}+i{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{1}\right)\;,&&\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\rho^{\#}}}\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{+}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\,\left({\rm v}_{\alpha}^{1}+i{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{2}\right)\;.\end{array}

(4.41)

Notice that this implies

\sqrt{\rho^{\#}}\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{-}=i\dfrac{{\cal M}}{\sqrt{\rho^{\#}}}v_{\alpha}^{+}\;,\qquad\frac{1}{\sqrt{\rho^{\#}}}\bar{v}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{+}=i\dfrac{\sqrt{\rho^{\#}}}{{\cal M}}v_{\alpha}^{-}\;\qquad

(4.42)

which breaks SL
(2,\mathbb{C})
invariance of 4D action down to SL
(2,\mathbb{R})
but preserves the complete
\text{GL}(2,\mathbb{C})
gauge symmetry of the 4D Lorentz harmonic formalism. The
\text{SO}(1,1)
subgroup leaves both sides of (
4.42
), while
\text{U}(1)\subset\text{GL}(2,\mathbb{C})
transformations of both sides are nontrivial and coincident.

Using (
4.41
), the (pull-backs of) relevant 4D supervielbein forms (
3.6
)-(
3.9
) can be expressed in terms of (pull-backs of)
3D supervielbein forms (
4.34
), (
4.35
):

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}\rho^{\#}\text{E}^{=}={\cal M}\text{E}^{0}+\frac{i}{2}{\cal M}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\Pi^{\alpha\dot{\beta}}&={\cal M}{\rm E}^{0}-{\cal M}\Pi^{2}=\\
&={\cal M}{\rm E}^{0}-{\cal M}({\rm d}x^{2}-{\rm d}\theta^{\alpha}\bar{\theta}_{\alpha}+\theta^{\alpha}{\rm d}\bar{\theta}_{\alpha})\penalty\ ,\end{split}\end{array}

(4.43)

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}\qquad\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \frac{1}{\rho^{\#}}\text{E}^{\#}=\frac{1}{{\cal M}}{\rm E}^{0}-\frac{i}{2}\frac{1}{{\cal M}}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\Pi^{\alpha\dot{\beta}}&=\frac{1}{{\cal M}}{\rm E}^{0}+\frac{1}{{\cal M}}\Pi^{2}=\\
&=\frac{1}{{\cal M}}{\rm E}^{0}+\frac{1}{{\cal M}}({\rm d}x^{2}-{\rm d}\theta^{\alpha}\bar{\theta}_{\alpha}+\theta^{\alpha}{\rm d}\bar{\theta}_{\alpha})\;,\end{split}\end{array}

(4.44)

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\rho^{\#}}}\text{E}^{+}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\,\left({\cal E}^{1}-i{\cal E}^{2}\right)\penalty\ ,&&\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\rho^{\#}}}\bar{\text{E}}^{+}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\,\left(\bar{{\cal E}}^{1}+i\bar{{\cal E}}^{2}\right)\;.\end{array}

(4.45)

The dimensional reduction of the massless superparticle action requires to use only the first of these expressions, (
4.43
). Substituting it into the action and using the equations of motion for
x^{2}
,

{\rm d}{\cal M}=0\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad{\cal M}=m={\rm const}\;

(4.46)

we arrive at the 3D super-D
0
-brane action (
4.28
).

4.3.2

Cartan forms and covariant derivatives in 3D

To proceed with the dimensional reduction of the nAmW action, we begin by introducing the Cartan forms of the coset
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})/\text{SO}(2)

f^{pq}:={\rm v}^{\alpha p}{\rm d}{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{q}=f^{qp}\;,\qquad{\rm v}^{\alpha p}=\epsilon^{pq}{\rm v}^{\alpha}_{q}=\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}{\rm v}_{\beta}^{p}\;.\qquad

(4.47)

Due to the constraint (
4.26
), this matrix 1-form is symmetric,
f^{pq}=f^{qp}
and the derivative of the 3D spinor frame variables are expressed in terms of these by

{\rm d}{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{q}={\rm v}_{\alpha p}f^{pq}\,,\qquad

(4.48)

where
{\rm v}_{\alpha p}=\epsilon_{pq}{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{q}
. Explicitly, this yields

{\rm d}{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{1}={\rm v}_{\alpha}^{1}f^{21}-{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{2}f^{11}\;,\qquad{\rm d}{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{2}={\rm v}_{\alpha}^{1}f^{22}-{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{2}f^{12}\;.\qquad

(4.49)

It is important to note that
f^{qq}
transforms as
\text{SO}(2)
connection, while
f^{12}=f^{21}
and
f^{11}-f^{22}
1-forms are covariant under
\text{SO}(2)
. This structure becomes transparent when using the 3D moving frame vectors defined in (
4.29
) and (
4.30
), which allows us to identify the
\text{SO}(2)
connection as

{\rm u}^{1}{\rm d}{\rm u}^{2}=-{\rm u}^{2}{\rm d}{\rm u}^{1}=\frac{1}{2}{\rm u}^{1\alpha\beta}{\rm d}{\rm u}^{2}_{\alpha\beta}=f^{qq}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad{\rm u}^{I}{\rm d}{\rm u}^{J}=\epsilon^{IJ}f^{qq}\;.\qquad

(4.50)

Similarly, we define the
\text{SO}(2)
covariant forms
f^{I}=(f^{1},f^{2})
, which serve as the vielbein for the coset
\text{SO}(1,2)/\text{SO}(2)

f^{1}:={\rm u}^{0}{\rm d}{\rm u}^{1}=-f^{11}+f^{22}\;,\qquad f^{2}:={\rm u}^{0}{\rm d}{\rm u}^{2}=2f^{12}\;.\qquad

(4.51)

For our discussion in the next section, it is important to note that the ansatz (
4.41
), together with (
3.14
), leads to the following expressions for the 4D connections

\omega^{(0)}-\bar{\omega}^{(0)}=i{\rm v}^{\alpha q}{\rm d}{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{q}=:if^{qq}\;,\qquad\omega^{(0)}+\bar{\omega}^{(0)}=\frac{{\rm d}\rho^{\#}}{\rho^{\#}}\,-\frac{{\rm d}{\cal M}}{{\cal M}}\,.

(4.52)

The second equation implies that the covariant derivative of the Stükelberg field in the 4D nAmW system can be written in terms of the derivative of the field
{\cal M}
:

{\rm D}\rho^{\#}={\rm d}\rho^{\#}-(\omega^{(0)}+\bar{\omega}^{(0)})\rho^{\#}=\rho^{\#}\frac{{\rm d}{\cal M}}{{\cal M}}\,.\qquad

(4.53)

4.3.3

{\cal N}
=2 3D mD0-brane action from dimensional reduction of the 4D nAmW

Let us turn to the problem of the dimensional reduction of the
\text{D}=4
nAmW action (
3.26
). This procedure simplifies if, before substituting the above discussed ansatz (
4.41
) for the 4D spinor frame variables, we first redefine the matrix fields by making them
\text{SO}(1,1)
invariant. This is achieved by multiplying them by suitable powers of the
\text{D}=4
Stückelberg field
\rho^{\#}
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathbb{Z}}}=\dfrac{1}{\rho^{\#}}{\mathbb{Z}}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\bar{\widetilde{{\mathbb{Z}}}}=\frac{1}{\rho^{\#}}\bar{\mathbb{Z}}\,,\qquad

(4.54)

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}}=\dfrac{1}{(\rho^{\#})^{2}}{\mathbb{P}}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\bar{\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}}}=\frac{1}{(\rho^{\#})^{2}}\bar{\mathbb{P}}\,,\qquad

(4.55)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}=\dfrac{1}{(\rho^{\#})^{\frac{3}{2}}}{\Psi}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\bar{\widetilde{\Psi}}=\frac{1}{(\rho^{\#})^{\frac{3}{2}}}\bar{{\Psi}}\,,\qquad

(4.56)

Using (
4.54
)-(
4.56
) one can check that the new matrix fields have opposite left and right “charges”

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Z}}={\mathbb{Z}}_{-|+}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbb{Z}}=\bar{\mathbb{Z}}_{+|-}\,,\qquad

(4.57)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}={\mathbb{P}}_{-|+}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbb{P}}=\bar{\mathbb{P}}_{+|-}\,,\qquad

(4.58)

\displaystyle{\Psi}={\Psi}_{\frac{1}{2}|-\frac{1}{2}}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\bar{{\Psi}}=\bar{{\Psi}}_{-\frac{1}{2}|\frac{1}{2}}\,,\qquad

(4.59)

confirming their
\text{SO}(1,1)
invariance.

Such redefinition of the matrix fields clearly generates terms proportional to
{\rm d}\rho^{\#}
in the action. However, after reducing the spinor moving frame sector according to the ansatz (
4.41
), these derivatives are replaced by those of the new scalar field
{\cal M}
, which is inert under both
\text{SO}(1,1)
and
\text{SO}(2)
transformations.

Indeed, using (
4.53
), the covariant derivatives of the old and new matrix fields are related by

\displaystyle{\rm D}\widetilde{{\mathbb{Z}}}=\dfrac{1}{\rho^{\#}}\left({\rm D}{\mathbb{Z}}-\frac{{\rm d}{\cal M}}{{\cal M}}{\mathbb{Z}}\right)\;,\qquad

(4.60)

\displaystyle{\rm D}\bar{\widetilde{{\mathbb{Z}}}}=\dfrac{1}{\rho^{\#}}\left({\rm D}\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}-\frac{{\rm d}{\cal M}}{{\cal M}}\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}\right)\;,\qquad

(4.61)

\displaystyle{\rm D}\widetilde{\Psi}=\dfrac{1}{(\rho^{\#})^{\frac{3}{2}}}\left({\rm D}\Psi-\frac{3}{2}\frac{{\rm d}{\cal M}}{{\cal M}}\Psi\right)\;,\qquad

(4.62)

\displaystyle{\rm D}\bar{\widetilde{\Psi}}=\dfrac{1}{(\rho^{\#})^{\frac{3}{2}}}\left({\rm D}\bar{\Psi}-\frac{3}{2}\frac{{\rm d}{\cal M}}{{\cal M}}\bar{\Psi}\right)\;,\penalty\ \qquad

(4.63)

where (see (
4.52
))

\displaystyle{\rm D}{\mathbb{Z}}={\rm d}{\mathbb{Z}}+if^{qq}{\mathbb{Z}}+[{\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{Z}}]\;,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \qquad

(4.64)

\displaystyle{\rm D}\Psi={\rm d}\Psi-\frac{i}{2}f^{qq}\Psi+[{\mathbb{A}},\Psi]\;.\qquad

(4.65)

Now, writing the 4D nAmW action in terms of new matrix variables and reduced spinor frame variables (
4.41
), and using the new covariant derivatives (
4.64
), (
4.65
) and the supervielbein forms (
4.43
)-(
4.45
), we arrive at

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}S^{\text{4D}}_{\rm nAmW}|_{\eqref{rv-=v2-iv1}}&=\int_{{\cal W}^{1}}{\rm E}^{0}\left({\cal M}+\frac{1}{{\cal M}}\,\frac{{\cal H}}{\mu^{6}}\right)+\int\limits_{{\cal W}^{1}}\left(-{\cal M}+\frac{1}{{\cal M}}\,\frac{{\cal H}}{\mu^{6}}\right)\,({\rm d}x^{2}-{\rm d}\theta^{\alpha}\bar{\theta}_{\alpha}+\theta^{\alpha}{\rm d}\bar{\theta}_{\alpha})\penalty\ +\\
&+{1\over\mu^{6}}\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}{\rm tr}\left(\bar{\mathbb{P}}{\rm D}{\mathbb{Z}}+{\mathbb{P}}{\rm D}\bar{\mathbb{Z}}-{i\over 8}{\rm D}{\Psi}\,\bar{\Psi}+{i\over 8}{\Psi}{\rm D}\bar{\Psi}\right)-{1\over\mu^{6}}\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\dfrac{{\rm d}{\cal M}}{{\cal M}}{\rm tr}\left(\bar{\mathbb{P}}{\mathbb{Z}}+{\mathbb{P}}\bar{\mathbb{Z}}\right)+\\
&+{1\over\mu^{6}}\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\left({\cal E}^{1}-i{\cal E}^{2}\right){\rm tr}(\bar{\Psi}\bar{\mathbb{P}}+\Psi[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{\mathbb{Z}}])\penalty\ +\\
&+{1\over\mu^{6}}\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\left(\bar{{\cal E}}^{1}+i\bar{{\cal E}}^{2}\right){\rm tr}({\Psi}{\mathbb{P}}+\bar{\Psi}[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{\mathbb{Z}}])\penalty\ .\end{split}\end{array}

(4.66)

Here,
{\cal H}
has the same structure as
\widetilde{\cal H}
(
3.27
), but is expressed through the new
\text{SO}(1,1)
invariant matrix fields,

\displaystyle{\cal H}={\rm tr}\left({\mathbb{P}}\bar{\mathbb{P}}+[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{\mathbb{Z}}]^{2}-{i\over 2}{\mathbb{Z}}{\Psi}{\Psi}+{i\over 2}\bar{\mathbb{Z}}\bar{\Psi}\bar{\Psi}\right)\;.\qquad

(4.67)

The complex fermionic forms
{\cal E}^{q}=(\bar{\cal E}^{q})^{*}
