and real bosonic form
{\rm E}^{0}
are defined in (
4.34
) and (
4.35
), respectively.

As already seen in simpler examples, the dimensional reduction requires using the equations of motion for the extra bosonic coordinate, in this case
x^{2}(\tau)
. This coordinate appears only once, in the second term of (
4.66
), and its equation of motion

{\rm d}\left({\cal M}-\frac{1}{{\cal M}}\,\frac{{\cal H}}{\mu^{6}}\right)=0\;

(4.68)

implies

{\cal M}-\frac{1}{{\cal M}}\,\frac{{\cal H}}{\mu^{6}}=m=\rm const\;

(4.69)

where
m
is a constant of dimension of mass. Eq. (
4.69
) can be solved by

{\cal M}_{\pm}=\frac{m}{2}\pm\sqrt{\frac{m^{2}}{4}+\frac{{\cal H}}{\mu^{6}}}\;.

(4.70)

Only the solution with positive sign in front of the root is physical, since it approaches
m
when
{\cal H}\to 0
(in particular when all matrix fields vanish). Thus we will consider

{\cal M}={\cal M}({{\cal H}}/\mu^{6}):=\frac{m}{2}+\sqrt{\frac{m^{2}}{4}+\frac{{{\cal H}}}{\mu^{6}}}=m\left[1+\frac{{{\cal H}}}{m^{2}\mu^{6}}+{\cal O}\left(\left(\frac{{{\cal H}}}{m^{2}\mu^{6}}\right)^{2}\right)\right]\;.

(4.71)

The last expression can be viewed as a power series expansion in
1/\mu^{6}
, or equivalently, as a weak field expansion in the matrix fields. Below we will refer on it as on decomposition in coupling constant
1/{\mu^{6}}
just for convenience.

Substituting the solution (
4.71
) of the
x^{2}
equation of motion into the action (
4.66
) we find the following action for 3D counterpart of 10D mD
0
system:

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}S_{\rm mD0}^{\rm 3D}&=\int_{{\cal W}^{1}}\left[m{\rm E}^{0}+m({\rm d}\theta^{\alpha}\bar{\theta}_{\alpha}-\theta^{\alpha}{\rm d}\bar{\theta}_{\alpha})\right]+{1\over\mu^{6}}\int_{{\cal W}^{1}}{\rm E}^{0}\frac{2}{{\cal M}}\,{{\cal H}}\penalty\ +\\
&+{1\over\mu^{6}}\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}{\rm tr}\left(\bar{\mathbb{P}}{\rm D}{\mathbb{Z}}+{\mathbb{P}}{\rm D}\bar{\mathbb{Z}}-{i\over 8}{\rm D}{\Psi}\,\bar{\Psi}+{i\over 8}{\Psi}{\rm D}\bar{\Psi}\right)\penalty\ -\\
&-{1\over\mu^{6}}\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\frac{{\rm d}{\cal M}}{{\cal M}}{\rm tr}\left(\bar{\mathbb{P}}{\mathbb{Z}}+{\mathbb{P}}\bar{\mathbb{Z}}\right)+\\
&+{1\over\mu^{6}}\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\left({\cal E}^{1}-i{\cal E}^{2}\right){\rm tr}(\bar{\Psi}\bar{\mathbb{P}}+\Psi[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{\mathbb{Z}}])\penalty\ +\\
&+{1\over\mu^{6}}\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\left(\bar{{\cal E}}^{1}+i\bar{{\cal E}}^{2}\right){\rm tr}({\Psi}{\mathbb{P}}+\bar{\Psi}[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{\mathbb{Z}}])\penalty\ ,\end{split}\end{array}

(4.72)

with
{\cal H}
defined in (
4.67
), bosonic and fermionic 1-forms defined in
(
4.35
) and (
4.34
), covariant derivatives defined in (
4.64
), (
4.65
)
and
{\cal M}={\cal M}({\cal H}/\mu^{6})
given in (
4.71
). Actually, as we will discuss below, the action (
4.72
) with arbitrary (nonvanishing) function
{\cal M}({{\cal H}}/\mu^{6})
also makes sense.

Notice that the derivative of
{\cal M}={\cal M}({\cal H}/\mu^{6})
(independently of whether it is given by (
4.71
) or considered to be arbitrary) is proportional to
1/{\mu^{6}}
(actually to
1/m^{2}\mu^{6}
),

{\rm d}{\cal M}=\mathcal{M}^{\prime}({\cal H}/\mu^{6})\text{d}\mathcal{H}=\dfrac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\dfrac{{\rm d}{{\cal H}}}{2\sqrt{\dfrac{m^{2}}{4}+\dfrac{{{\cal H}}}{\mu^{6}}}}\;,\qquad\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(y)=\dfrac{\text{d}}{\text{d}y}\mathcal{M}(y)\penalty\ ,

(4.73)

so that at the first order in
1/{\mu^{6}}
the action does not contain d
{{\cal H}}
and reads

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}S_{\rm mD0}^{\rm 3D}|_{{\cal M}\mapsto m}&=\int_{{\cal W}^{1}}\left[m{\rm E}^{0}+m({\rm d}\theta^{\alpha}\bar{\theta}_{\alpha}-\theta^{\alpha}{\rm d}\bar{\theta}_{\alpha})\right]+{1\over\mu^{6}}\int_{{\cal W}^{1}}{\rm E}^{0}\frac{2}{{m}}\,{{\cal H}}\penalty\ +\\
&+{1\over\mu^{6}}\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}{\rm tr}\left(\bar{\mathbb{P}}{\rm D}{\mathbb{Z}}+{\mathbb{P}}{\rm D}\bar{\mathbb{Z}}-{i\over 8}{\rm D}{\Psi}\,\bar{\Psi}+{i\over 8}{\Psi}{\rm D}\bar{\Psi}\right)\penalty\ +\\
&+{1\over\mu^{6}}\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}\sqrt{{m}}}\left({\cal E}^{1}-i{\cal E}^{2}\right){\rm tr}(\bar{\Psi}\bar{\mathbb{P}}+\Psi[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{\mathbb{Z}}])\penalty\ +\\
&+{1\over\mu^{6}}\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}\sqrt{{m}}}\left(\bar{{\cal E}}^{1}+i\bar{{\cal E}}^{2}\right){\rm tr}({\Psi}{\mathbb{P}}+\bar{\Psi}[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{\mathbb{Z}}])\penalty\ .\end{split}\end{array}

(4.74)

4.4

Worldline supersymmetry of the
D
=3
{\cal N}
=2 mD0-brane action

In this section we show that the characteristic properties of the mD
0
system are also exhibited by a more general class of models, described by the functional (
4.72
) with an arbitrary positive definite function
{\cal M}={\cal M}({\cal H}/\mu^{6})
. Namely we will show that such action possesses, besides the manifest
\text{D}=3

{\cal N}=2
supersymmetry, also the worldline supersymmetry generalizing the
\kappa
-symmetry (
4.36
) of single D
0
-brane action (
4.28
).

4.4.1

Worldline supersymmetry transformations of the center of mass variables

Our previous experience with the 4D nAmW system and the 10D action studied in
[
130
]
suggested that, when investigating the worldline supersymmetry of the mD
0
-brane system, it is natural to assume that it acts on the center of mass variables of the mD
0
system, i.e. on the coordinate functions and spinor frame variables, in the same manner as the
\kappa
-symmetry of single D
0
brane,

\begin{array}[]{lr}\delta_{\epsilon}\theta^{\alpha}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}({\rm v}^{\alpha 2}-i{\rm v}^{\alpha 1})\epsilon\penalty\ ,&\delta_{\epsilon}\bar{\theta}{}^{\alpha}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}({\rm v}^{\alpha 2}+i{\rm v}^{\alpha 1})\,\bar{\epsilon}\;,\\
&\delta_{\epsilon}x^{\alpha\beta}=2i\delta_{\epsilon}\theta^{(\alpha}\bar{\theta}{}^{\beta)}-2i\theta^{(\alpha}\delta_{\epsilon}\bar{\theta}{}^{\beta)}\;,\\
&\delta_{\epsilon}{\rm v}^{q}_{\alpha}=0\;.\end{array}

(4.75)

In the formalism presented in section
1.2
this is tantamount to stating that

\begin{array}[]{r}\iota_{\epsilon}({\cal E}^{1}+i{\cal E}^{2})=0\;,\qquad\iota_{\epsilon}(\bar{{\cal E}}{}^{1}-i\bar{{\cal E}}{}^{2})=0\;,\qquad\iota_{\epsilon}{\rm E}^{0}=0\;,\qquad\iota_{\epsilon}{\rm E}^{I}=0\;,\\
\iota_{\epsilon}f^{I}=0\;,\qquad\iota_{\epsilon}f^{qq}=0\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\delta_{\epsilon}{\rm v}_{\alpha}^{\mp}=0\;,\end{array}

(4.76)

and, consequently,

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\iota_{\epsilon}{\cal E}^{1}=\epsilon/\sqrt{2}&\Longrightarrow&\qquad\penalty\ \penalty\ \iota_{\epsilon}{\cal E}^{2}=\;i\epsilon/\sqrt{2}\;,\;\qquad\penalty\ \iota_{\epsilon}({\cal E}^{1}-i{\cal E}^{2})=\sqrt{2}\epsilon\;,\\
\iota_{\epsilon}\bar{{\cal E}}{}^{1}=\bar{\epsilon}/\sqrt{2}&\Longrightarrow&\penalty\ \penalty\ \qquad\iota_{\epsilon}\bar{{\cal E}}{}^{2}=-i\bar{\epsilon}/\sqrt{2}\;,\qquad\iota_{\epsilon}(\bar{{\cal E}}{}^{1}+i\bar{{\cal E}}{}^{2})=\sqrt{2}\bar{\epsilon}\;.\end{array}

(4.77)

In this form it is easier to find the following transformation properties of the bosonic and fermionic 1-forms entering the part of the action containing the matrix fields

\displaystyle\delta_{\epsilon}{\rm E}^{0}=-2i\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}({\cal E}^{1}-i{\cal E}^{2})\bar{\epsilon}-2i\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\bar{\cal E}{}^{1}+i\bar{\cal E}{}^{2})\epsilon\;,\qquad

\displaystyle\delta_{\epsilon}({\cal E}^{1}-i{\cal E}^{2})=\sqrt{2}{\rm D}\epsilon\;,\qquad\delta_{\epsilon}(\bar{\cal E}{}^{1}+i\bar{\cal E}{}^{2})=\sqrt{2}{\rm D}\bar{\epsilon}\;,\qquad

(4.78)

which are the typical transformations of the
\text{d}=1

{\cal N}=2
supergravity multiplet. This supergravity is induced by embedding of the worldline into
\text{D}=3

{\cal N}=2
superspace (
cf.
(
3.24
) and discussion around it). Eqs. (
4.78
) are useful to search for the worldline supersymmetry invariance of the part of the action containing the matrix fields.

4.4.2

Worldline supersymmetry transformations of the matrix matter fields

Now, writing the variation of the action (
4.72
) with (
4.75
)-(
4.78
) and extracting from this variation the terms proportional to D
{\mathbb{P}}
, D
{\mathbb{Z}}
, D
{\Psi}
and their hermitian conjugates, we find the following equations for the basic
\kappa
-symmetry variations of matrix “matter” fields

\delta_{\epsilon}{\mathbb{Z}}=-\frac{i}{\sqrt{\cal M}}\epsilon\bar{\Psi}+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\frac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\;\left({\mathbb{Z}}\delta_{\epsilon}{\tilde{{\cal H}}}-{\mathbb{P}}\Delta_{\epsilon}{\cal K}\right)\;,

(4.79)

\delta_{\epsilon}\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}=-\frac{i}{\sqrt{\cal M}}\bar{\epsilon}\Psi+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\frac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\;\left(\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}\delta_{\epsilon}{\tilde{{\cal H}}}-\bar{{\mathbb{P}}}\Delta_{\epsilon}{\cal K}\right)\;,

(4.80)

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{split}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \delta_{\epsilon}{\mathbb{P}}&=\frac{i}{\sqrt{\cal M}}\left(\epsilon[\Psi,{{\mathbb{Z}}}]+\bar{\epsilon}[\bar{\Psi},{\mathbb{Z}}]\right)-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\frac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\;{\mathbb{P}}\delta_{\epsilon}{\tilde{{\cal H}}}\penalty\ +\\
&+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\frac{2{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\;\left([[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}],{\mathbb{Z}}]+\frac{i}{4}\bar{\Psi}\bar{\Psi}\right)\,\Delta_{\epsilon}{\cal K}\;,\end{split}\end{array}

(4.81)

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{split}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \delta_{\epsilon}\bar{{\mathbb{P}}}&=\frac{i}{\sqrt{\cal M}}\left(\epsilon[\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}},\Psi]+\bar{\epsilon}[\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}},\bar{\Psi}]\right)-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\frac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\;\bar{{\mathbb{P}}}\delta_{\epsilon}{\tilde{{\cal H}}}-\\
&-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\frac{2{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\;\left([[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}],\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}]+\frac{i}{4}\Psi{\Psi}\right)\,\Delta_{\epsilon}{\cal K}\;,\end{split}\end{array}

(4.82)

\qquad\delta_{\epsilon}{\Psi}=\frac{4}{\sqrt{\cal M}}\left(\epsilon\bar{{\mathbb{P}}}+\bar{\epsilon}[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}]]\right)-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\frac{2{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\;[\bar{\Psi},\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}]\Delta_{\epsilon}{\cal K}\;,\\

(4.83)

\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \delta_{\epsilon}\bar{{\Psi}}=\frac{4}{\sqrt{\cal M}}\left(\epsilon[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}]+\bar{\epsilon}{\mathbb{P}}]\right)+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\frac{2{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\;[\Psi,{\mathbb{Z}}]\Delta_{\epsilon}{\cal K}\;.

(4.84)

These expressions are treated as equations because their right-hand sides involve the variations

\delta_{\epsilon}{{{\cal H}}}
of
{{{\cal H}}}
from (
4.67
) and

\Delta_{\epsilon}{\cal K}=\delta_{\epsilon}{\cal K}-\frac{i}{2\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\,(\epsilon\,{\nu}+\bar{\epsilon}\bar{\nu})

(4.85)

where

\nu={\rm tr}\left(\bar{\Psi}\bar{{\mathbb{P}}}+\Psi[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}]\right)\;,\qquad\bar{\nu}={\rm tr}\left(\Psi{\mathbb{P}}+\bar{\Psi}[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}]\right)

(4.86)

and
\delta_{\epsilon}{\cal K}
is the variation
of

{\cal K}={\rm tr}(\bar{{\mathbb{P}}}{\mathbb{Z}}+{\mathbb{P}}\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}})\,.

(4.87)

As both of these composite variations enter with coefficients proportional to
1/\mu^{6}
, the equations certainly admit a solution (at least in the form of a power series in
1/\mu^{6}
). Moreover, there is a straightforward method for solving these equations: first, use them to derive closed algebraic expressions for
\delta_{\epsilon}{\cal H}
and
\delta_{\epsilon}{\cal K}
; second, solve these expressions; and third, substitute the resulting solutions into the final terms of equations (
4.79
)-(
4.83
).

Indeed, using (
4.79
)-(
4.83
) we find

\delta_{\epsilon}{{{\cal H}}}=\frac{i}{\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\,{\rm tr}\left[\left(\bar{\epsilon}\bar{\Psi}-\epsilon\Psi\right)\,\left([{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{{\mathbb{P}}}]+[\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}},{\mathbb{P}}]-\frac{i}{4}\{\Psi\,,\bar{\Psi}\}\right)\right]+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\frac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\,{\mathfrak{H}}\,\delta_{\epsilon}{{{\cal H}}}

(4.88)

and

\delta_{\epsilon}{\cal K}=-\frac{i}{\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\,\epsilon\,{\rm tr}\left(\bar{\Psi}\bar{{\mathbb{P}}}-2\Psi[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}]\right)\,-\frac{i}{\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\,\bar{\epsilon}\,{\rm tr}\left(\Psi{\mathbb{P}}-2\bar{\Psi}[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}]\right)\,+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\frac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\,{\mathfrak{H}}\,\Delta_{\epsilon}{\cal K}\;,

(4.89)

where

{\mathfrak{H}}={\rm tr}\left(-2{\mathbb{P}}\bar{\mathbb{P}}+4[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{\mathbb{Z}}]^{2}-{i\over 2}{\mathbb{Z}}{\Psi}{\Psi}+{i\over 2}\bar{\mathbb{Z}}\bar{\Psi}\bar{\Psi}\right)\;\qquad

(4.90)

(
cf.
(
4.67
)). Eqs. (
4.88
) and (
4.89
) are closed algebraic equations for
\delta_{\epsilon}{{{\cal H}}}
and
\delta_{\epsilon}{\cal K}
, respectively, which are solved by

\displaystyle\delta_{\epsilon}{{{\cal H}}}=\frac{i}{\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\,\frac{1}{\left(1-\dfrac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\dfrac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\,{\mathfrak{H}}\right)}\;{\rm tr}\left[\left(\bar{\epsilon}\bar{\Psi}-\epsilon\Psi\right)\,\left([{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{{\mathbb{P}}}]+[\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}},{\mathbb{P}}]-\frac{i}{4}\{\Psi\,,\bar{\Psi}\}\right)\right]

(4.91)

and

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \Delta_{\epsilon}{\cal K}=-\frac{3i}{\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\,\frac{1}{\left(1-\dfrac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\dfrac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\,{\mathfrak{H}}\right)}\;\left[\epsilon\,{\rm tr}\left(\bar{\Psi}\bar{{\mathbb{P}}}-\Psi[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}]\right)\,+\bar{\epsilon}\,{\rm tr}\left(\Psi{\mathbb{P}}-\bar{\Psi}[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}]\right)\right]\;.

(4.92)

Thus the worldline supersymmetry transformations of the matrix matter fields are given by (
4.79
)-(
4.83
) with (
4.91
) and (
4.92
).

4.4.3

Worldline supersymmetry transformations of the non-Abelian gauge field

Taking into account the above relations, we find that the remaining expression of the action variation contains the terms proportional to the pull-backs of bosonic and fermionic supervielbein forms to the worldline, namely to

{\rm E}^{0}\;,\qquad({\cal E}^{1}-i{\cal E}^{2})\;,\qquad(\bar{{\cal E}}^{1}+i\bar{{\cal E}}^{2})\;

(4.93)

and to the variation of SU
(N)
gauge field 1-form
\delta{\mathbb{A}}
. This implies that, if the action is invariant under worldline supersymmetry, then

\delta_{\epsilon}{\mathbb{A}}={\rm E}^{0}\delta_{\epsilon}{\mathbb{A}}_{0}+({\cal E}^{1}-i{\cal E}^{2})\delta_{\epsilon}{\mathbb{A}}_{\eta}+(\bar{{\cal E}}^{1}+i\bar{{\cal E}}^{2})\delta_{\epsilon}{\mathbb{A}}_{\bar{\eta}}\;.\qquad

(4.94)

It is important to notice that
\delta_{\epsilon}{\mathbb{A}}
enters the action variation in the trace of its product with

{\rm tr}\left[\delta_{\epsilon}{\mathbb{A}}\,\left([{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{{\mathbb{P}}}]+[\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}},{\mathbb{P}}]-\frac{i}{4}\{\Psi,\bar{\Psi}\}\right)\right]\;,

(4.95)

so that the possibility of compensating all remaining terms in the action variation by choosing appropriate
\delta_{\epsilon}{\mathbb{A}}_{0}
,
\delta_{\epsilon}{\mathbb{A}}_{\eta}
and
\delta_{\epsilon}{\mathbb{A}}_{\bar{\eta}}
is a nontrivial check of consistency of our calculations.

For instance, this implies that the condition of vanishing the contribution proportional to
{\rm E}^{0}
in the
\kappa
-symmetry variation of the action,

{\rm tr}\left[\delta_{\epsilon}{\mathbb{A}}_{0}\,\left([{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{{\mathbb{P}}}]+[\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}},{\mathbb{P}}]-\frac{i}{4}\{\Psi,\bar{\Psi}\}\right)\right]=-\frac{2}{{\cal M}}\,\left(1-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\frac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\right)\,\delta_{\epsilon}{{\cal H}}\;,

(4.96)

can be solved because
\delta_{\epsilon}{\cal H}
(
4.91
) is also given by the trace of certain expression with

\left([{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{{\mathbb{P}}}]+[\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}},{\mathbb{P}}]-\frac{i}{4}\{\Psi,\bar{\Psi}\}\right)\penalty\ .

This allows to obtain

\displaystyle\delta_{\epsilon}{\mathbb{A}}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\left(1-\dfrac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\dfrac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\,{\cal H}\right)}{\left(1-\dfrac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\dfrac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\,{\mathfrak{H}}\right)}\;\frac{2i}{{\cal M}\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\,\left(\bar{\epsilon}\bar{\Psi}-\epsilon\Psi\right)\;.\qquad

(4.97)

Similarly studying the terms proportional to
({\cal E}^{1}-i{\cal E}^{2})
and their c.c.-s, we obtain

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}\delta_{\epsilon}{\mathbb{A}}_{\eta}&=\frac{8i}{\sqrt{2}{\cal M}}\,\epsilon\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\,\frac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{2\sqrt{2}{\cal M}^{2}}\left[2i\Psi\sqrt{{\cal M}}\Delta_{\epsilon}{\cal K}-\frac{3\left(\epsilon\Psi-\bar{\epsilon}\bar{\Psi}\right){\rm tr}\left(\bar{{\mathbb{P}}}\bar{\Psi}-\Psi[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}]\right)}{\left(1-\dfrac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\dfrac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\,{\mathfrak{H}}\right)}\right]=\\
&=\frac{8i}{\sqrt{2}{\cal M}}\,\epsilon\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\,\frac{3{\cal M}^{\prime}}{2\sqrt{2}{\cal M}^{2}}\frac{1}{\left(1-\dfrac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\dfrac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\,{\mathfrak{H}}\right)}\times\\
&\times(\bar{\epsilon}\bar{\Psi}-2{\epsilon}{\Psi}){\rm tr}\left(\bar{{\mathbb{P}}}\bar{\Psi}-\Psi[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}]\right)+\bar{\epsilon}\Psi{\rm tr}\left[\left(\bar{{\mathbb{P}}}\bar{\Psi}-\,\Psi[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}]\right)\right]\;\end{split}\end{array}

(4.98)

and

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}\delta_{\epsilon}{\mathbb{A}}_{\bar{\eta}}&=-\frac{8i}{\sqrt{2}{\cal M}}\,\bar{\epsilon}{\mathbb{Z}}+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\,\frac{3{\cal M}^{\prime}}{2\sqrt{2}{\cal M}^{2}}\frac{\epsilon\Psi-\bar{\epsilon}\bar{\Psi}}{\left(1-\dfrac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\dfrac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\,{\mathfrak{H}}\right)}\;{\rm tr}\left({\mathbb{P}}\Psi-\bar{\Psi}[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}]\right)+\\
&+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\frac{i{\cal M}^{\prime}}{\sqrt{2}\sqrt{{\cal M}}{\cal M}}\;\bar{\Psi}\;\Delta_{\epsilon}{\cal K}\,.\end{split}\end{array}

(4.99)

The second of these equations can be further specified with the use of (
4.92
).

4.4.4

Resume on the candidate mD0 action(s)

Thus, we have shown that the action (
4.72
), with a positive definite function
{\cal M}({\cal H}/\mu^{6})
of the matrix field Hamiltonian (
4.67
), possesses not only the target superspace
\text{D}=3

{\cal N}=2
supersymmetry but also local worldline supersymmetry. This local symmetry generalizes the
\kappa
-symmetry of the single D
0
-brane action. Consequently, any of these actions can be considered as a candidate for the role of
\text{D}=3
counterpart of the multiple D
0
-brane action.

A special representative of this family is the action where
{\cal M}({\cal H}/\mu^{6})
is given by (
4.71
), as it arises from the dimensional reduction of the
\text{D}=4
counterpart of the mM
0
-brane system (4D nAmW action). In general, the actions (
4.72
) are essentially nonlinear but the simplest representation of the family with
{\cal M}({\cal H}/\mu^{6})=m=\text{const}
, which provides the counterpart to the 10D action considered as a candidate for the mD
0
action in
[
130
]
. Our study suggests that more generic, essentially nonlinear candidates for the role of the 10D mD
0
action should be explored, and this investigation is actually presented in the next section.

We will return to this
\text{D}=3
counterpart of the 10D mD
0
system in chapter
7
, where we will construct the Hamiltonian formulation and perform the covariant quantization of the simplest
39
39

39

As we will see in chapter
7
, “simplest” corresponds to the 3D mD
0
representative with
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H}/\mu^{6})=m
.
representative within this diverse zoo of 3D mD
0
models.

5
Complete nonlinear action for supersymmetric multiple D0-brane system

…tenía la rara virtud de no existir por completo

sino en el momento oportuno.

Cien años de soledad

by Gabriel García Márquez.

In this section we present a complete nonlinear action for the dynamical system of 10D multiple D
0
-branes. This action exhibits not only manifest spacetime (target superspace) supersymmetry but also worldline supersymmetry, the generalization of the local fermionic
\kappa
-symmetry characteristic of a single D
0
-brane. Moreover, as its lower dimensional 3D counterpart, this action includes an arbitrary positive definite function
{\cal M}({\cal H})
of the SYM Hamiltonian
{\cal H}
constructed from the matrix fields describing relative motion of mD
0
constituents.

The properties of this set of candidate actions will be analysed in this section, while the
11
-dimensional origin of a specific
\text{D}=10
mD
0
model, corresponding to a particular choice of
{\cal M}({\cal H})
, will be discussed in section
6
.

5.1

The 10D mD0 action and its symmetries

The nonlinear action that we have constructed is expressed in terms of center of mass variables of mD
0
system, which are identical to those used in the single D
0
-brane case, and matrix variables describing the relative motion of mD
0
constituents. The field content of such systems is motivated by their low energy (and gauge fixed) description which, according to
[
103
]
, is given by U(
N
) SYM theory. This generalizes the well-known fact that, after suitable gauge fixing, the low energy description of a single D
p
-brane is provided by an Abelian U
(1)
SYM theory. Consequently, the Lorentz-covariant formulation of the mD
p
-brane system is expected to include both the fields known from the single D
p
-brane description, which are essentially coordinate functions and Abelian gauge field, and the fields of the maximally supersymmetric SU(
N
) SYM multiplet in
\text{d}=p+1
dimensions
[
103
]
.

Thus, focusing on the mD
0
-brane case, the set of worldline fields includes coordinate functions

z^{M}(\tau)=(x^{\mu}(\tau),\theta^{1\alpha}(\tau),\theta^{2}_{\alpha}(\tau))\penalty\ ,\qquad\mu=0,\ldots,9\penalty\ ,\qquad\alpha=1,\ldots,16\penalty\ ,

(5.1)

which describe the embedding of the worldline
{\cal W}^{1}
into the target type IIA superspace
\Sigma^{(10|32)}
whose bosonic and fermionic coordinates are
z^{M}=(x^{\mu},\theta^{\alpha 1},\theta_{\alpha}^{2})
,

{\cal W}^{1}\;\subset\;\Sigma^{(10|32)}\;:\qquad z^{M}=z^{M}(\tau)\;\penalty\ .

(5.2)

In addition, according to
[
103
]
, the system includes matrix fields from the
\text{d}=1

{\cal N}=16
SU(
N
) SYM multiplet, and some auxiliary fields. The set of these latter includes the 10D spinor moving frame fields, discussed in detail below, and the matrix momentum field which allows the SYM sector to be written in a first order form.

The matrix field content of the mD
0
system includes
9+9
bosonic Hermitian traceless
N\times N
matrix fields

{\mathbb{X}}^{i}(\tau)\penalty\ ,\quad{\mathbb{P}}^{i}(\tau)

(5.3)

where
{\mathbb{P}}^{i}
serves as the conjugate momentum to the matrix field
{\mathbb{X}}^{i}
which is a physical bosonic field of the SU
(N)
SYM multiplet; both are labelled by the SO
(9)
vector index
i=1,\ldots,9
. The system also includes an anti-Hermitian bosonic traceless
N\times N
matrix valued 1-form

{\mathbb{A}}=\text{d}\tau{\mathbb{A}}_{\tau}\penalty\ ,

(5.4)

where
{\mathbb{A}}_{\tau}(\tau)
is a worldline gauge field valued in the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{su}(N)
. The fermionic part consists in 16 fermionic Hermitian traceless
N\times N
matrix fields

{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}(\tau)\penalty\ ,

(5.5)

which transform as a spinor under SO
(9)
(i.e. as Spin
(9)
fundamental representation) with indices
q=1,\ldots,16
. This completes the set of matrix fields which captures the dynamics of the relative motion sector of the mD
0
system.

5.1.1

10D Moving frame and spinor moving frame fields

Finally to write the candidate for mD
0
action we need to introduce the moving frame and spinor moving frame fields. These generalize to
\text{D}=10
the moving frame and spinor moving frame formalism described in chapter
2
.

The moving frame vectors are described by Lorentz group valued 10
\times
10 matrix

(u_{\mu}^{0},u_{\mu}^{i})\in\text{SO}(1,9)\penalty\ ,\qquad i=1,\ldots,9

(5.6)

which obey

u^{\mu 0}u_{\mu}^{0}=1\;,\qquad u^{\mu 0}u_{\mu}^{i}=0\;,\qquad u^{\mu i}u_{\mu}^{j}=-\delta^{ij}\;.

(5.7)

The spinor moving frame is described by Spin
(1,9)
valued matrix

\displaystyle v_{\alpha}{}^{q}\in\text{Spin}(1,9)\;.

(5.8)

This spinor frame is related to the above described moving frame by the conditions of the Lorentz invariance of 10D sigma-matrices

u^{(b)}_{\mu}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}=v_{\alpha}^{q}\sigma^{(b)}_{qp}v_{\beta}^{p}\;,\qquad u^{(b)}_{\mu}\tilde{\sigma}{}_{(b)}^{qp}=v_{\alpha}^{q}\tilde{\sigma}{}_{\mu}^{\alpha\beta}v_{\beta}^{p}\,.

(5.9)

providing a kind of square root of the moving frame vectors in the sense of Cartan-Penrose-like relations. The indices
q,p=1,\dots,16
correspond to SO
(9)
spinor indices, reflecting the local SO
(9)
gauge symmetry that preserves the splitting in (
5.6
),

u^{\mu 0}(\tau)\mapsto u^{\mu 0}(\tau)\;,\qquad u^{\mu i}(\tau)\mapsto u^{\mu j}(\tau){\cal O}^{ji}(\tau)\;,\qquad{\cal O}^{ji}{\cal O}^{jk}=\delta^{ik}\;.

(5.10)

Choosing an SO
(9)
invariant representation of sigma-matrices

\sigma^{(a)}_{qp}=(\delta_{qp},\gamma^{i}_{qp})=\tilde{\sigma}{}_{(a)}^{qp}\;,\qquad\gamma^{i}_{qp}=\gamma^{i}_{pq}\;,\qquad\gamma^{(i}\gamma^{j)}=\delta^{ij}

(5.11)

in which
\gamma^{i}_{qp}=\gamma^{i}_{pq}
are
\text{d}=9
gamma matrices, we find that (
5.9
) leads to

\displaystyle\sigma^{{\mathbf{0}}}_{\alpha\beta}:=u_{\mu}^{{0}}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}=v_{\alpha}{}^{q}v_{\beta}{}^{q}\;,\qquad\penalty\ \sigma^{{\mathbf{i}}}_{\alpha\beta}:=u_{\mu}^{{i}}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}=v_{\alpha}{}^{q}\gamma^{i}_{qp}v_{\beta}{}^{p}\;\qquad

(5.12)

\displaystyle{\rm and}\qquad v_{\alpha}^{q}\tilde{\sigma}{}_{\mu}^{\alpha\beta}v_{\beta}^{p}=u_{\mu}^{{0}}\delta_{qp}+u_{\mu}^{{i}}\gamma^{i}_{qp}\;.\qquad

Unlike their
\text{D}=4
counterparts (see
[
144
]
and section
2.1.4
), these relations (
5.12
) impose strong constraints on the spinor moving frame field
v_{\alpha}{}^{q}
reducing its initial
16\times 16=256
components to
45={\rm dim}(\text{SO}(1,9))
.
Additionally, we can define

\displaystyle\tilde{\sigma}^{{\mathbf{0}}\alpha\beta}:=u_{\mu}^{0}\tilde{\sigma}^{\mu\alpha\beta}=v_{q}{}^{\alpha}v_{q}{}^{\beta}\;,\qquad\tilde{\sigma}^{{\mathbf{i}}\alpha\beta}:=u_{\mu}^{i}\tilde{\sigma}^{\mu\alpha\beta}=-v_{q}{}^{\alpha}\gamma^{i}_{qp}v_{p}{}^{\beta}\;\qquad

(5.13)

where

\displaystyle v_{\alpha}^{q}\tilde{\sigma}^{{\mathbf{0}}\alpha\beta}=v_{q}{}^{\beta}\;,\qquad{\sigma}^{{\mathbf{0}}}_{\alpha\beta}v_{q}{}^{\beta}=v_{\alpha}{}^{q}\;.

(5.14)

This matrix
v_{q}{}^{\alpha}
satisfies the relations:

\displaystyle v_{q}{}^{\alpha}v_{\alpha}{}^{p}=\delta_{q}{}^{p}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad v_{\alpha}{}^{q}v_{q}{}^{\beta}=\delta_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}\;\qquad

(5.15)

and hence can be identified as inverse spinor frame matrix. Below we will also use the Cartan forms

\displaystyle\Omega^{i}=u_{\mu}^{0}\text{d}u^{\mu i},\qquad

(5.16)

\displaystyle\Omega^{ij}=u_{\mu}^{i}\text{d}u^{\mu j}\;\qquad

(5.17)

where
\Omega^{i}
transforms covariantly under SO
(9)
gauge group (
5.10
), and
\Omega^{ij}
serves as the SO
(9)
connection. Using (
5.7
) derivatives of the moving frame can be expressed in terms of Cartan forms:

\displaystyle\text{D}u_{\mu}^{0}:=\text{d}u_{\mu}^{0}=u_{\mu}^{i}\Omega^{i}\;,\qquad\text{D}u_{\mu}^{i}:=\text{d}u_{\mu}^{i}+u_{\mu}^{j}\Omega^{ji}=u_{\mu}^{0}\Omega^{i}\;.\qquad

(5.18)

Here we have also introduced the SO(9) covariant derivatives with the composite connection given by the Cartan form
\Omega^{ji}
(
5.17
).

The spinor moving frame derivatives are also expressed in terms of the same SO
(1,9)
Cartan forms by

\displaystyle\text{D}v_{\alpha}{}^{q}:=\text{d}v_{\alpha}{}^{q}+\frac{1}{4}\Omega^{ij}v_{\alpha}{}^{p}\gamma^{ij}_{pq}=\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{i}_{qp}v_{\alpha}{}^{p}\Omega^{i}\;\qquad

(5.19)

which implies

\displaystyle\text{D}v_{q}^{\alpha}:=\text{d}v_{q}^{\alpha}-\frac{1}{4}\Omega^{ij}\gamma^{ij}_{qp}v_{p}^{\alpha}=-\frac{1}{2}v_{p}^{\alpha}\gamma^{i}_{pq}\Omega^{i}\;.\qquad

(5.20)

The variation of the moving frame and spinor moving frame variables can be also expressed in terms of Cartan forms or, more precisely, in terms of contraction of these with the variational symbol:

\iota_{\delta}\Omega^{i}=u^{\mu 0}\delta u_{\mu}^{i}\;,\qquad\iota_{\delta}\Omega^{ij}=u^{\mu i}\delta u_{\mu}^{j}\;.\qquad

(5.21)

The latter parametrize the SO
(9)
transformations which will be the manifest gauge symmetry of our action. Thus, the essential variations of moving frame vectors and spinor moving frame matrices are given by

\displaystyle\delta u_{\mu}^{0}=u_{\mu}^{i}\iota_{\delta}\Omega^{i}\;,\qquad\delta u_{\mu}^{i}=u_{\mu}^{0}\iota_{\delta}\Omega^{i}\;,\qquad

(5.22)

\displaystyle\delta v_{\alpha}{}^{q}=\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{i}_{qp}v_{\alpha}{}^{p}\iota_{\delta}\Omega^{i}\;,\qquad\delta v_{q}^{\alpha}=-\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{i}_{qp}v_{p}^{\alpha}\iota_{\delta}\Omega^{i}\penalty\ .

(5.23)

The moving frame formalism allows to define the Lorentz invariant 1-forms on the worldline by contracting the pull-back of the VA 1-form of the type IIA superspace

\Pi^{\mu}=\text{d}x^{\mu}-i\text{d}\theta^{1}\sigma^{\mu}\theta^{1}-i\text{d}\theta^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{\mu}\theta^{2}\;

(5.24)

with the moving frame vectors:

\text{E}^{0}=\Pi^{\mu}u_{\mu}^{0}\;,\qquad\text{E}^{i}=\Pi^{\mu}u_{\mu}^{i}\;.\qquad

(5.25)

Similarly, spinor moving frame formalism allows to define on the worldline the following Lorentz invariant fermionic 1-forms:

\text{E}^{1q}=\text{d}\theta^{\alpha 1}v_{\alpha}^{\;q}\,,\qquad\text{E}^{2}_{q}=\text{d}\theta_{\alpha}^{2}v_{q}^{\alpha}\;.\qquad

(5.26)

5.1.2

Candidate mD0 action(s)

The action for
N
nearly coincident D
0
-brane system has the form

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}S_{\text{mD0}}&=m\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\text{E}^{0}-im\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}(\text{d}\theta^{1}\theta^{2}-\theta^{1}\text{d}\theta^{2})-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\frac{\text{d}{\cal M}}{{\cal M}}{\rm tr}({\mathbb{P}}^{i}{\mathbb{X}}^{i})\penalty\ +\\
&\penalty\ \\
&+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\left[\text{tr}\left({\mathbb{P}}^{i}\text{D}{\mathbb{X}}^{i}+4i{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}\text{D}{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}\right)+\frac{2}{\cal M}\text{E}^{0}\,{\cal H}\right]\penalty\ +\\
&\penalty\ \\
&+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2{\cal M}}}(\text{E}{}^{1q}-\text{E}{}^{2}_{q}){\rm tr}\left[-4i(\gamma^{i}{\bm{\Psi}})_{q}{\mathbb{P}}^{i}+{1\over 2}(\gamma^{ij}{\bm{\Psi}})_{q}[{\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}]\right]\penalty\ ,\end{split}\end{array}

(5.27)

where
m
and
\mu
are constants of dimension of mass. In the above equation,
\text{E}^{0}
is the projection (
5.25
) of the pull-back of VA 1-form (
5.24
) to the vector
u_{\mu}^{0}
of the moving frame (
5.6
),
\text{E}{}^{1q}
and
\text{E}{}^{2}_{q}
are the contractions (
5.26
) of the pull-backs of the fermionic forms. The combination of bosonic
{\mathbb{P}}^{i}
and
{\mathbb{X}}^{i}
and fermionic
{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}
matrix fields

\displaystyle{\cal H}={1\over 2}\text{tr}\left({\mathbb{P}}^{i}{\mathbb{P}}^{i}\right)-{1\over 64}\text{tr}\left[{\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}\right]^{2}-2\,\text{tr}\left({\mathbb{X}}^{i}\,{\bm{\Psi}}\gamma^{i}{\bm{\Psi}}\right)\penalty\ ,

(5.28)

which has the form of 1d
\mathcal{N}=16
SU
(N)
SYM Hamiltonian; abusing a bit the terminology, we will call it below the relative motion Hamiltonian.

Actually the two first terms in (
5.27
) formally coincide with the action of single D
0
-brane, i.e. massive
\text{D}=10
type IIA superparticle in its spinor moving frame formulation
[
130
,
168
]
(see Appendix
C
for more details). In this case
m
plays the role of the superparticle mass. In contrast, the constant
\mu
characterizes the interaction of the center of mass and relative motion sector as well as the self-interaction of this latter. Notice that to simplify and to make more transparent the dependence of the action on this parameter we have chosen non-canonical dimensions for the matrix matter fields (
5.3
) and (
5.5
).

In particular, with this choice of dimensions of matrix fields, the relative motion Hamiltonian
{\cal H}
(
5.28
) is
\mu
-independent. However its dimension becomes (mass
6
) so that
{\cal H}/\mu^{6}
is dimensionless.

The calligraphic
{\cal M}
in (
5.27
) is a
positive definite function
of this dimensionless combination of the relative motion Hamiltonian and coupling constant,

{\cal M}={\cal M}({\cal H}/\mu^{6})\;,

(5.29)

for which, the consistency requires the function
{\cal M}={\cal M}({\cal H}/\mu^{6})
to be positive and we will assume this below,

{\cal M}({\cal H}/\mu^{6})>0\;.

(5.30)

In section
6
, we will see how a particular case of the action (
5.27
) with

{\cal M}=\frac{m}{2}+\sqrt{\frac{m^{2}}{4}+\frac{\cal H}{\mu^{6}}}\;

(5.31)

can be obtained by dimensional reduction of the 11D multiple M-wave (or M
0
-branes) system from
[
126
,
127
]
, which is similar to dimensional reduction of its
\text{D}=4
counterpart described in chapter
4
. Another representative of the family (
5.27
) with
{\cal M}=m
was studied in
[
130
]
where it was noticed that it cannot be obtained by dimensional reduction from 11D mM
0
action.

The covariant derivatives in the second line of (
5.27
)

\text{D}{\mathbb{X}}^{i}=\text{d}{\mathbb{X}}^{i}-\Omega^{ij}{\mathbb{X}}^{j}+[{\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{X}}^{i}]\;,

(5.32)

\text{D}\Psi_{q}=\text{d}{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}-{1\over 4}\Omega^{ij}\gamma^{ij}_{qp}{\bm{\Psi}}_{p}+[{\mathbb{A}},{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}]\;

(5.33)

contain, beside the SU
(N)
gauge field (
5.4
), also the composite SO
(9)
connection. The formal Ricci identities for such a covariant derivatives
40
40

40

These can be calculated on the extension of the worldline to some space of two or more dimensions. Such an extension can be realized by considering the forms depending on both differentials and variations of the variables, i.e. on
\text{d}x^{\mu}
and
\delta x^{\mu}
, etc.
read

\begin{array}[]{l}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{DD}{\mathbb{X}}^{i}=\Omega^{i}\wedge\Omega^{j}\,{\mathbb{X}}^{j}+[{\mathbb{F}},{\mathbb{X}}^{i}]\penalty\ ,\\
\text{DD}{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}=\dfrac{1}{4}\,\Omega^{i}\wedge\Omega^{j}\,(\gamma^{ij}{\bm{\Psi}})_{q}+[{\mathbb{F}},{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}]\penalty\ ,\end{array}

(5.34)

where
\mathbb{F}=\text{d}\mathbb{A}-\mathbb{A}\wedge\mathbb{A}
is the formal 2-form field strength of the 1-form gauge field
\mathbb{A}
. When deriving (
5.34
) we have used the Maurer-Cartan equations

\text{D}\Omega^{i}=\text{d}\Omega^{i}-\Omega^{j}\wedge\Omega^{ji}=0\;,\qquad\text{d}\Omega^{ij}+\Omega^{ik}\wedge\Omega^{kj}=-\Omega^{i}\wedge\Omega^{j}\;

(5.35)

which can be found by taking formal exterior derivatives of (
5.18
).

5.2

Local worldline supersymmetry

The action (
5.27
) is manifestly invariant under 10D type IIA superPoincaré transformations, including spacetime (actually target superspace) supersymmetry with constant fermionic parameters
\epsilon^{\alpha 1}
and
\epsilon_{\alpha}^{2}
acting nontrivially only on the center of mass variables,

\displaystyle\delta_{\epsilon}\theta^{1\alpha}=\epsilon^{\alpha 1}\;,\qquad\delta_{\epsilon}\theta_{\alpha}^{2}=\epsilon_{\alpha}^{2}\;,\qquad\delta_{\epsilon}v_{\alpha}^{q}=0\;\penalty\ \penalty\ ,

(5.36)

\displaystyle\delta_{\epsilon}x^{\mu}=i\theta^{1}\sigma^{\mu}\epsilon^{1}+i\theta^{2}\tilde{\sigma}{}^{\mu}\epsilon^{2}\;.

It is also invariant under the SU(
N
) gauge symmetry acting on the matrix matter fields by its adjoint representation, provided the
\mathfrak{su}(N)
valued 1-form

{\mathbb{A}}
transforms as SU
(N)
connection, as well as under the SO
(9)
symmetry acting by vector representation on index
i
of
u_{\mu}^{i}
,
{\mathbb{X}}^{i}
,
{\mathbb{P}}^{i}
and by its spinor representation on index
q
of
{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}
and
v_{\alpha}^{q}
.

Furthermore the action is invariant under local fermionic worldline supersymmetry which is parametrized by fermionic function
\kappa^{q}(\tau)
carrying spinor index of SO
(9)
. It acts on the center of mass variables exactly in the same manner as irreducible
\kappa
-symmetry of single D
0
-brane in its spinor moving frame formulation
[
130
,
168
]
(see Appendix
C
for details),

\displaystyle\delta_{\kappa}\theta^{1\alpha}={\kappa^{q}}v_{q}^{\alpha}/{\sqrt{2}}\;,\qquad\delta_{\kappa}\theta_{\alpha}^{2}=-{\kappa^{q}}v_{\alpha}{}^{q}/{\sqrt{2}}\;,\qquad

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \delta_{\kappa}x^{\mu}=i\delta_{\kappa}\theta^{1}\sigma^{\mu}\theta^{1}+i\delta_{\kappa}\theta^{2}\tilde{\sigma}{}^{\mu}\theta^{2}\;,\qquad

(5.37)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \delta_{\kappa}v_{\alpha}^{q}=0\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\delta_{\kappa}u_{{\mu}}^{0}=0=\delta_{\kappa}u_{{\mu}}^{i}\;,

hence notation
\kappa^{q}(\tau)
. On the matrix fields sector, the action of worldline supersymmetry includes nonlinear terms some of which are proportional to the derivative of the function
{\cal M}({\cal H}/{\mu^{6}})
with respect to its argument (hence to additional power of
{1}/{\mu^{6}}
),

\delta{\cal M}({\cal H}/{\mu^{6}})=\dfrac{1}{{\mu^{6}}}{\cal M}^{\prime}({\cal H}/{\mu^{6}})\,\delta{\cal H},\quad\text{with }{\cal M}^{\prime}(y)=\dfrac{\text{d}}{\text{d}y}{\cal M}(y)\;.\qquad

(5.38)

The worldline supersymmetry transformations of the matrix matter fields are (see Appendix
D
for their derivation)

\delta_{\kappa}{\mathbb{X}}^{i}=\frac{4i}{\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\,\kappa\gamma^{i}{\bm{\Psi}}+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\frac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\,\delta_{\kappa}{\cal H}\;{\mathbb{X}}^{i}-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\frac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\,\Delta_{\kappa}{\cal K}\,{\mathbb{P}}^{i}\;,

(5.39)

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{split}\delta_{\kappa}{\mathbb{P}}^{i}&=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\,[\kappa\gamma^{ij}{\bm{\Psi}},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}]-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\frac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\,\delta_{\kappa}{\cal H}{\mathbb{P}}^{i}+\\
&\penalty\ \\
&+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\frac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\Delta_{\kappa}{\cal K}\left(\frac{1}{16}[[{\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}],{\mathbb{X}}^{j}]-\gamma^{i}_{pq}\{{\bm{\Psi}}_{p},{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}\}\right),\;\end{split}\end{array}

(5.40)

\delta_{\kappa}{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}=-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\,(\kappa\gamma^{i})_{q}{\mathbb{P}}^{i}-\frac{i}{16\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\,(\kappa\gamma^{ij})_{q}[{\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}]-\frac{i}{4\mu^{6}}\,\frac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\,\Delta_{\kappa}{\cal K}\,[(\gamma^{i}{\bm{\Psi}})_{q},{\mathbb{X}}^{i}]\;.

(5.41)

Here

\displaystyle\delta_{\kappa}{\cal H}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\,\frac{{\rm tr}\left(\kappa^{q}{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}\left([{\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{P}}^{i}]-4i\{{\bm{\Psi}}_{q},{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}\}\right)\right)}{1+\dfrac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\dfrac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\,{\mathfrak{H}}}\;\qquad

(5.42)

with

{\mathfrak{H}}:=\text{tr}\left({\mathbb{P}}^{i}{\mathbb{P}}^{i}\right)+{1\over 16}\text{tr}\left[{\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}\right]^{2}+2\,\text{tr}\left({\mathbb{X}}^{i}\,\mathbf{\Psi}\gamma^{i}{\bm{\Psi}}\right)\;

(5.43)

is the worldline supersymmetry variation of the relative motion Hamiltonian (
5.28
) and

\displaystyle\Delta_{\kappa}{\cal K}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\,\frac{{\rm tr}\left(4i(\kappa\gamma^{i}{\bm{\Psi}}){\mathbb{P}}^{i}+{5\over 2}(\kappa\gamma^{ij}{\bm{\Psi}})[{\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}]\right)}{1+\dfrac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\dfrac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\,{\mathfrak{H}}}.\;

(5.44)

This latter is related to the worldline supersymmetry variation of
{\cal K}={\rm tr}({\mathbb{X}}^{i}\,{\mathbb{P}}^{i})

by

\Delta_{\kappa}{\cal K}=\delta_{\kappa}({\rm tr}({\mathbb{X}}^{i}\,{\mathbb{P}}^{i}))+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{{\cal M}}}i\kappa^{q}\nu_{q}

(5.45)

where

\displaystyle i\nu_{q}:={\rm tr}\left(-4i(\gamma^{i}{\bm{\Psi}})_{q}{\mathbb{P}}^{i}+{1\over 2}(\gamma^{ij}{\bm{\Psi}})_{q}[{\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}]\right)\;.\qquad

(5.46)

In terms of the above blocks the worldline supersymmetry variation of the SU
(N)
connection 1-form (gauge field) can be written as (see Appendix
D
for its derivation)

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}\delta_{\kappa}{\mathbb{A}}&=-\frac{2}{{\cal M}\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\,\text{E}^{0}\,(\kappa^{q}{\bm{\Psi}}_{q})\frac{\left(1-\dfrac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\dfrac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}{\cal H}\right)}{\left(1+\dfrac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\dfrac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\,{\mathfrak{H}}\right)}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}{\cal M}}\,(\text{E}^{1q}-\text{E}_{q}^{2})(\gamma^{i}\kappa)_{q}\,{\mathbb{X}}^{i}-\\
&-(\text{E}^{1q}-\text{E}_{q}^{2})\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\frac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{\sqrt{2}{\cal M}^{2}}\,\frac{1}{\left(1+\dfrac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\dfrac{{\cal M}^{\prime}}{{\cal M}}\,{\mathfrak{H}}\right)}\kappa^{p}\,{{\bm{\Psi}}_{(q}\,{\rm tr}\left[4i(\gamma^{i}{\bm{\Psi}})_{p)}{\mathbb{P}}^{i}+\frac{5}{2}(\gamma^{ij}{\bm{\Psi}})_{p)}[\mathbb{X}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}]\right]}\,.\end{split}\end{array}

(5.47)

5.3

Equations of motion

In this section we write the complete set of equations of motion for the multiple D
0
-brane system which follow from our action (
5.27
).

5.3.1

Equations for the center of mass variables

Beginning from the center of mass variables, we regroup the variation with respect to the coordinate functions,
\delta z^{M}(\tau)=(\delta x^{\mu}(\tau),\delta\theta^{\alpha 1}(\tau),\delta\theta^{2}_{\alpha}(\tau))
, into

\iota_{\delta}\text{E}^{i}=\delta z^{M}\text{E}_{M}^{a}(z)u_{a}^{i}\;,\qquad\iota_{\delta}\text{E}^{0}=\delta z^{M}\text{E}_{M}^{a}(z)u_{a}^{0}\;\qquad

(5.48)

and

\iota_{\delta}(\text{E}^{q1}-\text{E}_{q}^{2})\;,\qquad\iota_{\delta}(\text{E}^{q1}+\text{E}_{q}^{2})\;,\qquad

(5.49)

where

\iota_{\delta}\text{E}^{q1}=\delta\theta^{\alpha 1}v_{\alpha}{}^{q}\;,\qquad\iota_{\delta}\text{E}_{q}^{2}=\delta\theta^{2}_{\alpha}v_{q}{}^{\alpha}\;.\qquad

(5.50)

This choice simplifies the form of the equations of motion for the coordinate functions (originally defined as
\frac{\delta S_{\text{mD}0}}{\delta z^{M}}=0
) and splits their set in a Lorentz-covariant manner into

\displaystyle\Omega^{i}\left(m+\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{M}}\right)=0\;,\qquad

(5.51)

\displaystyle\left(m+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{M}}\right)\left(\text{E}^{1q}+\text{E}^{2}_{q}\right)=\frac{-i}{4\sqrt{2\mathcal{M}}\mu^{6}}\gamma^{i}_{qp}i\nu_{p}\Omega^{i}\;,

(5.52)

and

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\mathcal{M}}\left(1-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{H}\right)\text{d}\mathcal{H}=0\;,\qquad

(5.53)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\mathcal{M}}}i\text{D}\nu_{q}-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2\mathcal{M}}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}i\nu_{q}\text{d}\mathcal{H}-\frac{2i}{\mathcal{M}}\left(\text{E}^{1q}-\text{E}^{2}_{q}\right)\mathcal{H}=0\;,

(5.54)

where

i\nu_{q}:={\rm tr}\left(-4i(\gamma^{i}{\bm{\Psi}})_{q}{\mathbb{P}}^{i}+{1\over 2}(\gamma^{ij}{\bm{\Psi}})_{q}[{\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}]\right)\;.\qquad

(5.55)

As we will show below, Eqs. (
5.53
) and (
5.54
) are dependent, meaning they are satisfied identically when the other equations of motion are taken into account. This reflects the Noether identities associated with the worldline reparametrization invariance and the worldline supersymmetry (
\kappa
-symmetry) whose “local parameters” can be identified with

\iota_{\delta}\text{E}^{0}
and
\iota_{\delta}(\text{E}^{q1}-\text{E}_{q}^{2})
, respectively. With this in mind, we begin by analysing (
5.51
) and (
5.52
), along with the equations for the spinor frame variables. We then proceed to the matrix equations, which will allow us to confirm the aforementioned dependence of (
5.53
) and (
5.54
)).

Given that the function
{\cal M}(\mathcal{H}/\mu^{6})
appearing in our action is positive definite (as stated in (
5.30
)), Eq. (
5.51
) immediately implies

\Omega^{i}=0\,.

(5.56)

Using this result, the fermionic equation (
5.52
) simplifies to

\text{E}^{1q}+\text{E}^{2}_{q}=0\;.

(5.57)

Finally, the essential variations of the moving frame and spinor moving frame variables are given by (
5.22
), (
5.23
)

\delta u_{\mu}^{0}=u_{\mu}^{i}\iota_{\delta}\Omega^{i}\,,\qquad\delta v_{\alpha}{}^{q}=\frac{1}{2}\,\iota_{\delta}\Omega^{i}\,\gamma^{i}_{qp}v_{\alpha}{}^{p}\,,\qquad\delta v_{q}{}^{\alpha}=-\frac{1}{2}\,\iota_{\delta}\Omega^{i}\,v_{p}{}^{\alpha}\gamma^{i}_{pq}\,,\qquad

(5.58)

these leads to the bosonic vector equation

\text{E}^{i}\left(m+\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{M}}\right)-\frac{1}{2\mu^{6}\sqrt{2\mathcal{M}}}\left(\text{E}^{1q}+\text{E}^{2}_{q}\right)\gamma^{i}_{qp}i\nu_{p}-\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\,\text{tr}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\left[i\right.}\mathbb{X}^{\left.j\right]}+i{\bm{\Psi}}\gamma^{ij}{\bm{\Psi}}\right)\,\Omega^{j}=0\;,

(5.59)

where
i\nu_{q}
is defined in (
5.55
). Substituting (
5.56
) and the fermionic equations (
5.57
) into this, we find that (
5.59
) reduces to the simple condition

\text{E}^{i}=0\;.

(5.60)

Thus the complete set of equations governing the center of mass sector is given by
Eqs. (
5.56
), (
5.57
) and (
5.60
). This coincides precisely with the set of equations of motion for a single D
0
-brane in its spinor moving frame formulation
[
168
]
.

5.3.2

Equations of the relative motion of the constituents of mD0 system

The equations of motion for the matrix fields describing the relative motion of the mD
0
constituents read

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}\text{D}\mathbb{X}^{i}=-\frac{2}{\mathcal{M}}\left(1-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{H}\right)&\text{E}^{0}\mathbb{P}^{i}+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\left(\mathbb{X}^{i}\text{d}\mathcal{H}-\mathbb{P}^{i}\text{d}\mathcal{K}\right)\penalty\ +\qquad\\
&\\
&+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\mathcal{M}}}\left(\text{E}^{1q}-\text{E}^{2}_{q}\right)\left(4i\left(\gamma^{i}{\bm{\Psi}}\right)_{q}-\frac{1}{2\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}i\nu_{q}\mathbb{P}^{i}\right)\;,\end{split}\end{array}

(5.61)

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}\text{D}\mathbb{P}^{i}&=\frac{2}{\mathcal{M}}\left[\left(1-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{H}\right)\text{E}^{0}+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{4\sqrt{2\mathcal{M}}}\left(\text{E}^{1q}-\text{E}^{2}_{q}\right)i\nu_{q}\right]\times\\
&\penalty\ \\
&\times\left(\frac{1}{16}\left[[\mathbb{X}^{i},\mathbb{X}^{j}],\mathbb{X}^{j}\right]-\gamma^{i}_{pr}\left\{{\bm{\Psi}}_{p},{\bm{\Psi}}_{r}\right\}\right)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\mathcal{M}}}\left(\text{E}^{1q}-\text{E}^{2}_{q}\right)[\left(\gamma^{ij}{\bm{\Psi}}\right)_{q},\mathbb{X}^{j}]+\\
&\penalty\ \\
&+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\text{d}\mathcal{K}\left(\frac{1}{16}\left[[\mathbb{X}^{i},\mathbb{X}^{j}],\mathbb{X}^{j}\right]-\gamma^{i}_{pr}\left\{{\bm{\Psi}}_{p},{\bm{\Psi}}_{r}\right\}\right)-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{P}^{i}\text{d}\mathcal{H}\;,\end{split}\end{array}

(5.62)

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{D}{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}&=-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2\mathcal{M}}}\left(\text{E}^{1p}-\text{E}^{2}_{p}\right)\left(\gamma^{i}_{pq}\mathbb{P}^{i}+\frac{i}{8}\gamma^{ij}_{pq}[\mathbb{X}^{i},\mathbb{X}^{j}]\right)\penalty\ -\\
&\penalty\ \\
&-\frac{i}{2\mathcal{M}}\left[\left(1-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{H}\right)\text{E}^{0}+\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{2\mu^{6}}\text{d}\mathcal{K}+\frac{1}{4\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\sqrt{2\mathcal{M}}}\left(\text{E}^{1p}-\text{E}^{2}_{p}\right)i\nu_{p}\right][(\gamma^{i}{\bm{\Psi}})_{q},\mathbb{X}^{i}]\end{split}\end{array}

(5.63)

and

[\mathbb{X}^{i},\mathbb{P}^{i}]=4i\left\{{\bm{\Psi}}_{q},{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}\right\}\penalty\ .

(5.64)

In Eqs. (
5.61
)-(
5.63
)

{\cal H}
is given in (
5.28
),

{\cal K}={\rm tr}({\mathbb{X}}^{i}\,{\mathbb{P}}^{i})\;

(5.65)

and
i\nu_{q}
is defined in (
5.55
).
Eq. (
5.64
) appears as a result of variation with respect to the worldline gauge field
\mathbb{A}=\text{d}\tau\mathbb{A}_{\tau}
and is the (non-Abelian version of the) Gauss law of 10D SYM reduced to 1d.

Using Eqs. (
5.61
)-(
5.63
) and (
5.64
) one can check (by straightforward although a bit involving calculations) that

\text{d}{\cal H}=0

(5.66)

and

i\text{D}\nu_{q}=\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{{\cal M}}}(\text{E}^{1q}-\text{E}_{q}^{2}){\cal H}\;,

(5.67)

which implies that Eqs. (
5.53
) and (
5.54
) are satisfied identically (as we have announced and discussed just after their derivation).

Taking into account (
5.66
) we find that the equations for bosonic matrix matter fields, (
5.61
) and (
5.62
) simplify a bit:

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{D}\mathbb{X}^{i}&=-\frac{2}{\mathcal{M}}\left(1-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{H}\right)\text{E}^{0}\mathbb{P}^{i}-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{P}^{i}\text{d}\mathcal{K}\penalty\ +\qquad\\
&\penalty\ \\
&\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\mathcal{M}}}\left(\text{E}^{1q}-\text{E}^{2}_{q}\right)\left(4i\left(\gamma^{i}{\bm{\Psi}}\right)_{q}-\frac{1}{2\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}i\nu_{q}\mathbb{P}^{i}\right)\;,\end{split}\end{array}

(5.68)

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}\text{D}\mathbb{P}^{i}&=\frac{2}{\mathcal{M}}\left[\left(1-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{H}\right)\text{E}^{0}+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{4\sqrt{2\mathcal{M}}}\left(\text{E}^{1q}-\text{E}^{2}_{q}\right)i\nu_{q}\right]\times\qquad\qquad\\
&\penalty\ \\
&\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \times\left(\frac{1}{16}\left[[\mathbb{X}^{i},\mathbb{X}^{j}],\mathbb{X}^{j}\right]-\gamma^{i}_{pr}\left\{{\bm{\Psi}}_{p},{\bm{\Psi}}_{r}\right\}\right)\penalty\ .\end{split}\end{array}

(5.69)

This form of equations is not yet final since it involves
\text{d}\mathcal{K}={\rm tr}\left(\text{D}\mathbb{X}^{i}\,\mathbb{P}^{i}+\mathbb{X}^{i}\text{D}\mathbb{P}^{i}\right)
. Calculating the r.h.s. of this expression with the use of (
5.68
) and (
5.69
), we find

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}\text{d}\mathcal{K}&=-\frac{2}{{\cal M}}\frac{\mathfrak{H}}{\left(1+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathfrak{H}\right)}\left(1-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{H}\right)\text{E}^{0}+\frac{(\text{E}^{1q}-\text{E}^{2}_{q})}{\sqrt{2{\cal M}}}\times\qquad\qquad\\
&\penalty\ \\
&\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \times\frac{{\rm tr}\left(4i(\gamma^{i}{\bm{\Psi}})_{q}{\mathbb{P}}^{i}+(\gamma^{ij}{\bm{\Psi}})_{q}[{\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}]\right)-\frac{1}{2\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathfrak{H}\,i\nu_{q}}{\left(1+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathfrak{H}\right)}\,\end{split}\end{array}

(5.70)

with

{\mathfrak{H}}:=\text{tr}\left({\mathbb{P}}^{i}{\mathbb{P}}^{i}\right)+{1\over 16}\text{tr}\left[{\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}\right]^{2}+2\,\text{tr}\left({\mathbb{X}}^{i}\,{\bm{\Psi}}\gamma^{i}{\bm{\Psi}}\right)\;.

(5.71)

Thus the final form of the equations for the matrix matter (or relative motion) fields is given by
(
5.68
), (
5.69
) and (
5.63
) with
\text{d}\mathcal{K}
substituted by the r.h.s. of (
5.70
). While the resulting expressions may appear frighteningly complicated, we will show that they can be significantly simplified by an appropriate choice of gauge fixing for the symmetries of the system. Before proceeding with the gauge fixing, however, we must first address the issue of the mass of the mD
0
system.

5.3.3

Mass of mD0 system and its center of mass motion

It is instructive to compute the canonical momentum for the center of mass coordinate function of mD
0
system

p_{\mu}=\left(m+\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{M}}\,\right)u_{\mu}^{0}=:\mathfrak{M}u_{\mu}^{0}\,.

(5.72)

The mass
\mathfrak{M}
of the mD
0
is defined by the square of this 10-momentum,
p_{\mu}p^{\mu}=\mathfrak{M}^{2}
, and is thus given by

\mathfrak{M}=m+\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{M}}\,\,.

(5.73)

Note that this mass depends essentially on the choice of the positive definite function
{\mathcal{M}}(\mathcal{H}/\mu^{6})
in the action (
5.27
).
However, as a consequence of (
5.66
), this mass is conserved,

\text{d}\mathfrak{M}=0\;.

(5.74)

It is important that the mass depends on the relative motion of the mD
0
constituents. As a result, in this supersymmetric model, the relative motion influences the center of mass motion in the same way it does in the purely bosonic dynamical system from
[
109
]
.

The constant parameter
m
in the action (
5.27
) corresponds to the mass of the center of mass motion of mD
0
when the relative motion sector is in its ground state, i.e. when
{\cal H}=0
. As we will see below, supersymmetric configurations of mD
0
systems satisfy this condition.
Therefore, any excitation of the relative motion inevitably increases the mass of the center of mass of the mD
0
system (due to the positivity condition (
5.30
)) and, as we will also show, necessarily breaks supersymmetry.

Now observe that, by definition of Cartan forms,
\text{d}u_{\mu}^{0}=u_{\mu}^{i}\Omega^{i}
. Hence, Eq. (
5.56
) implies that on-shell we have

\text{d}u_{\mu}^{0}=0\;.

(5.75)

Together with (
5.74
), this implies that the momentum (
5.72
) is conserved

\text{d}p_{\mu}=0\penalty\ .

(5.76)

5.3.4

Gauge fixing of the local SO(9) and SU(
N
) symmetries

For future use, let us also notice that, on the surface defined by Eq. (
5.56
), the derivatives of the orthogonal moving frame vectors
u_{\mu}^{i}
decompose entirely along these vectors,

\text{d}u_{\mu}^{i}=\Omega^{ij}u_{\mu}^{j}\;.

(5.77)

Furthermore, since
\Omega^{ij}
transforms as a connection under local gauge
SO
(9)
symmetry of the mD
0
action (
5.27
), and since any 1-dimensional connection can be gauged away, we can fix the gauge

\Omega^{ij}=0

(5.78)

in which the 9 spacelike vectors of the moving frame become constant

\text{d}u_{\mu}^{i}=0\;.

(5.79)

Similarly, the local
\text{SU}(N)
gauge symmetry can be used to set the
\mathfrak{su}(N)
valued gauge field to zero,

{\mathbb{A}}=0\;.

(5.80)

In this gauge, the covariant derivative
\text{D}=\text{d}\tau\text{D}_{\tau}
reduces to a (proper-)time derivative,

\text{D}{\mathbb{X}}^{i}=\text{d}\tau\frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}\tau}{\mathbb{X}}^{i}=\text{d}\tau\dot{{\mathbb{X}}}{}^{i}\;,\qquad\text{D}{\mathbb{P}}^{i}=\text{d}\tau\frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}\tau}{\mathbb{P}}^{i}=\text{d}\tau\dot{{\mathbb{P}}}{}^{i}\;,\qquad\text{D}{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}=\text{d}\tau\frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}\tau}{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}=\text{d}\tau\dot{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}\;.\qquad

(5.81)

Notice that, as far as moving frame and spinor moving frame variables are now (proper-)time independent, we obtain

\text{E}^{0}=\text{d}\tau\text{E}_{\tau}^{0}=\text{d}x^{{\mathbf{0}}}-i\text{d}\theta^{1q}\,\theta^{1q}-i\text{d}\theta_{q}^{2}\,\theta_{q}^{2}\;,\qquad\text{E}^{q1}=\text{d}\theta^{1q}\;,\qquad\text{E}_{q}^{2}=\text{d}\theta_{q}^{2}\;,\qquad

(5.82)

and

\text{E}^{i}=\text{d}\tau\text{E}_{\tau}^{i}=\text{d}x^{{\mathbf{i}}}-i\text{d}\theta^{1q}\gamma^{i}_{qp}\theta^{1p}+i\text{d}\theta_{q}^{2}\gamma^{i}_{qp}\theta_{p}^{2}\;,\qquad

(5.83)

where

x^{{\mathbf{0}}}=x^{\mu}u_{\mu}^{0}\;,\qquad x^{{\mathbf{i}}}=x^{\mu}u_{\mu}^{i}\;,\qquad\theta^{1q}=\theta^{\alpha 1}v_{\alpha}{}^{q}\;,\qquad\theta^{2}_{q}=\theta^{2}_{\alpha}v^{\alpha}_{q}\;\qquad

(5.84)

are the supersymmetric generalizations of co-moving coordinates for the center of mass of the mD
0
-brane.

5.3.5

Gauge fixing of
\kappa
-symmetry and reparametrization symmetry

Using the consequences (
5.82
) and the gauge fixing condition (
5.78
), Eq. (
5.57
) simplifies to

\text{d}\theta^{1q}=-\text{d}\theta_{q}^{2}\;,\qquad

(5.85)

This equation is invariant under global supersymmetry and local
\kappa
-symmetry, under which (in the coordinate basis (
5.84
)) we have

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\delta\theta^{1q}=\epsilon^{1q}+\kappa^{q}/\sqrt{2}\;,\qquad\quad\delta\theta_{q}^{2}=\epsilon_{q}^{2}-\kappa^{q}/\sqrt{2}\penalty\ ,\\
\\
\delta x^{{\mathbf{0}}}=i\text{d}\theta^{1q}\,(\epsilon^{1q}-\kappa^{q}/\sqrt{2})+i\theta_{q}^{2}\,(\epsilon_{q}^{2}+\kappa^{q}/\sqrt{2})\penalty\ .\end{array}

(5.86)

This implies

\delta\text{E}^{0}=-2i(\text{d}\theta^{1q}-\text{d}\theta_{q}^{2})\kappa^{q}/\sqrt{2}\;.

(5.87)

Clearly, we can now use the worldline supersymmetry to fix one (but not both) of the fermionic coordinate functions to zero (as the
\kappa
-symmetry of single D
0
-brane can be). Let us choose

\theta_{q}^{2}=0\;.

(5.88)

This gauge is preserved by a combination of global supersymmetry and local worldline supersymmetry satisfying

\epsilon^{2}_{q}=\kappa^{q}/\sqrt{2}\;.

(5.89)

This condition also implies that the local worldline supersymmetry parameter becomes a constant spinor at this stage. In this gauge (
5.88
), Eq. (
5.85
) simplifies to

\text{d}\theta^{1q}=0\;,\qquad

(5.90)

which further reduces
\text{E}^{0}
and
\text{E}^{i}
to:

\text{E}^{0}=\text{d}x^{\mathbf{0}}\penalty\ ,\qquad\text{E}^{i}=\text{d}x^{\mathbf{i}}\penalty\ .

(5.91)

This gauge choice remains supersymmetric because now

\delta x^{{\mathbf{0}}}=i(\epsilon^{1q}+\epsilon^{2}_{q})\theta^{1q}\;,\qquad\delta x^{{\mathbf{i}}}=i(\epsilon^{1q}+\epsilon^{2}_{q})\gamma^{i}_{qp}\theta^{1p}\;

(5.92)

and both r.h.s.-s are constants due to (
5.90
),
\text{d}v_{\alpha}^{q}=0
and
\text{d}\epsilon^{\alpha 1,2}=0
.

Having fixed
\kappa
-symmetry, we may now fix the reparametrization symmetry by setting

\text{d}\tau=\text{d}x^{{\mathbf{0}}}=\text{E}^{0}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\text{E}_{\tau}^{0}=\dot{x}^{{\mathbf{0}}}=1\;

(5.93)

still preserving the supersymmetry. However, for convenience, we will not fix
\tau
-reparametrization symmetry at this stage and instead preserve its explicit form for subsequent analysis.

5.3.6

Gauge fixed form of the field equations

Thus, the above gauge fixing and the field equations for the center of mass variables imply that

\displaystyle\text{d}u^{0}_{\mu}=0\;,\qquad\text{d}u^{i}_{\mu}=0\;,\qquad\text{d}v_{\alpha}^{q}=0\;,

(5.94)

\displaystyle\qquad\text{E}^{i}=\text{d}x^{{\mathbf{i}}}=0\;,

(5.95)

\displaystyle\text{E}^{1q}=0\;,\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{E}^{2}_{q}=0\;,\penalty\

(5.96)

and that
\text{E}^{0}=\text{d}\tau\dot{x}^{\mathbf{0}}
. With this in mind, the equations for the matrix fields reduce to

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \dot{\mathbb{X}}^{i}=-\frac{2}{\mathcal{M}}\left(1-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{H}\right)\dot{x}^{\mathbf{0}}\mathbb{P}^{i}-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{P}^{i}\dot{\mathcal{K}}\;,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\

(5.97)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \dot{\mathbb{P}}^{i}=\left[\frac{2}{\mathcal{M}}\left(1-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{H}\right)\dot{x}^{\mathbf{0}}+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\dot{\mathcal{K}}\right]\left(\frac{1}{16}\left[[\mathbb{X}^{i},\mathbb{X}^{j}],\mathbb{X}^{j}\right]-\gamma^{i}_{pr}\left\{{\bm{\Psi}}_{p},{\bm{\Psi}}_{r}\right\}\right)\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\

(5.98)

\displaystyle\dot{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}=-\frac{i}{2\mathcal{M}}\left[\left(1-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{H}\right)\dot{x}^{\mathbf{0}}+\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{2\mu^{6}}\dot{\mathcal{K}}\right][(\gamma^{i}{\bm{\Psi}})_{q},\mathbb{X}^{i}]\;,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\

(5.99)

where

\dot{\mathcal{K}}=-\frac{2}{{\cal M}}\frac{\mathfrak{H}}{\left(1+\dfrac{1}{\mu^{6}}\dfrac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathfrak{H}\right)}\,\left(1-\dfrac{1}{\mu^{6}}\dfrac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{H}\right)\,\dot{x}^{\mathbf{0}}\qquad

(5.100)

with
{\mathfrak{H}}
defined in (
5.71
). Substituting (
5.100
), we find that the final gauge fixed form of Eqs. (
5.97
)-(
5.99
) is

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \dot{\mathbb{X}}{}^{i}=-\frac{2}{\mathcal{M}}\,\frac{\left(1-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{H}\right)}{\left(1+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathfrak{H}\right)}\dot{x}^{\mathbf{0}}\,\mathbb{P}^{i}\;,\qquad

(5.101)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \dot{\mathbb{P}}{}^{i}=\frac{2}{\mathcal{M}}\frac{\left(1-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{H}\right)}{\left(1+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathfrak{H}\right)}\dot{x}^{\mathbf{0}}\,\left(\frac{1}{16}\left[[\mathbb{X}^{i},\mathbb{X}^{j}],\mathbb{X}^{j}\right]-\gamma^{i}_{pr}\left\{{\bm{\Psi}}_{p},{\bm{\Psi}}_{r}\right\}\right)\;,\qquad

(5.102)

\displaystyle\dot{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}=-\frac{i}{2\mathcal{M}}\,\frac{\left(1-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{H}\right)}{\left(1+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathfrak{H}\right)}\,\dot{x}^{\mathbf{0}}[(\gamma^{i}{\bm{\Psi}})_{q},\mathbb{X}^{i}]\;.

(5.103)

Notice that, although one might fix the gauge
\dot{x}^{\mathbf{0}}=1
(
5.93
), we prefer to keep it unfixed to emphasize the invariance of our equations of motion under
\tau
-reparametrization, a feature that will be important in the discussion below.

5.3.7

Relation of the relative motion equations of mD0 with SYM equations

Formally, we can define the new time variable by

\text{d}t=\text{d}x^{{\mathbf{0}}}\,\frac{2}{\mathcal{M}}\frac{\left(1-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{H}\right)}{\left(1+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathfrak{H}\right)}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\frac{\text{d}t(\tau)}{\text{d}\tau}=\dot{x}^{{\mathbf{0}}}\,\frac{2}{\mathcal{M}}\frac{\left(1-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{H}\right)}{\left(1+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{M^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{M}}\mathfrak{H}\right)}\;,

(5.104)

and rewrite the above equations using the derivative
\frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}t}
arriving at

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}t}{\mathbb{X}}{}^{i}=-\,\mathbb{P}^{i}\;,\qquad

(5.105)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}t}{\mathbb{P}}{}^{i}=\frac{1}{16}\left[[\mathbb{X}^{i},\mathbb{X}^{j}],\mathbb{X}^{j}\right]-\gamma^{i}_{pr}\left\{{\bm{\Psi}}_{p},{\bm{\Psi}}_{r}\right\}\;,\qquad

(5.106)

\displaystyle\frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}t}{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}=-\frac{i}{4}\,[(\gamma^{i}{\bm{\Psi}})_{q},\mathbb{X}^{i}]\;

(5.107)

which coincide with the equations of motion of the 1d reduction of 10D SYM theory in the gauge
{\mathbb{A}}=0
.

However, this procedure does not correspond to a
\tau
-reparametrization (i.e. a 1d general coordinate transformation of the proper time). The simplest way to see this is to appreciate that the definition of (
5.104
) for the “new time” is actually invariant under the
\tau
-reparametrization and therefore cannot serve as a gauge fixing condition for this symmetry
41
41

41

In fact, since equations (
5.97
)-(
5.99
) are manifestly reparametrization invariant, they cannot be transformed into (
5.105
)-(
5.107
) via a
\tau
-reparametrization.
.

The other more physical observation is that changing the form of the positive definite function
{\cal M}({\cal H})
affects the physical properties of the system. In particular, it changes the mass
{\mathfrak{M}}
of the mD
0
system (
cf.
(
5.73
)), which depends on the choice of
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H})
. Hence, such a change cannot be achieved by transformation of a gauge
symmetry of the system.

Thus what we have found is not a kind of gauge equivalence, but rather an interesting correspondence between the gauge fixed form of the relative motion equations of our mD
0
system and those of maximally supersymmetric
{\cal N}=16
1d SU(
N
) SYM theory. Note, however, that the formal definition (
5.104
) of the new time variable involves the matrix fields
{\mathbb{X}}^{i}
,
{\mathbb{P}}^{i}
,
{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}
and thus, in general, differs for different solutions of the field equations. Indeed, while
{\cal H}
(and hence
{\cal M}({\cal H})
) is constant on-shell, the same is not true for
{\mathfrak{H}}={\mathfrak{H}}({\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{P}}^{i},{\bm{\Psi}}_{q})
.
However, for special solutions with
\mathcal{H}=0
(implying also
\mathfrak{H}=0
), the variable
t
becomes proportional to
x^{\mathbf{0}}
via a constant rescaling. In this case, the correspondence between the mD
0
relative motion solutions and SU(
N
) SYM equations is one-to-one.

As we will see in a moment, this situation arises for bosonic supersymmetric solutions of the mD
0
equations.

5.4

Supersymmetric bosonic solutions

As we have discussed in sec.
5.3.5
, on the mass shell, one of two center of mass fermions can be gauged away using worldline supersymmetry (
\kappa
-symmetry), as shown in (
5.88
), and then the remaining fermionic coordinate function becomes constant (
5.90
). By setting this constant to zero as well, we arrive at a purely bosonic center of mass configuration with

\theta^{\alpha 1}=0\;,\qquad\theta_{\alpha}^{2}=0\;.

(5.108)

This configuration still preserves half of the target (super)space supersymmetry, with the two supersymmetry spinor parameters related to a single
\text{SO}(9)
spinor parameter of the worldline supersymmetry via constant spinor frame variables (fixed in the gauge (
5.94
)) by

\epsilon^{\alpha 1}=-\kappa^{q}v_{q}^{\alpha}/\sqrt{2}\;,\qquad\epsilon_{\alpha}^{2}=\kappa^{q}v_{\alpha}^{q}/\sqrt{2}\;.

(5.109)

Then, after fixing the reparametrization invariance by setting
x^{{\mathbf{0}}}(\tau)=\tau
, the general solution of the bosonic center of mass equations takes the form

x^{\mu}(\tau)=x^{\mu}_{0}+\frac{p^{\mu}}{\mathfrak{M}}\tau\;,

(5.110)

where
p^{\mu}
and
\mathfrak{M}
are defined in (
5.72
) and (
5.73
). These are constants due to (
5.66
) which is the Noether identity associated with reparametrization symmetry. Let us stress that any of these bosonic solutions describing the center of mass motion preserves
1/2
of the type IIA spacetime supersymmetry, which can be preserved or broken by the relative motion of the mD
0
constituents.

Originally, the matrix fields that encode the relative motion of the mD
0
constituents are inert under spacetime supersymmetry. However, after gauge fixing the worldline supersymmetry via Eq. (
5.88
), this is not longer the case. The worldline supersymmetry parameter, which acts on the matrix fields, becomes identified with the second spacetime supersymmetry parameter through Eq.(
5.89
). Furthermore, setting the remaining fermionic coordinate function to zero, (
5.108
), similarly identifies it with the first supersymmetry parameter (
5.109
). As a result, spacetime supersymmetry now acts nontrivially on the matrix fields, coinciding with worldline supersymmetry, now parametrised by a constant fermionic spinor
\kappa^{q}
.

Setting to zero the fermionic matrix field

{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}=0

(5.111)

we can still preserve the worldline supersymmetry (completely or partially), and hence also a part of the spacetime supersymmetry if the following Killing spinor equation (see (
5.41
))

\delta_{\kappa}{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}=-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\,(\kappa\gamma^{i})_{q}{\mathbb{P}}^{i}-\frac{i}{16\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\,(\kappa\gamma^{ij})_{q}[{\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}]=0\;

(5.112)

admits a nontrivial solution. Notice that the contribution of the positive definite function
{\cal M}({\cal H})
can be factored out, thus reducing the equation to

\kappa^{p}{\mathbb{L}}_{pq}:=\kappa^{p}\left(\gamma^{i}_{pq}{\mathbb{P}}^{i}-\frac{i}{8}\,(\gamma^{ij})_{pq}[{\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}]\right)=0\;\;

(5.113)

which formally coincides with the BPS condition in the case of (the first order formulation of) the maximally supersymmetric SU(
N
) SYM theory
42
42

42

The term “formally” highlights that the relation between
{\mathbb{P}}^{i}
and
\dot{{\mathbb{X}}}^{i}
in the generic mD
0
action involves the function
{\cal M}({\cal H})
and, for
{\cal M}^{\prime}\not=0
, additional dependence on
\mathbb{P}^{i}
and
\mathbb{X}^{i}
through the function
\mathfrak{H}
defined in Eq.(
5.71
); see also Eq.(
5.102
).
. It also formally coincides with the equation defining the supersymmetry which can be preserved by purely bosonic solutions of 11D mM
0
equations
[
126
]
, and thus our discussion below parallels that analysis in
[
126
]
.

Preservation of the 1/2 of the global supersymmetry implies, in the light of (
5.109
), that Eq. (
5.113
) does not impose any restriction on the parameter
\kappa^{q}
. This, in turn, requires that the matrix
{\mathbb{L}}_{pq}
vanishes, i.e.
\gamma^{i}_{pq}{\mathbb{P}}^{i}-\frac{i}{8}\,(\gamma^{ij})_{pq}[{\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}]=0
. Then

{\mathbb{P}}^{i}=0\;,\qquad[{\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}]=0\,,

(5.114)

which characterizes the vacuum of the relative motion of the mD
0
constituents. In this case
{\cal H}=0
and, indeed (
5.114
) follows directly from this condition.

To examine whether more parts of spacetime supersymmetry can be preserved we analyze Eq. (
5.113
), which is actually a set of 16 traceless SU(
N
) matrix equations. To reduce it to a more manageable form, we multiply it by the matrix

\tilde{{\mathbb{L}}}_{qr}:=\left(\gamma^{i}_{qr}{\mathbb{P}}^{i}+\frac{i}{8}\,(\gamma^{ij})_{qr}[{\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}]\right)\penalty\ ,

(5.115)

which differs by relative sign from the matrix
{{\mathbb{L}}}_{qr}
in (
5.113
), and trace the result with respect to the
\text{SU}(N)
indices. In such a way we arrive at

\kappa^{p}{\rm tr}({\mathbb{L}}\tilde{\mathbb{L}})_{pq}:=\kappa^{q}\left({\rm tr}({\mathbb{P}}^{i}{\mathbb{P}}^{i})-\frac{1}{32}\,{\rm tr}([{\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}]^{2})\right)+\frac{i}{4}\,\kappa^{p}(\gamma^{j})_{pq}{\rm tr}({\mathbb{P}}^{i}[{\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}])=0\;,

(5.116)

where we have used

\gamma^{ijkl}{\rm tr}\left([\mathbb{X}^{i},\mathbb{X}^{j}][\mathbb{X}^{k},\mathbb{X}^{l}]\right)=\gamma^{ijkl}\penalty\ {\rm tr}\left(\mathbb{X}^{i}[\mathbb{X}^{j},[\mathbb{X}^{k},\mathbb{X}^{l}]]\right)=0\penalty\ ,

(5.117)

which follows from the Jacobi identity
\left[\mathbb{X}^{[j},\left[\mathbb{X}^{k},\mathbb{X}^{l]}\right]\right]\equiv 0
. Now one can recognize in the multiplier of the first term the bosonic limit of the relative motion Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
(
5.28
) multiplied by
2
and also appreciate that the coefficient for the second term vanishes as a consequence of the (bosonic limit of the) Gauss constraint (
5.64
),
{\rm tr}({\mathbb{P}}^{i}[{\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}])={\rm tr}([{\mathbb{P}}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{i}]{\mathbb{X}}^{j})=0
. Thus, if we use this constraint, the final form of the consistency condition (
5.116
) for the supersymmetry preservation by mD
0
configuration simplifies to

\kappa^{q}\mathcal{H}=0\penalty\ .

(5.118)

Thus the BPS condition for a purely bosonic supersymmetric configuration of the mD
0
system is just the vanishing of the (bosonic limit of the) relative motion Hamiltonian,

\displaystyle{\cal H}={1\over 2}\text{tr}\left({\mathbb{P}}^{i}{\mathbb{P}}^{i}\right)-{1\over 64}\text{tr}\left[{\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}\right]^{2}=0\;.\quad

(5.119)

As we have already noticed, the general solution is given by the vacuum (
5.114
), meaning that only
1/2
of the spacetime supersymmetry can be preserved by the mD
0
system.

This result allows us to conclude that any supersymmetric solution of the maximally supersymmetric SU(
N
) SYM theory yields a corresponding supersymmetric solution of the mD
0
equations for any positive definite function
{\cal M}({\cal H})
since the relative motion mD
0
equations then differ from the SYM equations by rescaling of the time variable by the constant factor
{\cal M}(0)/2
. These solutions form a family characterized by distinct properties of the center of mass motion
x^{\mu}_{0}
and
p^{\mu}=\mathfrak{M}u^{0\mu}
in (
5.110
). Thus, as far as the supersymmetric configurations of the relative motion sector in the mD
0
model is concerned, these are in one-to-one correspondence with those solutions of the SU(
N
) SYM equations.

The correspondence of the relative motion equations of the mD
0
brane constituents and the maximally supersymmetric
\text{d}=1

{\cal N}=16
SU(
N
) SYM equations can be also used to search for non-supersymmetric solutions of the mD0 equations. See Appendix
E
for an example.

6
Dimensional reduction of the 11D mM0 action and its relation to mD0 system

No, not Northward. Upward.

Flatland: A Romance of Many Dimensions

by Edwin A. Abbott.

In this chapter we demonstrate that a specific representative of the family of the candidate mD
0
actions (
5.27
), namely the one with
{\cal M}({\cal H}/\mu^{6})
as given in Eq.(
4.71
), can be derived by dimensional reduction of the 11D mM
0
action proposed in
[
126
]
.

6.1

11D mM0 action and its symmetries

The mM
0
brane system, which is conjectured to arise as the decompactification limit (M-theory lifting) of mD
0
system, contains the same matrix fields as the
\text{d}=1

{\cal N}=16
SYM on its worldline. However, as we will see, the set of variables describing the center of energy sector differs from that of the mD
0
system.

6.1.1

Center of energy variables for mM0

The center of energy degrees of freedom include the coordinate functions

z^{\underline{M}}(\tau)=(X^{\underline{\mu}}(\tau),\Theta^{\underline{\alpha}}(\tau))\;,\qquad{\underline{\mu}}=0,1,\ldots,9,10\;,\qquad{\underline{\alpha}}=1,\ldots,32\;

(6.1)

which describe the embedding of the worldline into the 11D superspace
\Sigma^{(11|32)}
with coordinates
z^{\underline{M}}=(X^{\underline{\mu}},\Theta^{\underline{\alpha}})
. These are supplemented by spinor moving frame fields (described below) and a Lagrange multiplier

\rho^{\#}(\tau)
whose role will also be clarified below.

The 11D VA 1-form is given by

\Pi^{\underline{\mu}}=\text{d}X^{\underline{\mu}}-i\text{d}\Theta\Gamma^{\underline{\mu}}\Theta=(\Pi^{\mu},\Pi^{*})\penalty\ ,

(6.2)

where
\Gamma^{\underline{\mu}}_{\underline{\alpha}\underline{\beta}}
are real symmetric
32\times 32
matrices

\Gamma^{\underline{\mu}}_{\underline{\alpha}\underline{\beta}}=\Gamma^{\underline{\mu}}{}_{\underline{\alpha}}{}^{\underline{\gamma}}C_{\underline{\gamma}\underline{\beta}}=\Gamma^{\underline{\mu}}_{\underline{\beta}\underline{\alpha}}\penalty\

(6.3)

constructed from the 11D Dirac matrices
\Gamma^{\underline{\mu}}{}_{\underline{\alpha}}{}^{\underline{\gamma}}=-(\Gamma^{\underline{\mu}}{}_{\underline{\alpha}}{}^{\underline{\gamma}})^{*}
obeying the Clifford algebra

\Gamma^{a}\Gamma^{b}+\Gamma^{b}\Gamma^{a}=2\eta^{ab}{\mathbb{1}}_{32\times 32}

(6.4)

and the charge conjugation matrix
C
, which is antisymmetric and imaginary in our mostly minus signature:
C_{\underline{\gamma}\underline{\beta}}=-C_{\underline{\beta}\underline{\gamma}}=-(C_{\underline{\gamma}\underline{\beta}})^{*}
. We will also need the symmetric matrices with upper indices

\tilde{\Gamma}^{{\underline{\mu}}\;\underline{\alpha}\underline{\beta}}=\tilde{\Gamma}^{{\underline{\mu}}\;\underline{\beta}\underline{\alpha}}=C^{\underline{\alpha}\underline{\gamma}}{\Gamma}^{\underline{\mu}}{}_{\underline{\gamma}}{}^{\underline{\beta}}

(6.5)

which can be used to write the Clifford algebra (
6.4
) in the form

\Gamma^{(\underline{\mu}}{}_{\underline{\alpha}\underline{\delta}}\tilde{\Gamma}^{\underline{\nu})\underline{\delta}\underline{\beta}}=\eta^{\underline{\mu}\underline{\nu}}\delta_{\underline{\alpha}}^{\underline{\beta}}\penalty\ .

(6.6)

We use the following SO
(1,9)
invariant decomposition of 11D fermionic Majorana spinor coordinates on two 10D Majorana-Weyl (with opposite chiralities)

\Theta^{\underline{\alpha}}=\left(\begin{matrix}\theta^{1\alpha}\cr\theta^{2}_{\alpha}\end{matrix}\right)\;.\qquad

(6.7)

Then, with the appropriate
\text{SO}(1,9)
invariant representation of the 11D gamma matrices (presented in Appendix
F
), the 11D
\mathcal{N}=1
VA 1-form decomposes, as indicated already in (
6.2
), yielding the 10D
\mathcal{N}=2
VA 1-form (
5.24
)

\Pi^{{\mu}}=\text{d}X^{{\mu}}-i\text{d}\theta^{1}\sigma^{{\mu}}\theta^{1}-i\text{d}\theta^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{{\mu}}\theta^{2}

(6.8)

and the scalar (with respect to SO
(1,9)
) 1-form

\Pi^{*}=\text{d}X^{*}+i\text{d}\theta^{1}\theta^{2}+i\text{d}\theta^{2}\theta^{1}\;.

(6.9)

The 11D spinor moving frame variables (or 11D Lorentz harmonics
[
169
]
; see

[
126
,
127
]
and refs. therein) are defined as rectangular blocks of Spin
(1,10)
valued matrix

\displaystyle V_{\underline{\alpha}}^{(\underline{\beta})}=\left(\begin{matrix}v_{\underline{\alpha}{q}}^{\;+},\penalty\ \penalty\ v_{\underline{\alpha}q}^{\;-}\end{matrix}\right)\in\text{Spin}(1,10)\;\qquad

(6.10)

which serves as a square root of the
\text{SO}(1,10)
valued moving frame matrix

\displaystyle U_{\underline{\mu}}^{(\underline{a})}=\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(U_{\underline{\mu}}^{\#}+U_{\underline{\mu}}^{=}\right),U_{\underline{\mu}}^{i}\,,\frac{1}{2}\left(U_{\underline{\mu}}^{\#}-U_{\underline{\mu}}^{=}\right)\right)\in\text{SO}(1,10)\;\qquad

(6.11)

constructed from two lightlike vectors
U^{=}
and
U^{\#}
normalized in their contraction, together with 9 orthonormal spacelike vectors
U^{i}
orthogonal to that two,

\displaystyle U_{\underline{\mu}}^{=}U^{{\underline{\mu}}=}=0\;,\qquad U_{\underline{\mu}}^{\#}U^{{\underline{\mu}}\#}=0\;,\qquad U_{\underline{\mu}}^{=}U^{\underline{\mu}\#}=2\;,\qquad

(6.12)

\displaystyle\penalty\ U_{\underline{\mu}}^{i}U^{{\underline{\mu}}=}=0\;,\qquad\penalty\ U_{\underline{\mu}}^{i}U^{{\underline{\mu}}\#}=0\;,\qquad\penalty\ \penalty\ U_{\underline{\mu}}^{i}U^{{\underline{\mu}}j}=-\delta^{ij}\;.\qquad

(6.13)

The moving frame vectors also obey
U_{\underline{\mu}}^{(\underline{c})}\eta_{(\underline{c})(\underline{d})}U_{\underline{\nu}}^{(\underline{d})}=\eta_{{\underline{\mu}}{\underline{\nu}}}
, which can be written in the form of

\displaystyle\delta_{\underline{\mu}}{}^{\underline{\nu}}={1\over 2}U_{\underline{\mu}}^{=}U^{{\underline{\nu}}\#}+{1\over 2}U_{\underline{\mu}}^{\#}U^{{\underline{\nu}}=}-U_{\underline{\mu}}^{i}U^{{\underline{\nu}}i}\;.\qquad

(6.14)

The square root relations discussed above encode the Lorentz invariance of the 11D Dirac matrices. They take the form,

\displaystyle V\Gamma_{\underline{\mu}}V^{T}=U_{\underline{\mu}}^{({\underline{a}})}{\Gamma}_{(\underline{a})}\,,\qquad V^{T}\tilde{\Gamma}^{({\underline{a}})}V=\tilde{\Gamma}^{{\underline{\mu}}}U_{\underline{\mu}}^{({\underline{a}})}\;\,.\qquad

(6.15)

The spinor moving frame variables also satisfy the constraint

VCV^{T}=C\;,\qquad

(6.16)
