which reflects the Lorentz invariance of the charge conjugation matrix. From this, it follows that the blocks of the inverse spinor moving frame matrix obeying

\displaystyle v^{-{\underline{\alpha}}}_{{q}}v_{{\underline{\alpha}}{p}}^{\;+}=\delta_{{q}{p}}\;,\qquad

\displaystyle v^{-{\underline{\alpha}}}_{{q}}v_{{\underline{\alpha}}q}^{\;-}=0\;,\qquad

(6.17)

\displaystyle v^{+{\underline{\alpha}}}_{{q}}v_{{\underline{\alpha}}{p}}^{\;+}=0\;,\qquad

\displaystyle v^{+{\underline{\alpha}}}_{{q}}v_{{\underline{\alpha}}{p}}^{\;-}=\delta_{qp}\;\qquad

are constructed from the spinor frame variables of (
6.10
) as

\displaystyle v_{q}^{+{\underline{\alpha}}}=iC^{{\underline{\alpha}}{\underline{\beta}}}v_{{\underline{\beta}}q}^{\;+}\,,\qquad v_{q}^{-{\underline{\alpha}}}=-iC^{{\underline{\alpha}}{\underline{\beta}}}v_{{\underline{\beta}}q}^{\;-}\,.\qquad

(6.18)

Using an appropriate (SO
(1,1)\otimes\text{SO}(9)
invariant) representation of the 11D gamma matrices (see Appendix
F
), Eqs. (
6.15
) split into

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ v^{-}_{{q}}\tilde{\Gamma}_{{\underline{\mu}}}v^{-}_{{p}}=U_{\underline{\mu}}^{=}\delta_{{q}{p}}\;,

\displaystyle\qquad U_{\underline{\mu}}^{=}\Gamma^{\underline{\mu}}_{\underline{\alpha}\underline{\beta}}=2v_{\underline{\alpha}q}{}^{-}v_{\underline{\beta}q}{}^{-}\;,\qquad

(6.19)

\displaystyle\;v_{{q}}^{+}\tilde{\Gamma}_{{\underline{\mu}}}v_{{p}}^{+}=U_{{\underline{\mu}}}^{\#}\delta_{{q}{p}}\;,

\displaystyle\qquad\penalty\ U_{{\underline{\mu}}}^{\#}{\Gamma}^{{\underline{\mu}}}_{{{\underline{\alpha}}}{{\underline{\beta}}}}=2v_{{{\underline{\alpha}}}{q}}{}^{+}v_{{{\underline{\beta}}}{q}}{}^{+}\;,\qquad

(6.20)

\displaystyle\penalty\ v_{{q}}^{-}\tilde{\Gamma}_{{\underline{\mu}}}v_{{p}}^{+}=U_{{\underline{\mu}}}^{i}\gamma^{i}_{q{p}}\;,

\displaystyle\qquad\penalty\ \penalty\ U_{{\underline{\mu}}}^{i}{\Gamma}^{{\underline{\mu}}}_{{\underline{\alpha}}{\underline{\beta}}}=2v_{({{\underline{\alpha}}}|{q}}{}^{-}\gamma^{i}_{qp}v_{|{{\underline{\beta}}}){p}}{}^{+}\;,\quad

(6.21)

where
\gamma^{i}_{qp}=\gamma^{i}_{p{q}}
are the SO
(9)
Dirac matrices obeying

\gamma^{i}_{qr}\gamma^{j}_{rp}+\gamma^{j}_{qr}\gamma^{i}_{rp}=2\delta^{ij}\delta_{qp}\;.

(6.22)

The derivatives of the moving frame and spinor moving frame variables can be expressed in terms of Cartan forms

\displaystyle\underline{\Omega}^{ij}:=U^{\underline{\mu}i}\text{d}U_{\underline{\mu}}^{j}\,,\qquad

(6.23)

\displaystyle\underline{\Omega}^{(0)}:=U^{\underline{\mu}=}\text{d}U_{\underline{\mu}}^{\#}\,,\qquad

(6.24)

\displaystyle\underline{\Omega}^{\#j}:=U^{\underline{\mu}\#}\text{d}U_{\underline{\mu}}^{j}\,,\qquad\underline{\Omega}^{=j}:=U^{\underline{\mu}=}\text{d}U_{\underline{\mu}}^{j}\,\qquad

(6.25)

(see e.g.
[
170
,
171
]
and refs. therein for more details). The form
\underline{\Omega}^{ij}
transforms as a connection under the SO
(9)
symmetry which acts on both the 9-vector indices
i,j
and the 16-component spinor indices
q,p
of the moving frame variables and the matrix fields. This is the 11D counterpart of the Cartan form
\Omega^{ij}
from the 10D SO
(1,9)/
SO
(9)
spinor moving frame (Lorentz harmonics) formalism.

The Cartan form
\underline{\Omega}^{(0)}
transforms as a connection under the SO
(1,1)
group, under which the moving frame, spinor moving frame variables, and the Lagrange multiplier
\rho^{\#}
scale according to the weights indicated by their sign indices. In particular, since
\#=++
we have

\displaystyle U^{\#}_{\underline{\mu}}\mapsto e^{2\beta}U^{\#}_{\underline{\mu}}\;,\qquad U^{=}_{\underline{\mu}}\mapsto e^{-2\beta}U^{=}_{\underline{\mu}}\;,\qquad U^{i}_{\underline{\mu}}\mapsto U^{i}_{\underline{\mu}}\;,\qquad

\displaystyle\underline{\Omega}^{(0)}\mapsto\underline{\Omega}^{(0)}+d\beta\;,\qquad\underline{\Omega}^{ij}\mapsto\underline{\Omega}^{ij}\;,\qquad

\displaystyle\rho^{\#}\mapsto e^{2\beta}\rho^{\#}\;\qquad

(6.26)

under SO
(1,1)
group. The remaining Cartan forms,
\underline{\Omega}^{\#j}
and
\underline{\Omega}^{=j}
, transform covariantly under both SO
(9)
and SO
(1,1)
which are gauge symmetries of the mM
0
action.

6.1.2

Matrix fields describing the relative motion of mM0 constituents

The matrix fields describing the relative motion of the mM0 constituents are exactly the same as those appearing in the mD
0
system. These include bosonic traceless
N\times N
matrices
{\mathbb{X}}^{i}
and
{\mathbb{P}}^{i}
, carrying the SO(9) vector indices, fermionic traceless
N\times N
matrices
{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}
, carrying SO(9) spinor index
q=1,\ldots,16
; and a bosonic traceless
N\times N
matrix 1-form
{\mathbb{A}}=\text{d}\tau{\mathbb{A}}_{\tau}
corresponding to the SU(
N
) gauge connection on the worldline.

6.1.3

The mM0 action

The action for the 11D mM0 system, originally proposed in
[
126
]
, can be written in the form

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}S_{\text{mM}0}&=\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\rho^{\#}\,\underline{\text{E}}^{=}+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\left(\text{tr}\left({\mathbb{P}}^{i}\text{D}{\mathbb{X}}^{i}+4i\mathbf{\Psi}_{q}\text{D}{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}\right)+\underline{\text{E}}^{\#}\,\frac{1}{\rho^{\#}}\,{\cal H}\right)+\\
&+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\underline{\text{E}}{}^{+q}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{\rho^{\#}}}\,\text{tr}\left(-4i(\gamma^{i}{\bm{\Psi}})_{q}{\mathbb{P}}^{i}+{1\over 2}(\gamma^{ij}{\bm{\Psi}})_{q}[{\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}]\right)-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\frac{\text{D}\rho^{\#}}{\rho^{\#}}\text{tr}({\mathbb{P}}^{i}{\mathbb{X}}^{i})\penalty\ ,\end{split}\end{array}

(6.27)

in which the matrix fields are redefined (with respect to those in
[
126
]
) to be inert under the SO
(1,1)
symmetry acting on the spinor moving frame variables and Lagrange multiplier
\rho^{\#}
, as shown in (
6.26
).

The covariant derivatives of the matrix fields appearing in (
6.27
) are given by

\text{D}{\mathbb{X}}^{i}:=\text{d}{\mathbb{X}}^{i}-\underline{\Omega}^{ij}{\mathbb{X}}^{j}+[{\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{X}}^{i}]\;,

(6.28)

\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{D}{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}:=\text{d}{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}-{1\over 4}\underline{\Omega}^{ij}\gamma^{ij}_{qp}{\bm{\Psi}}_{p}+[{\mathbb{A}},{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}]\;

(6.29)

and include, besides the SU(
N
) connection 1-form
{\mathbb{A}}
, also the composite SO
(9)
connection
\underline{\Omega}^{ij}
(
6.23
). In addition, the derivative of the Lagrange multiplier
\rho^{\#}

\text{D}\rho^{\#}=\text{d}\rho^{\#}-2\underline{\Omega}^{(0)}\rho^{\#}\;

(6.30)

includes the SO
(1,1)
connection (
6.24
). Let us recall that these composite connections are Cartan forms arising from the nonvanishing components of the derivatives of the moving frame and spinor moving frame variables.

The moving frame and spinor moving frame variables also enter the action explicitly through the projections

\underline{\text{E}}^{\#}=\Pi^{\underline{\mu}}U_{\underline{\mu}}^{\#}\;,\qquad\underline{\text{E}}^{=}=\Pi^{\underline{\mu}}U_{\underline{\mu}}^{=}\;,\qquad

(6.31)

where
\Pi^{\underline{\mu}}
is the pull-back of the 11D bosonic supervielbein 1-form (see Eq. (
6.2
)), and

\underline{\text{E}}^{+q}=\text{d}\Theta^{\underline{\alpha}}\,v^{\;+}_{\underline{\alpha}q}\,

(6.32)

is the pull-back of the fermionic supervielbein form.

The “relative motion Hamiltonian” in (
6.27
)

{\cal H}={1\over 2}\text{tr}\left({\mathbb{P}}^{i}{\mathbb{P}}^{i}\right)-{1\over 64}\text{tr}\left[{\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}\right]^{2}-2\,\text{tr}\left({\mathbb{X}}^{i}\,{\bm{\Psi}}\gamma^{i}{\bm{\Psi}}\right)\qquad

(6.33)

coincides with that of the mD
0
system (
cf
. Eq. (
5.28
)).

The action (
6.27
) is manifestly invariant under the 11D target space supersymmetry and, as shown in
[
126
]
, possesses an additional 16 parametric local worldline supersymmetry, generalizing the
\kappa
-symmetry of single M
0
-brane action formulated in the spinor moving frame approach
[
162
,
172
]
.

The properties of this mM
0
system described by the action (
6.27
) were further studies in
[
127
]
. The problem of its dimensional reduction to
\text{D}=10
was addressed in
[
130
]
, but not resolved there. The reason was that a suitable convenient choice of the basic matrix fields was not found in
[
130
]
. This issue was recently overcome, first for the simpler case of
\text{D}=4
counterpart of mM
0
system described in chapter
3
. Using this insight in
[
5
]
we have successfully rewritten the mM
0
action from
[
126
]
in terms of more convenient matrix variables, as presented in Eq. (
6.27
). The dimensional reduction of this reformulated mM
0
action to 10D is the subject of the next subsection.

6.2

Dimensional reduction of mM0 action to D=10

As we have already noticed, with the suitable representation for 11D gamma matrices and charge conjugation matrix (see Appendix
F
, in particular Eqs. (
F.1
), (
F.2
), and (
F.3
)), the 11D VA 1-form can be decomposed as in (
6.2
) into the 10D VA 1-form (
6.8
) and SO
(1,9)
invariant 1-form (
6.9
).

6.2.1

SO(1,9) invariant form oh the 11D spinor moving frame and basic 1-forms

The next step is to solve the constraints defining the above described 11D spinor moving frame variables in terms of the 10D Spin
(1,9)/\text{Spin}(9)
spinor ones (
5.8
).
The convenient form of the solution is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\rho^{\#}}}v_{{\underline{\alpha}}q}^{\;+}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\left(\begin{matrix}v_{{\alpha}}^{\;q}\cr-v_{q}{}^{\alpha}\end{matrix}\right)\;,\qquad\sqrt{\rho^{\#}}\,v_{{\underline{\alpha}}q}^{\;-}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,\sqrt{{\cal M}}\,\left(\begin{matrix}v_{{\alpha}}^{\;q}\cr v_{q}{}^{\alpha}\end{matrix}\right)\;\qquad

(6.34)

which implies the complementary relations

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\rho^{\#}}}v^{+\underline{\alpha}}_{q}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\left(\begin{matrix}v_{q}{}^{\alpha}\cr v_{{\alpha}}^{\;q}\end{matrix}\right)\;,\qquad\sqrt{\rho^{\#}}\,v^{-\underline{\alpha}}_{q}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,\sqrt{{\cal M}}\,\left(\begin{matrix}v_{q}{}^{\alpha}\cr-v_{{\alpha}}^{\;q}\end{matrix}\right)\;.\qquad

(6.35)

These expressions involve both the Lagrange multiplier
\rho^{\#}
from the mM
0
action and an arbitrary function
{\cal M}(\tau)
of the proper time
\tau
parametrizing the center of energy worldline of the mM
0
system.

Importantly, this is not merely an ansatz, but the general solution of the constraints (
6.19
)-(
6.21
) and (
6.16
). This can be confirmed by counting the number of degrees of freedom, modulo natural gauge symmetries, on both sides of the relation
(
6.34
) which gives
1+9=1+9
. Indeed, both sides include one scalar (
\rho^{\#}
or
{\cal M}
) and spinor frame variables parametrizing cosets isomorphic to
{\mathbb{S}}^{9}
sphere:

\dfrac{\text{SO}(1,10)}{(\text{SO}(1,1)\otimes\text{SO}(9))\subset\!\!\!\!\times K_{9}}\simeq{\mathbb{S}}^{9}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{\text{SO}(1,9)}{\text{SO}(9)}\simeq{\mathbb{S}}^{9}

(6.36)

respectively (see e.g.
[
170
,
171
]
and refs. therein for more details). Notice also that the l.h.s. of (
6.34
) preserves the characteristic
\text{SO}(1,1)
gauge symmetry (
6.26
) of the 11D spinor moving frame formalism (which was taken into account in the above counting of the degrees of freedom). From the 11D perspective, the solution (
6.34
) explicitly breaks the full Lorentz symmetry
\text{SO}(1,10)
down to its
\text{SO}(1,9)
subgroup, which becomes Lorentz symmetry of the reduced 10D theory.

Substituting (
6.34
) into (the pull-backs of) 11D fermionic 1-form entering the mM0 action (
6.27
), we obtain

\underline{\text{E}}{}^{+q}={\sqrt{\rho^{\#}}}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2{\cal M}}}(\text{E}^{1q}-\text{E}_{q}^{2})\;,

(6.37)

where

\text{E}^{1q}=\text{d}\theta^{1\alpha}v_{\alpha}^{q}\;,\qquad\text{E}^{2}_{q}=\text{d}\theta^{2}_{\alpha}v_{q}^{\alpha}\;\qquad

(6.38)

can be naturally identified with the pull-backs of the 10D type IIA supervielbein forms (
5.26
).

Now, substituting (
6.34
) into Eqs. (
6.19
) and (
6.20
)
and using the suitable representation for 11D gamma matrices (see Appendix
F
) as well as (
5.12
) we find that the 11D moving frame vectors are related to its 10D counterparts by

U^{=}_{\mu}=\frac{{\cal M}}{\rho^{\#}}u^{0}_{\mu}\;,\qquad U^{=}_{*}=-\frac{{\cal M}}{\rho^{\#}}\;,

(6.39)

U^{\#}_{\mu}=\frac{\rho^{\#}}{{\cal M}}\,u^{0}_{\mu}\;,\qquad U^{\#}_{*}=\frac{\rho^{\#}}{{\cal M}}\;

(6.40)

so that

\rho^{\#}\underline{\text{E}}^{=}={\cal M}({\rm E}^{0}-\Pi^{*})\;,\qquad\frac{\underline{\text{E}}^{\#}}{\rho^{\#}}=\frac{1}{{\cal M}}({\rm E}^{0}+\Pi^{*})\;.\qquad

(6.41)

Moreover, from (
6.21
) we find

U_{\underline{\mu}}^{i}=\delta_{\underline{\mu}}{}^{\nu}u_{\nu}^{i}\;

(6.42)

which can be used to identify the composite
\text{SO}(9)
connection of the mM
0
system with its 10D counterpart (
5.17
),

\underline{\Omega}^{ij}:=U^{\underline{\mu}i}\text{d}U_{\underline{\mu}}^{j}=u^{{\mu}i}\text{d}u_{{\mu}}^{j}=:{\Omega}^{ij}\;.

(6.43)

Thus the covariant derivative of the matrix fields in the mM
0
action (Eqs. (
6.28
) and (
6.29
)) coincide with (
5.61
) and (
5.63
) used in the mD
0
action (
5.27
).

As far as
\text{SO}(1,1)
connection is concerned, we find from (
6.39
) and (
6.40
)

\underline{\Omega}{}^{(0)}=\frac{1}{4}U^{=\underline{\mu}}\text{d}U_{\underline{\mu}}^{\#}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\text{d}\rho^{\#}}{\rho^{\#}}-\frac{\text{d}{\cal M}}{{\cal M}}\right)\;,

(6.44)

which gives

\text{D}\rho^{\#}=\text{d}\rho^{\#}-2\rho^{\#}\underline{\Omega}^{(0)}=\rho^{\#}\frac{\text{d}{\cal M}}{{\cal M}}\;.

(6.45)

Thus, after using in (
6.27
) the relations (
6.34
) and their consequences,
\rho^{\#}
disappears from the 11D action and it is fully replaced by
{\cal M}
.

6.2.2

Dimensional reduction of the mM0 action

With the solution (
6.34
) and the splitting (
6.2
), the mM0 action (
6.27
) acquires the form

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}S_{\text{mM}0}|_{\eqref{hV11=hv10}}&=\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}{\cal M}(\text{E}^{0}-\Pi^{*})+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\frac{1}{\cal M}(\text{E}^{0}+\Pi^{*})\,{\cal H}+\\
&+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\text{tr}\left({\mathbb{P}}^{i}\text{D}{\mathbb{X}}^{i}+4i{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}\text{D}{\bm{\Psi}}_{q}\right)-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\frac{\text{d}{\cal M}}{{\cal M}}\text{tr}({\mathbb{P}}^{i}{\mathbb{X}}^{i})+\\
&+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\int_{\mathcal{W}^{1}}\frac{1}{2\sqrt{{\cal M}}}(\text{E}{}^{1q}-\text{E}{}^{2}_{q})\,\text{tr}\left(-4i(\gamma^{i}{\bm{\Psi}})_{q}{\mathbb{P}}^{i}+{1\over 2}(\gamma^{ij}{\bm{\Psi}})_{q}[{\mathbb{X}}^{i},{\mathbb{X}}^{j}]\right)\penalty\ ,\end{split}\end{array}

(6.46)

where the covariant derivatives are given by (
5.61
) and (
5.63
), and the relative motion Hamiltonian
{\cal H}
has the form of (
5.28
). Here,

\text{E}^{0}=\Pi^{\mu}u_{\mu}^{0}\penalty\ .

(6.47)

with
\Pi^{\mu}
from (
6.8
) (see (
5.25
)),
\Pi^{*}
is defined in (
6.9
) and, according to (
6.38
),

\text{E}^{1q}-\text{E}^{2}_{q}=\text{d}\theta^{1\alpha}v_{\alpha}^{q}-\text{d}\theta^{2}_{\alpha}v_{q}^{\alpha}\;.\qquad

(6.48)

To complete the dimensional reduction, we derive the equation of motion, from the action (
6.46
), for the 11th bosonic coordinate field
X^{*}
,

\text{d}\left({\cal M}-\frac{1}{{\cal M}}\,\frac{\cal H}{\mu^{6}}\right)=0\;,

(6.49)

and substituting its solution back into the functional (
6.46
). This solution can be equivalently written as

{\cal M}-\frac{1}{{\cal M}}\,\frac{\cal H}{\mu^{6}}=m\;

(6.50)

for some integration constant
m
. Its solution (with nonvanishing limit when
{\cal H}\mapsto 0
) is

{\cal M}=\frac{m}{2}+\sqrt{\frac{m^{2}}{4}+\frac{\cal H}{\mu^{6}}}\;.

(6.51)

Substituting this result back into the action (
6.46
) we find the 10D mD
0
action (
5.27
) with a particular function
{\cal M}={\cal M}\left({{\cal H}}/{\mu^{6}}\right)
given by the above expression (
6.51
).

Thus, among the family of models described by Eq. (
5.27
), this specific case is singled out as having a clear origin in 11D M-theory.

7
Towards field theory of multiple D0-branes: quantization of simplest 3D mD0 prototype

They were close to the end of the beginning…

The Dark Tower I: The Gunslinger

by Stephen King.

In this thesis we have constructed and presented a completely supersymmetric nonlinear action that exhibits the properties expected of a multiple D
0
-brane system. Its quantization is anticipated to yield an interesting supersymmetric field theory formulated in the superspace extended by additional matrix coordinates, which can provide important insights into the study of String/M-Theory.

As a first step toward this goal, in this chapter we develop the Hamiltonian formulation and perform a covariant quantization of the simplest 3-dimensional counterpart (see chapter
4
) of the 10-dimensional multiple D
0
-brane model (see chapter
5
). Through this process, we derive a supersymmetric equations of the field theory of this simplest 3D counterpart of mD
0
system and discuss some of its solutions.

7.1

Simplest 3D counterpart of the 10D mD0 action

In this section we recall the fields used to describe the 3D mD
0
counterpart. However, as we will discuss in section
7.1.3
, we adopt a different representation for the 3D spinor moving frame variables compared to the one used in chapter
4
.

7.1.1

Physical and auxiliary fields of the 3D mD0 system

The dynamical variables of 3D mD
0
system, can be naturally decomposed into the center of mass variables and the matrix fields describing the relative motion of the mD
0
constituents. This latter set contains the fields of
\text{D}=3
SU(
N
) SYM model dimensionally reduced to
\text{d}=1
. These are traceless
N\times N
bosonic matrix fields

{\mathbb{Z}}=||{\mathbb{Z}}_{i}{}^{j}(\tau)||\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}=||\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{i}{}^{j}(\tau)||\penalty\ ,

(7.1)

which are related by Hermitian conjugation

{{\mathbb{Z}}}=(\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}})^{\dagger}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{i}{}^{j}=(\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{j}{}^{i})^{*}\;,\qquad{\rm tr}\,{{\mathbb{Z}}}={{\mathbb{Z}}}_{i}{}^{i}=0\;,\qquad i,j=1,\ldots,N\;,

(7.2)

and two Hermitian conjugate traceless
N\times N
fermionic matrix fields
43
43

43

Here we have replaced the notation for the matrix fermionic fields from
\Psi\mapsto{\bm{\Psi}}
compared to chapter
4
. This distinction allows us to differentiate between the fermionic matrix fields
{\bm{\Psi}}
and the fermionic coordinates
\Psi
that will appear in the quantization of the mD
0
system in section
7.4
.

{\bm{\Psi}}=||{{\bm{\Psi}}}_{i}{}^{j}(\tau)||\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}=||\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}_{i}{}^{j}(\tau)||\penalty\ ,

(7.3)

satisfying

{{\bm{\Psi}}}=\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}{}^{\dagger}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad{{\bm{\Psi}}}_{i}{}^{j}=(\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}_{j}{}^{i})^{*}\;,\qquad{\rm tr}\,{{\bm{\Psi}}}\equiv{{\bm{\Psi}}}_{i}{}^{i}=0\penalty\ ,

(7.4)

as well as anti-Hermitian 1-form

{\mathbb{A}}=\text{d}\tau{\mathbb{A}}_{\tau}=\text{d}\tau||{\mathbb{A}}_{\tau}{}_{i}{}^{j}(\tau)||=-{\mathbb{A}}^{\dagger}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad{\mathbb{A}}_{\tau}{}_{i}{}^{j}=-({\mathbb{A}}_{\tau}{}_{j}{}^{i})^{*}\;.

(7.5)

All these fields depend on the proper time
\tau
which parametrizes the center of mass worldline
{\cal W}^{1}
of the 3D mD
0
system. This is described by the same variables as that used to describe a single D
0
-brane in the spinor moving frame formulation. The center of mass degrees of freedom are described by 3-vector bosonic and two complex conjugate fermionic spinor coordinate functions

z^{M}(\tau)=(x^{\mu}(\tau),\theta^{\alpha}(\tau),\bar{\theta}{}^{\alpha}(\tau)),\quad\quad\bar{\theta}{}^{\alpha}(\tau)=(\theta^{\alpha}(\tau))^{*}\;,\quad\mu=0,1,2\;,\quad\alpha=1,2\;,

(7.6)

which parametrize the worldline
{\cal W}^{1}
as a trajectory in flat
\text{D}=3

{\cal N}=2
superspace
\Sigma^{(3|4)}
with coordinates

z^{M}=(x^{\mu},\theta^{\alpha},\bar{\theta}{}^{\alpha})
,

\displaystyle{\cal W}^{1}\;\subset\;\Sigma^{(3|4)}\;:\qquad z^{M}=z^{M}(\tau)\;.

(7.7)

To write the action we also need to introduce some auxiliary fields, in particular the spinor moving frame variables, which will be described below. However, as a preliminary step, it is useful to revisit the de Azcárraga-Lukierski action, formulated purely in terms of the coordinate functions introduced above.

7.1.2

3D version of the de Azcárraga-Lukierski action for a single D0-brane

When single D
0
-brane is considered, its action can be written only in terms of the above described coordinate functions. We present it here as a reference point.

The standard action for single D
0
-brane
[
94
]
is given by 10D counterpart of the
\text{D}=4
action originally constructed by de Azcárraga and Lukierski
[
149
,
165
]
. Here we discuss its 3D counterpart given by

S_{\text{AL}}=-m\int d\tau\left(\sqrt{E_{\tau}^{a}E_{\tau}^{b}\eta_{ab}}+\dot{\theta}^{\alpha}\bar{\theta}_{\alpha}-\theta^{\alpha}\dot{\bar{\theta}}_{\alpha}\right)\,

(7.8)

where the dot denotes a derivative with respect to proper time
\tau
, and

E_{\tau}^{a}=\dot{x}{}^{a}-i\dot{\theta}\gamma^{a}\bar{\theta}+i{\theta}\gamma^{a}\dot{\bar{\theta}}\;,\qquad

(7.9)

is the pull-back (coefficient of
\text{d}\tau
) of the VA 1-form,

E^{a}=\text{d}{x}{}^{a}-i\text{d}{\theta}\gamma^{a}\bar{\theta}+i{\theta}\gamma^{a}\text{d}{\bar{\theta}}=:E^{a}(z)\;,\qquad E^{a}(z(\tau))=\text{d}\tau E_{\tau}^{a}\;,\qquad a=0,1,2\;.\qquad

(7.10)

(See sections
4.1
and
4.2.1
for the notation).

As key property of the action (
7.8
) is its invariance under local fermionic
\kappa
-symmetry

\displaystyle\delta_{\kappa}x^{a}=i\delta_{\kappa}{\theta}\gamma^{a}\bar{\theta}-i{\theta}\gamma^{a}\delta_{\kappa}{\bar{\theta}}\;,\qquad

(7.11)

\displaystyle\delta_{\kappa}{\theta}{}^{\alpha}=\kappa_{\beta}\left(\epsilon-i\tilde{\gamma}_{a}E_{\tau}^{a}/\sqrt{E_{\tau}^{b}E_{\tau b}}\right)^{\beta\alpha}\;,\qquad\delta_{\kappa}\bar{\theta}{}^{\alpha}=\bar{\kappa}_{\beta}\left(\epsilon+i\tilde{\gamma}_{a}E_{\tau}^{a}/\sqrt{E_{\tau}^{b}E_{\tau b}}\right)^{\beta\alpha}\;,

which, as we have emphasized on several occasions throughout this thesis, implies that the ground state of the system preserves
1/2
of the spacetime supersymmetry
[
101
]
, making it a BPS state, the 3D counterpart of the D
0
-brane BPS state in String Theory.

Thus, moving frame and spinor moving frame variables are not obligatory for describing a single D
0
-brane: in this case, the spinor moving frame formulation (as presented in chapter
4
) is classically equivalent to the formulation provided by the de Azcárraga-Lukierski action described above. However, the action for the 3D mD
0
system is known only in its form involving spinor moving frame variables which we are going to describe now.

7.1.3

3D complex spinor moving frame variables (Lorentz harmonics)

In chapter
4
we have used the real spinor moving frame variables
(v_{\alpha}^{1},v_{\alpha}^{2})
subject to the constraint
v^{\alpha 2}v_{\alpha}^{1}=1
. However, for the purpose of this chapter, we found it much more convenient to describe the spinor frame variables using complex spinors

w_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(v_{\alpha}^{1}-iv_{\alpha}^{2})\qquad\text{ and its c.c.}\qquad\bar{w}_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(v_{\alpha}^{1}+iv_{\alpha}^{2})

(7.12)

which obey the relation

\bar{w}{}^{\alpha}w_{\alpha}=i\,.

(7.13)

This allows us to write the unity decomposition and to decompose the unit antisymmetric tensors as

\delta_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}=\,i\bar{w}{}_{\alpha}w^{\beta}-iw_{\alpha}\bar{w}{}^{\beta}\;,\qquad\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}=-i\bar{w}{}_{\alpha}w_{\beta}+iw_{\alpha}\bar{w}{}_{\beta}\;.

(7.14)

Taking these constraints into account, we find that the derivatives of these complex spinors (providing a 3D version of the 4D Newman-Penrose diad
[
155
]
) are expressed by

\text{D}w_{\alpha}:=\text{d}w_{\alpha}+iaw_{\alpha}=if\bar{w}_{\alpha}\;,\qquad\text{D}\bar{w}_{\alpha}:=\text{d}\bar{w}_{\alpha}-ia\bar{w}_{\alpha}=-i\bar{f}{w}_{\alpha}\;,\qquad

(7.15)

in terms of Cartan forms
44
44

44

These relate to the real Cartan forms
f^{pq}=v^{\alpha p}\text{d}v_{\alpha}^{q}
used in chapter
4
by

f=\frac{1}{2}(f^{11}-f^{22})-if^{12}\;,\qquad\bar{f}=\frac{1}{2}(f^{11}-f^{22})+if^{12}\;,\qquad a=\frac{1}{2}(f^{11}+f^{22})=\frac{1}{2}f^{qq}\;.

(7.16)

f=w^{\alpha}\text{d}w_{\alpha}\;,\qquad\bar{f}=\bar{w}^{\alpha}\text{d}\bar{w}_{\alpha}\;,\qquad a=w^{\alpha}\text{d}\bar{w}_{\alpha}=\bar{w}^{\alpha}\text{d}w_{\alpha}\;.\qquad

(7.17)

The first two of these provide a covariant basis of the cotangent space to the coset SU
(1,1)/\text{U}(1)=\text{SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})/\text{SO}(2)
, while the third,
a
, transforms as a U
(1)
connection. This allowed to introduce the U
(1)
covariant derivatives
D
in (
7.15
). It is straightforward to check that these Cartan forms satisfy the Maurer-Cartan structure equations

\text{D}f=\text{d}f+2if\wedge a=0\;,\qquad\text{D}\bar{f}=\text{d}\bar{f}-2i\bar{f}\wedge a=0\;,\qquad\text{d}a=-if\wedge\bar{f}\;.\

(7.18)

The moving frame vectors (a 3D version of the complex 4D lightlike Newman-Penrose tetrad
[
155
]
) are constructed as bilinears of the complex spinor
w
and
\bar{w}

u_{a}^{(0)}=w\gamma_{a}\bar{w}\;,\qquad u_{a}=w\gamma_{a}w\;,\qquad\bar{u}_{a}=\bar{w}\gamma_{a}\bar{w}\;.\qquad

(7.19)

Due to the properties of
\text{D}=3
gamma matrices, these vectors obey

\begin{array}[]{lclcl}u_{a}^{(0)}u^{a(0)}=1\;,&u_{a}^{(0)}u^{a}=0\;,&u_{a}^{(0)}\bar{u}{}^{a}=0\penalty\ ,\\
\end{array}

(7.20)

\begin{array}[]{lclcl}u_{a}u^{a}=0\;,&\bar{u}_{a}\bar{u}{}^{a}=0\penalty\ ,&u_{a}\bar{u}{}^{a}=-2\penalty\ .\\
\end{array}

(7.21)

Furthermore, they satisfy

u_{a}^{(0)}\gamma^{a}_{\alpha\beta}=2w_{(\alpha}\bar{w}_{\beta)}\;,\qquad u_{a}\gamma^{a}_{\alpha\beta}=2w_{\alpha}{w}_{\beta}\;,\qquad\bar{u}_{a}\gamma^{a}_{\alpha\beta}=2\bar{w}_{\alpha}\bar{w}_{\beta}\;.\qquad

(7.22)

Eqs. (
7.20
) and (
7.21
) imply that the real
3\times 3
matrix

u_{a}^{(b)}=\left(u_{a}^{(0)},\frac{1}{2}(u_{a}+\bar{u}_{a}),\frac{1}{2i}(u_{a}-\bar{u}_{a})\right)\;\in\;\text{O}(1,2)\;\

(7.23)

belongs to O
(1,2)
group. Moreover, their construction from spinor bilinears further implies
45
45

45

To see this we can take the constant “vacuum” value of the spinors
v_{\alpha}^{q}=\delta_{\alpha}^{q}
in (
7.12
) and find that the vectors are
u_{a}^{(0)}=\delta_{a}^{0}
,
u_{a}=-\delta_{a}^{0}+i\delta_{a}^{1}
,
\bar{u}_{a}=-\delta_{a}^{0}-i\delta_{a}^{1}
and obey (
7.24
) with our representation for 3D gamma matrices (see section
4.1
).

\epsilon^{abc}u_{a}^{(0)}u_{b}\bar{u}_{c}=-2i\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad u_{[b}\bar{u}_{c]}=-i\epsilon_{abc}u^{a(0)}\;,\qquad

(7.24)

which ensures this matrix to be SO
(1,2)
valued (not just O
(1,2)
valued as guaranteed by (
7.20
), (
7.21
)),

u_{a}^{(b)}=\left(u_{a}^{(0)},\frac{1}{2}(u_{a}+\bar{u}_{a}),\frac{1}{2i}(u_{a}-\bar{u}_{a})\right)\;\in\;\text{SO}(1,2)\;,\qquad

(7.25)

meaning that this set of vectors defines an oriented Lorentz frame. Thus (
7.22
), or equivalently (
7.19
), establish how the moving frame is constructed from the complex spinor moving frame.

The derivatives of the moving frame vectors are expressed through the Cartan forms (
7.22
)

\text{d}u^{(0)}_{a}=if\bar{u}_{a}-i\bar{f}u_{a}\;,\qquad\text{D}u_{a}=\text{d}{u}_{a}+2ia{u}_{a}=2ifu^{(0)}_{a}\;,\qquad\text{D}\bar{u}_{a}=\text{d}\bar{u}_{a}-2ia\bar{u}_{a}=-2i\bar{f}u^{(0)}_{a}\;.\qquad

(7.26)

Consequently, Cartan forms (
7.17
) can be equivalently written as

f=\frac{i}{2}{u}{}^{a}\text{d}u^{(0)}_{a}\;,\qquad\bar{f}=-\frac{i}{2}\bar{u}{}^{a}\text{d}u^{(0)}_{a}\;,\qquad a=-\frac{i}{4}\bar{u}{}^{a}\text{d}u_{a}=\frac{i}{4}u^{a}\text{d}\bar{u}_{a}\;.\qquad

(7.27)

When differentiating a function
\mathfrak{h}(\bar{w},w)
of the spinor frame variables only, the constraint (
7.13
) implies that its exterior derivative decomposes on the Cartan forms (
7.17
)

\text{d}=\text{d}\bar{w}_{\alpha}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{w}_{\alpha}}+\text{d}{w}_{\alpha}\frac{\partial}{\partial w_{\alpha}}=if\bar{{\mathbb{D}}}-i\bar{f}{\mathbb{D}}+ia{\mathbb{D}}^{(0)}\;,

(7.28)

where we have used Eqs. (
7.15
) at the second stage and the covariant derivatives are defined as

\displaystyle{\mathbb{D}}=w_{\alpha}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{w}_{\alpha}}\;,\qquad\bar{{\mathbb{D}}}=\bar{w}_{\alpha}\frac{\partial}{\partial{w}_{\alpha}}\;,\qquad{\mathbb{D}}^{(0)}=\bar{w}_{\alpha}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{w}_{\alpha}}-{w}_{\alpha}\frac{\partial}{\partial{w}_{\alpha}}\;.

(7.29)

These covariant derivatives have the characteristic property to “annihilate” the constraint (
7.13
)

{\mathbb{D}}(\bar{w}{}^{\alpha}w_{\alpha})=0\;,\qquad\bar{{\mathbb{D}}}(\bar{w}{}^{\alpha}w_{\alpha})=0\;,\qquad{\mathbb{D}}^{(0)}(\bar{w}{}^{\alpha}w_{\alpha})=0\;\qquad

(7.30)

and obey the
\mathfrak{su}(1,1)\simeq\mathfrak{su}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
algebra

{}[{\mathbb{D}}\,,\,\bar{{\mathbb{D}}}]={\mathbb{D}}^{(0)}\;,\qquad[{\mathbb{D}}^{(0)}\,,\,{\mathbb{D}}]=-2{\mathbb{D}}\;,\qquad[{\mathbb{D}}\,,\,\bar{{\mathbb{D}}}]=2\bar{{\mathbb{D}}}\;.\qquad

(7.31)

Finally, using the spinor and moving frame vectors, we can split the pull-back of the
\text{D}=3

{\cal N}=2
superspace supervielbein forms

E^{a}=\delta^{a}_{\mu}\text{d}{x}{}^{\mu}-i\text{d}{\theta}\gamma^{a}\bar{\theta}+i{\theta}\gamma^{a}\text{d}{\bar{\theta}}\;,\qquad E^{\alpha}=\text{d}\theta^{\alpha}\;,\qquad\bar{E}{}^{\alpha}=\text{d}\bar{\theta}{}^{\alpha}\qquad

(7.32)

into a set of three bosonic and four fermionic Lorentz invariant 1-forms

{\rm E}^{(0)}=E^{a}u_{a}^{(0)}\;,\qquad\qquad{\rm E}=E^{a}u_{a}\;,\qquad\bar{{\rm E}}=E{}^{a}\bar{u}_{a}\;,

(7.33)

\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{E}^{w}=\text{d}\theta^{\alpha}w_{\alpha}\;,\qquad\text{E}^{\bar{w}}=\text{d}\theta^{\alpha}{\bar{w}}_{\alpha}\;,

(7.34)

\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \bar{\text{E}}{}^{w}=\text{d}\bar{\theta}{}^{\alpha}w_{\alpha}\;,\qquad\bar{\text{E}}{}^{\bar{w}}=\text{d}\bar{\theta}{}^{\alpha}{\bar{w}}_{\alpha}\;.

(7.35)

7.1.4

Lagrangian of the simplest 3D counterpart of the mD0 system

The Lagrangian for the 3D counterpart of the 10D mD
0
action contains two constants with dimension of mass,,
m
and
\mu
, as well as an arbitrary positive definite function

{\cal M}={\cal M}({\cal H}/\mu^{6})\quad

(7.36)

defined in terms of the bosonic composite constructed from matrix fields

\displaystyle{\cal H}={\rm tr}\left({\mathbb{P}}\bar{\mathbb{P}}+[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{\mathbb{Z}}]^{2}-{i\over 2}{\mathbb{Z}}{\bm{\Psi}}{\bm{\Psi}}+{i\over 2}\bar{\mathbb{Z}}\bar{\bm{\Psi}}\bar{\bm{\Psi}}\right)\;.\qquad

(7.37)

This expression can be recognized as the Hamiltonian of 3D SU(
N
) SYM model reduced to
\text{d}=1
. For convenience, let us also introduce a pair of conjugate fermionic currents

{\nu}:=\text{tr}({\bm{\Psi}}\mathbb{P}+\bar{\bm{\Psi}}[\mathbb{Z},\bar{\mathbb{Z}}])\penalty\ ,\qquad{\bar{\nu}}:=\text{tr}(\bar{\bm{\Psi}}\bar{\mathbb{P}}+{\bm{\Psi}}[\mathbb{Z},\bar{\mathbb{Z}}])\penalty\ ,\qquad

(7.38)

which can be identified as the SYM supercurrents. The Lagrangian corresponding to the action (
4.72
) can then be written as

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}&\mathcal{L}_{\tau}[m,\mu,\mathcal{M}({\cal H})]=m{\rm E}_{\tau}^{0}+m(\dot{\theta}^{\alpha}\bar{\theta}_{\alpha}-\theta^{\alpha}\dot{\bar{\theta}}_{\alpha})+{1\over\mu^{6}}{\rm E}_{\tau}^{0}\frac{2}{{\cal M}}{{\cal H}}+{1\over\mu^{6}}\frac{\dot{{\cal M}}}{{\cal M}}{\rm tr}\left(\bar{\mathbb{P}}{\mathbb{Z}}+{\mathbb{P}}\bar{\mathbb{Z}}\right)+\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \\
&+{1\over\mu^{6}}{\rm tr}\left(\bar{\mathbb{P}}{\rm D}_{\tau}{\mathbb{Z}}+{\mathbb{P}}{\rm D}_{\tau}\bar{\mathbb{Z}}-{i\over 8}{\rm D}_{\tau}{{\bm{\Psi}}}\,\bar{\bm{\Psi}}+{i\over 8}{\bm{\Psi}}{\rm D}_{\tau}\bar{\bm{\Psi}}\right)+{1\over\mu^{6}}\frac{i}{\sqrt{{\cal M}}}\left(\dot{\theta}{}^{\alpha}\,w_{\alpha}\bar{\nu}+\dot{\bar{\theta}}{}^{\alpha}\bar{w}_{\alpha}\,\nu\right)\end{split}\end{array}

(7.39)

Here
{\rm D}_{\tau}
denotes covariant derivatives which include not only the
\mathfrak{su}(N)
valued connection
{\mathbb{A}}=\text{d}\tau{\mathbb{A}}_{\tau}
, but also a composite U
(1)
defined by the Cartan form
a=\text{d}\tau a_{\tau}
(
7.17
) (see also (
7.27
))

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\text{D}_{\tau}{\mathbb{Z}}=\dot{{\mathbb{Z}}}+2ia_{\tau}{\mathbb{Z}}+[{\mathbb{A}}_{\tau},{\mathbb{Z}}]\;,&&\text{D}_{\tau}{\bm{\Psi}}=\dot{{\bm{\Psi}}}-ia_{\tau}{\bm{\Psi}}+[{\mathbb{A}}_{\tau},{\bm{\Psi}}]\;,\\
\text{D}_{\tau}\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}=\dot{\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}}-2ia_{\tau}\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}+[{\mathbb{A}}_{\tau},\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}]\;,&&\text{D}_{\tau}\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}=\dot{\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}}+ia_{\tau}\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}+[{\mathbb{A}}_{\tau},\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}]\;.\end{array}

(7.40)

Two special members within this family of Lagrangians are defined by function (
7.36
) being constant

{\cal M}=m={\text{const}}\;,

(7.41)

and by specific function

{\cal M}=\frac{m}{2}+\sqrt{\frac{m^{2}}{4}+\frac{\cal H}{\mu^{6}}}\;.

(7.42)

The latter was derived in section
4.3.3
by dimensional reduction of the 4D counterpart of the mM
0
action
[
126
]
, while the former is the simplest representative of the family which can be recognized as 3D counterpart of the 10D action discussed previously in
[
130
]
.

In this chapter, we focus on the simplest model with constant
{\cal M}=m
, with the Lagrangian

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}\mathcal{L}_{\tau}[m,\mu]&=m{\rm E}_{\tau}^{0}+m(\dot{\theta}^{\alpha}\bar{\theta}_{\alpha}-\theta^{\alpha}\dot{\bar{\theta}}_{\alpha})+{1\over\mu^{6}}{\rm E}_{\tau}^{0}\frac{2}{m}{{\cal H}}+{1\over\mu^{6}}\frac{i}{\sqrt{m}}\left(\dot{\theta}{}^{\alpha}\,w_{\alpha}\bar{\nu}+\dot{\bar{\theta}}{}^{\alpha}\bar{w}_{\alpha}\,\nu\right)+\\
&+{1\over\mu^{6}}{\rm tr}\left(\bar{\mathbb{P}}{\rm D}_{\tau}{\mathbb{Z}}+{\mathbb{P}}{\rm D}_{\tau}\bar{\mathbb{Z}}-{i\over 8}{\rm D}_{\tau}{{\bm{\Psi}}}\,\bar{\bm{\Psi}}+{i\over 8}{\bm{\Psi}}{\rm D}_{\tau}\bar{\bm{\Psi}}\right)\penalty\ .\end{split}\end{array}

(7.43)

We will develop Hamiltonian formalism for this model and perform its quantization, thereby deriving the equation of the field theory of simplest 3D counterpart of the mD
0
-brane.

7.2

Hamiltonian formalism of the simplest 3D prototype of mD0 brane model

In this section we develop Hamiltonian formalism for our simplest 3-dimensional counterpart of the mD
0
system.

7.2.1

Canonical momenta and Poisson brackets

In general, the canonical momenta
p_{A}
are defined as derivatives of the Lagrangian
L(q^{A},\dot{q}{}^{A})
with respect to generalized velocities
\dot{q}^{A}=\frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}\tau}q^{A}
associated with the coordinates
q^{A}
,

p_{A}=\dfrac{\partial{L}}{\partial\dot{q}^{A}}\penalty\ .

(7.44)

They are canonically conjugate to these coordinates which implies the following structure of the Poisson brackets algebra

[p_{A},q^{B}\}_{\text{PB}}=-(-)^{\varepsilon(A)\varepsilon(B)}[q^{B},p_{A}\}_{\text{PB}}=-\delta_{A}^{B}\;,

[q^{A},q^{B}\}_{\text{PB}}=-(-)^{\varepsilon(A)\varepsilon(B)}[q^{B},q^{A}\}_{\text{PB}}=0\;,\penalty\ \penalty\ [p_{A},p_{B}\}_{\text{PB}}=-(-)^{\varepsilon(A)\varepsilon(B)}[p_{B},p_{A}\}_{\text{PB}}=0\;,

(7.45)

where
\varepsilon(A)
is Grassmann (fermionic) parity of
p_{A}
and
q^{A}
equal to
0
for bosons and
1
for fermions
46
46

46

The Poisson brackets are essentially defined by relations (
7.45
) and the Leibniz rules
[fg,h\}_{\text{PB}}=f[g,h\}_{\text{PB}}+(-)^{\varepsilon(f)\varepsilon(h)}[f,h\}_{\text{PB}}g
, where
f
,
g
and
h
are arbitrary functions of
q^{A}
and
p_{A}
. An explicit expression is given by

[f,g\}_{\text{PB}}=(-)^{\varepsilon(A)\varepsilon(f)}\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial q^{A}}\,\frac{\partial g}{\partial p_{A}}-(-)^{\varepsilon(A)}\frac{\partial f}{\partial p_{A}}\,\frac{\partial g}{\partial q^{A}}\right)\penalty\ .

.

The canonical Hamiltonian
H_{0}(p_{A},q^{A})
is then defined by Legendre transform of the Lagrangian
47
47

47

We simplify the definition of the Legendre transform here since this is sufficient for our purposes. For a more precise treatment and examples of systems where this distinction is crucial see e.g. section 3 of
[
173
]
and references therein.

H_{0}(p_{A},q^{A})=\dot{q}{}^{A}p_{A}-L(q^{A},\dot{q}{}^{A})\;

(7.46)

and the evolution of the dynamical system is described by the Hamiltonian equations

\dot{f}(q,p)=[{f}(q,p)\,,\,H_{0}]_{\text{PB}}\;\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\begin{cases}\dot{q}^{A}=\dfrac{\partial H_{0}}{\partial p_{A}}\;,\cr\cr\dot{p}_{A}=-\dfrac{\partial H_{0}}{\partial q^{A}}\;.\end{cases}

(7.47)

In our case, we first denote the momenta conjugate to the bosonic and fermionic coordinate functions by

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}p_{a}=\dfrac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\tau}}{\partial\dot{x}^{a}}\penalty\ ,&&\bar{\Pi}_{\alpha}=\dfrac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\tau}}{\partial\dot{\theta}^{\alpha}}\penalty\ ,&&{\Pi}_{\alpha}=\dfrac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\tau}}{\partial\dot{\bar{\theta}}^{\alpha}}\end{array}

(7.48)

so that their nonvanishing Poisson brackets are

\begin{array}[]{lll}&\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ [p_{a},x^{b}]_{\text{PB}}=-[x^{b},p_{a}]_{\text{PB}}=-\delta_{a}^{b}\penalty\ ,&\\
\\
&\penalty\ \{\bar{\Pi}_{\alpha},\theta^{\beta}\}_{\text{PB}}=\{\theta^{\beta},\bar{\Pi}_{\alpha}\}_{\text{PB}}=-\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta}\penalty\ ,&\{{\Pi}_{\alpha},\bar{\theta}^{\beta}\}_{\text{PB}}=\{\bar{\theta}^{\beta},{\Pi}_{\alpha}\}_{\text{PB}}=-\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta}\penalty\ .\end{array}

(7.49)

7.2.2

Covariant momenta and Poisson/Dirac brackets in the spinor moving frame sector

Similarly, we can introduce the canonical momenta conjugate to spinor frame variables

\bar{P}_{\alpha}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\tau}}{\partial{\dot{w}}^{\alpha}}\;,\qquad P_{\alpha}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\tau}}{\partial{\dot{\bar{w}}}{}^{\alpha}}

(7.50)

which have nonvanishing Poisson brackets

{}[\bar{P}{}^{\alpha},w_{\beta}]_{\text{PB}}={-\delta^{\alpha}{}_{\beta}{}}\;,\qquad{}[P^{\alpha},\bar{w}_{\beta}]_{\text{PB}}={-\delta^{\alpha}{}_{\beta}{}}\;.\qquad

(7.51)

When working with these canonical momenta, we have to consider the condition (
7.13
) as a second class constraint (in terminology of Dirac
[
174
]
; see below for further discussion). It turns out that this constraint
48
48

48

Here and below,
\approx
denotes weak equality in the sense of
[
174
]
, i.e. relations between coordinates and momenta which can be used only after all Poisson brackets are calculated.
is conjugate to

w_{\alpha}\bar{P}{}^{\alpha}+\bar{w}_{\alpha}{P}{}^{\alpha}\approx 0\;,

(7.52)

which also appears in our model. Then we could resolve this pair of second class constraints by passing from Poisson brackets to the corresponding Dirac brackets (see
[
134
]
and refs. therein), after which both (
7.13
) and (
7.52
) can be considered as satisfied in the strong sense.

Alternatively, this procedure can be overcame by introducing the so-called covariant momenta

{\mathfrak{d}}=w_{\alpha}P^{\alpha}\;,\qquad\bar{{\mathfrak{d}}}=\bar{w}_{\alpha}\bar{P}{}^{\alpha}\;,\qquad{\mathfrak{d}}^{(0)}=\bar{w}_{\alpha}P^{\alpha}-w_{\alpha}\bar{P}{}^{\alpha}\;,\qquad

(7.53)

which have the Poisson brackets with spinor frame variables

\displaystyle{}[{\mathfrak{d}},w_{\alpha}]_{\text{PB}}=0\;,\qquad

\displaystyle[{\mathfrak{d}},\bar{w}_{\alpha}]_{\text{PB}}=-{w}_{\alpha}\;,\qquad

(7.54)

\displaystyle[\bar{{\mathfrak{d}}},w_{\alpha}]_{\text{PB}}=-\bar{w}_{\alpha}\;,\qquad

\displaystyle[\bar{{\mathfrak{d}}},\bar{w}_{\alpha}]_{\text{PB}}=0\;,\qquad

\displaystyle{}[{\mathfrak{d}}^{(0)},w_{\alpha}]_{\text{PB}}=w_{\alpha}\;,\qquad

\displaystyle[{\mathfrak{d}}^{(0)},\bar{w}_{\alpha}]_{\text{PB}}=-\bar{w}_{\alpha}\;.\qquad

It is straightforward to verify that these covariant momenta have vanishing Poisson brackets with the constraints (
7.13
). Therefore, by working only with the covariant momenta, we can consider the constraint (
7.13
) as satisfied in the strong sense.

Notice that (
7.54
) imply the following Poisson brackets of the covariant momenta with moving frame vectors

\displaystyle{}[{\mathfrak{d}},u_{a}^{(0)}]_{\text{PB}}=-u_{a}\;,\qquad

\displaystyle[{\mathfrak{d}},u_{a}]_{\text{PB}}=0\;,\qquad

\displaystyle[{\mathfrak{d}},\bar{u}_{a}]_{\text{PB}}=-2u_{a}^{(0)}\;,\qquad

\displaystyle{}[\bar{{\mathfrak{d}}},u_{a}^{(0)}]_{\text{PB}}=-\bar{u}_{a}\;,\qquad

\displaystyle[\bar{{\mathfrak{d}}},u_{a}]_{\text{PB}}=-2u_{a}^{(0)}\;,\qquad

\displaystyle[\bar{{\mathfrak{d}}},\bar{u}_{a}]_{\text{PB}}=0\;,\qquad\penalty\

(7.55)

\displaystyle{}[{\mathfrak{d}}^{(0)},u_{a}^{(0)}]_{\text{PB}}=0\;,\;\qquad

\displaystyle[{\mathfrak{d}}^{(0)},u_{a}]_{\text{PB}}=2u_{a}\;,\qquad

\displaystyle[{\mathfrak{d}}^{(0)},\bar{u}_{a}]_{\text{PB}}=-2\bar{u}_{a}\;.

The covariant momenta thus generate the
\mathfrak{so}(1,2)\simeq\mathfrak{su}(1,1)
algebra under Poisson brackets

\displaystyle{}[{\mathfrak{d}}^{(0)},{\mathfrak{d}}]_{\text{PB}}=2{\mathfrak{d}}\;,\;\qquad[{\mathfrak{d}}^{(0)},\bar{{\mathfrak{d}}}]_{\text{PB}}=-2\bar{{\mathfrak{d}}}\;,

(7.56)

\displaystyle{}[{\mathfrak{d}},\bar{{\mathfrak{d}}}]_{\text{PB}}={\mathfrak{d}}^{(0)}\;.

To express the canonical Hamiltonian (
7.46
) in terms of the Lagrangian and covariant momenta, we start from the standard relation

\text{d}\tau H_{0}=\text{d}w_{\alpha}\bar{P}^{\alpha}+\text{d}\bar{w}_{\alpha}{P}^{\alpha}+\ldots-\mathcal{L}\penalty\ ,

(7.57)

substitute for the derivatives of
w_{\alpha}
and
\bar{w}_{\alpha}
their expressions in terms of Cartan forms, Eq. (
7.15
) (valid when constraint (
7.13
) is satisfied), and use (
7.53
) arriving at

\text{d}\tau H_{0}=ia{\mathfrak{d}}^{(0)}+if\bar{{\mathfrak{d}}}-i\bar{f}{\mathfrak{d}}+\ldots-\mathcal{L}\;\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ H_{0}=ia_{\tau}{\mathfrak{d}}^{(0)}+if_{\tau}\bar{{\mathfrak{d}}}-i\bar{f}_{\tau}{\mathfrak{d}}+\ldots-\text{d}\tau\mathcal{L}_{\tau}\;.\qquad

(7.58)

This relation makes it manifest that the covariant momenta correspond to derivatives of the Lagrangian with respect to the Cartan forms

{\mathfrak{d}}^{(0)}=-i\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\tau}}{\partial a_{\tau}}\;,\qquad{\mathfrak{d}}=i\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\tau}}{\partial\bar{f}_{\tau}}\;,\qquad\bar{{\mathfrak{d}}}=-i\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\tau}}{\partial{f}_{\tau}}\;.\qquad

(7.59)

7.2.3

Simplifying Poisson/Dirac brackets in the sector of matrix fields

The canonical momenta of the bosonic matrix fields are

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\dot{\mathbb{Z}}_{j}^{i}}\approx\dfrac{1}{\mu^{6}}\bar{\mathbb{P}}^{j}_{i}\penalty\ ,\qquad\dfrac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\dot{\bar{\mathbb{P}}}{}^{j}_{i}}\approx 0\;,\qquad

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\dot{\bar{\mathbb{Z}}}_{j}^{i}}\approx\dfrac{1}{\mu^{6}}\mathbb{P}^{j}_{i}\penalty\ ,\qquad\dfrac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\dot{{\mathbb{P}}}{}^{j}_{i}}\approx 0\;.\qquad

(7.60)

These relations correspond to resolved pairs of second class constraints, allowing us to exclude the variables
\mathbb{P}^{j}_{i}
,
\bar{\mathbb{P}}^{j}_{i}
and their momenta from the consideration.

However, a more convenient equivalent procedure is to remove the momenta conjugate to
\mathbb{P}^{j}_{i}
and
\bar{\mathbb{P}}^{j}_{i}
and instead replace the momenta conjugate to
\mathbb{Z}=\mathbb{Z}_{i}^{j}
and
\bar{\mathbb{Z}}=\bar{\mathbb{Z}}_{i}^{j}

by
\bar{\mathbb{P}}^{j}_{i}
and
\mathbb{P}^{j}_{i}
. Then, the nonvanishing Poisson brackets for these new phase space variables (in fact, Dirac brackets) are
49
49

49

Note that the last terms on the r.h.s.’s of Eqs. (
7.61
),
-\dfrac{1}{N}\delta^{j}_{i}\delta^{l}_{k}
, ensure their consistency with the tracelessness condition of matrices (
7.2
).

\begin{array}[]{ccl}[\bar{\mathbb{P}}^{j}_{i},\mathbb{Z}_{k}{}^{l}]_{\text{PB}}=-\mu^{6}\left(\delta^{l}_{i}\delta_{k}^{j}-\dfrac{1}{N}\delta^{j}_{i}\delta^{l}_{k}\right)\penalty\ ,&&[\mathbb{P}^{j}_{i},\bar{\mathbb{Z}}_{k}{}^{l}]_{\text{PB}}=-\mu^{6}\left(\delta^{l}_{i}\delta_{k}^{j}-\dfrac{1}{N}\delta^{j}_{i}\delta^{l}_{k}\right)\penalty\ .\end{array}

(7.61)

A similar approach is standard when constructing Hamiltonian mechanics for fermionic fields with canonical kinetic term, where the conjugate momentum of fermionic field is identified with the field itself. In our case, the fermionic matrix fields also possess such a kinetic term.

The canonical momenta for the fermionic matrix field
{\bm{\Psi}}={\bm{\Psi}}_{i}^{j}
and its Hermitian conjugate
\bar{\bm{\Psi}}=\bar{\bm{\Psi}}_{i}^{j}
are given by

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\Pi_{\bar{\bm{\Psi}}}=\dfrac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\dot{\bar{\bm{\Psi}}}}\approx-\dfrac{i}{8\mu^{6}}{\bm{\Psi}}\penalty\ ,&&\bar{\Pi}_{\bm{\Psi}}=\dfrac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\dot{\bm{\Psi}}}\approx-\dfrac{i}{8\mu^{6}}\bar{\bm{\Psi}}\penalty\ .\end{array}

(7.62)

The canonical Poisson brackets with nonvanishing r.h.s. are

\left\{{\bm{\Pi}}_{i}{}^{j},\bar{\bm{\Psi}}_{k}{}^{l}\right\}_{\text{PB}}=\left\{{\bm{\Psi}}_{i}{}^{j},\bar{\bm{\Pi}}_{k}{}^{l}\right\}_{\text{PB}}=-(\delta^{l}_{i}\delta_{k}^{j}-\dfrac{1}{N}\delta^{j}_{i}\delta^{l}_{k})\penalty\

(7.63)

where, for shortness, we write
{\bm{\Pi}}_{i}{}^{j}:=(\Pi_{\bar{\bm{\Psi}}})_{i}{}^{j}
and
\bar{\bm{\Pi}}_{i}{}^{j}:=(\bar{\Pi}_{\bar{\bm{\Psi}}})_{i}{}^{j}
.

From these, we see that (
7.62
) are second class constraints. Since they are not in explicitly resolved form, to treat them as strong equalities, we have to replace Poisson brackets by Dirac brackets

\begin{split}[\ldots,\ldots\}_{\text{D}}=[\ldots,\ldots\}_{\text{PB}}&-4i\mu^{6}[\ldots,(\Pi_{\bar{\bm{\Psi}}}+\frac{i}{8\mu^{6}}{\bm{\Psi}})_{i}^{j}\}_{\text{PB}}[(\bar{\Pi}_{{\bm{\Psi}}}+\frac{i}{8\mu^{6}}\bar{\bm{\Psi}})_{j}^{i},\ldots\}_{\text{PB}}-\\
\\
&-4i\mu^{6}[\ldots,(\bar{\Pi}_{{\bm{\Psi}}}\frac{i}{8\mu^{6}}\bar{\bm{\Psi}})_{i}^{j}\}_{\text{PB}}[(\Pi_{\bar{\bm{\Psi}}}+\frac{i}{8\mu^{6}}{\bm{\Psi}})_{j}^{i},\ldots\}_{\text{PB}}\penalty\ .\end{split}

(7.64)

With these Dirac brackets, the complex fermionic matrix field and its Hermitian conjugate become canonically conjugate

\left\{{\bm{\Psi}}_{i}{}^{j},\bar{\bm{\Psi}}_{k}{}^{l}\right\}_{\text{D}}=-4i\mu^{6}(\delta^{l}_{i}\delta_{k}^{j}-\dfrac{1}{N}\delta^{j}_{i}\delta^{l}_{k})\penalty\ ,\qquad\left\{{\bm{\Psi}}_{i}{}^{j},{\bm{\Psi}}_{k}{}^{l}\right\}_{\text{D}}=0\;,\qquad\left\{\bar{\bm{\Psi}}_{i}{}^{j},\bar{\bm{\Psi}}_{k}{}^{l}\right\}_{\text{D}}=0\;.\qquad

(7.65)

Below for simplicity, and in line with the common practice of imposing conditions of the form (
7.65
) as canonical, we will call these Dirac brackets Poisson brackets and write (
7.65
) as

\left\{{\bm{\Psi}}_{i}{}^{j},\bar{\bm{\Psi}}_{k}{}^{l}\right\}_{\text{PB}}=-4i\mu^{6}(\delta^{l}_{i}\delta_{k}^{j}-\dfrac{1}{N}\delta^{j}_{i}\delta^{l}_{k})\penalty\ ,\qquad\left\{{\bm{\Psi}}_{i}{}^{j},{\bm{\Psi}}_{k}{}^{l}\right\}_{\text{PB}}=0\;,\qquad\left\{\bar{\bm{\Psi}}_{i}{}^{j},\bar{\bm{\Psi}}_{k}{}^{l}\right\}_{\text{PB}}=0\;.\qquad

(7.66)

This is more convenient also because we have already used implicitly the passage to Dirac brackets in the spinor moving frame sector of our model.

7.2.4

Primary constraints

With our Lagrangian (
7.43
), we compute the canonical momenta (
7.44
) and covariant momenta (
7.59
) for the fields in both the center of mass sector and the gauge field. This leads us to the following set of
primary constraints
of the system

\Phi_{a}:=p_{a}-mu_{a}^{(0)}-\dfrac{2}{\mu^{6}}\dfrac{\mathcal{H}}{m}u_{a}^{(0)}\approx 0\penalty\ ,

(7.67)

d_{\alpha}:=\bar{\Pi}_{\alpha}+ip_{a}(\gamma^{a}\bar{\theta})_{\alpha}-m\bar{\theta}_{\alpha}-\dfrac{i}{\mu^{6}\sqrt{m}}w_{\alpha}\,\bar{\nu}\approx 0\penalty\ ,

(7.68)

\bar{d}_{\alpha}:={\Pi}_{\alpha}+ip_{a}(\gamma^{a}\theta)_{\alpha}+m\theta_{\alpha}-\dfrac{i}{\mu^{6}\sqrt{m}}\bar{w}_{\alpha}\,\nu\approx 0\penalty\ ,

(7.69)

{\mathfrak{d}}\approx 0\penalty\ ,

(7.70)

\bar{{\mathfrak{d}}}\approx 0\;,

(7.71)

U:={\mathfrak{d}}^{(0)}-\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\,{\cal B}\approx 0\penalty\ ,

(7.72)

through

{\cal B}:={\rm tr}\left(\bar{\mathbb{P}}\mathbb{Z}-\mathbb{P}\bar{\mathbb{Z}}+\frac{i}{8}{\bm{\Psi}}\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}\right)\penalty\ ,

(7.73)

and

\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{A}}:=\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot{\mathbb{A}}_{\tau}}\approx 0\penalty\ .

(7.74)

The last relation is the only primary constraint imposed just on the matrix variables. However, the matrix fields also contribute to the constraints derived from the definition of momenta of the center of mass variables through
{\cal H}
of (
7.37
) in (
7.67
), via

\bar{\nu}=\text{tr}(\bar{\bm{\Psi}}\bar{\mathbb{P}}+{\bm{\Psi}}[\mathbb{Z},\bar{\mathbb{Z}}])\;\qquad{\text{and}}\qquad\nu=\text{tr}({\bm{\Psi}}\mathbb{P}+\bar{\bm{\Psi}}[\mathbb{Z},\bar{\mathbb{Z}}])\;

(7.75)

in (
7.68
) and (
7.69
).

7.2.5

Canonical Hamiltonian and secondary constraints

The canonical Hamiltonian of our system is defined by the Legendre transform of the Lagrangian, which we write in terms of SO
(1,2)/\text{SO}(2)
Cartan forms and covariant momenta (see equation (
7.58
)) as

\begin{split}\text{d}\tau H_{0}&=\text{d}x^{a}p_{a}+\text{d}\theta^{\alpha}\bar{\Pi}_{\alpha}+\text{d}\bar{\theta}^{\alpha}{\Pi}_{\alpha}+ia\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}^{(0)}+if\bar{{\mathfrak{d}}}-i\bar{f}{\mathfrak{d}}+\\
&+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\text{tr}(\text{d}\mathbb{Z}\bar{\mathbb{P}})+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\text{tr}(\text{d}\bar{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{P})-\frac{i}{8\mu^{6}}\text{tr}(\text{d}{\bm{\Psi}}\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}})-\frac{i}{8\mu^{6}}\text{tr}(\text{d}\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}{\bm{\Psi}})+\text{tr}(\text{d}\mathbb{A}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{A}})-\mathcal{L}_{\text{mD}0}\penalty\ .\end{split}

(7.76)

For our Lagrangian (
7.43
), after extracting the primary constraints, (
7.76
) reduces to

\displaystyle H_{0}=E_{\tau}^{a}\Phi_{a}+\dot{\theta}{}^{\alpha}d_{\alpha}+\dot{\bar{\theta}}{}^{\alpha}\bar{d}_{\alpha}+ia_{\tau}U+if_{\tau}\bar{\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}}-i\bar{f}_{\tau}\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}+\text{tr}(\text{d}\mathbb{A}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{A}})+{\rm tr}({\mathbb{A}}_{\tau}{\mathbb{G}})\approx{\rm tr}({\mathbb{A}}_{\tau}{\mathbb{G}})\penalty\ ,

(7.77)

where
\mathbb{G}
is a
N\times N
anti-Hermitian traceless matrix

\mathbb{G}:=\dfrac{1}{\mu^{6}}\left([\bar{\mathbb{Z}},\mathbb{P}]+[\mathbb{Z},\bar{\mathbb{P}}]-\frac{i}{4}\{{\bm{\Psi}},\bar{\bm{\Psi}}\}\right)\;.

(7.78)

The last expression, valid in the weak sense, can be used to check whether the preservation of the primary constraints under evolution, as defined by Hamiltonian equations (
7.47
), produces secondary constraints. Indeed, this happens: requiring the preservation of the primary constraint (
7.74
),
\dot{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{A}}=[\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{A}},H_{0}]\approx 0
, leads to the
secondary constraint

\mathbb{G}=\dfrac{1}{\mu^{6}}\left([\bar{\mathbb{Z}},\mathbb{P}]+[\mathbb{Z},\bar{\mathbb{P}}]-\frac{i}{4}\{{\bm{\Psi}},\bar{\bm{\Psi}}\}\right)\approx 0\;.

(7.79)

This reveals the role of the 1d gauge field
{\mathbb{A}}_{\tau}
as a Lagrange multiplier for the constraint (
7.79
), which we will call the
Gauss law
, keeping in mind its counterpart in higher dimensional supersymmetric gauge theories. Furthermore, since it can be shown that constraint (
7.74
) is the first class and generates a gauge symmetry corresponding to an arbitrary shift of
{\mathbb{A}}_{\tau}
, we will simplify the presentation by omitting the conjugate variables
{\mathbb{A}}_{\tau}
and
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{A}}
from further consideration
50
50

50

More precisely, one can understand this reduction of the phase space as fixing the gauge
{\mathbb{A}}_{\tau}=0
using the symmetry generated by the constraint
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{A}}
and then treating the constraint (
7.74
) as strong equality.
.

As a result, the Gauss law (
7.79
) implies the canonical Hamiltonian vanishes weakly

H_{0}\approx 0\;.

(7.80)

7.2.6

First class constraints and gauge symmetries

The vanishing of canonical Hamiltonian (
7.80
) implies that the total Hamiltonian of our system, as introduced by Dirac in
[
174
]
, is a linear combination of the constraints,

\displaystyle H=b^{a}\Phi_{a}+{\kappa}^{\alpha}d_{\alpha}+\bar{{\kappa}}^{\alpha}\bar{d}_{\alpha}+ik\bar{{\mathfrak{d}}}-i\bar{k}{\mathfrak{d}}+ik^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{d}}{}^{(0)}-\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\,{\cal B}\right)+\text{tr}(\mathbb{Y}\mathbb{G})\penalty\ ,

(7.81)

where the coefficients in this expression, functions of proper time
\tau
known as Lagrange multipliers
51
51

51

Notice the reappearance of the pure gauge 1d gauge field
{\mathbb{A}}_{\tau}
, which we removed from consideration using the gauge symmetry generated by (
7.74
), now reincarnated as the Lagrange multiplier
{\mathbb{Y}}
for the Gauss constraints (
7.79
).
, are restricted by requiring preservation of all the constraints under the evolution
[
174
]
,

\frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}\tau}(\text{constraints})=[\text{constraints}\,,\,H]_{\text{PB}}\approx 0\;\qquad

(7.82)

(
cf.
(
7.47
))
52
52

52

Generically this procedure might result in appearance of further secondary constraints, but in this case the system instead produces equations for the multipliers.
. The solution of Eqs. (
7.82
) expresses the original Lagrange multipliers in terms of fewer variables acting as (true) Lagrange multipliers for the first class constraints that generate, on the Poisson brackets, gauge symmetries of the dynamical system under consideration.

To specify the system of equations for Lagrange multipliers, it is useful first to determine the algebra of constraints under Poisson brackets. Let us start from the Gauss law constraint (
7.78
) which is (an anti-Hermitian traceless) matrix
{\mathbb{G}}={\mathbb{G}}_{i}{}^{j}
different matrix elements of which may have nonvanishing brackets. Actually these form a representation of the
\mathfrak{su}(N)
algebra. To write it in a compact and comprehensive form, it is convenient to introduce “reference” traceless matrices
\mathbb{Y}
and
\mathbb{Y}^{\prime}
and compute the brackets of the traces of
{\mathbb{G}}
with these matrices. In such a way we find the
\mathfrak{su}(N)
algebra in the form

[\text{tr}(\mathbb{Y}\mathbb{G}),\text{tr}(\mathbb{Y}^{\prime}\mathbb{G})]_{\text{PB}}=\text{tr}\left([\mathbb{Y},\mathbb{Y}^{\prime}]\mathbb{G}\right)\penalty\ .

(7.83)

Furthermore,
{\mathbb{G}}
has vanishing Poisson brackets with all other constraints, which reflecting their SU
(N)
invariance (see Appendix
G
for technical details). Thus,
{\mathbb{G}}
is a first class constraint generating the SU
(N)
gauge symmetry of the model.

The remaining constraint algebra, given by Eqs. (
7.67
)-(
7.74
), is characterised by the following nonvanishing Poisson brackets (see Appendix
G
):

\begin{array}[]{ccc}[\mathfrak{d},\mathfrak{d}^{(0)}-\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\mathcal{B}]_{\text{PB}}=-2\mathfrak{d}\penalty\ ,&&[\mathfrak{d},\Phi_{a}]{}_{\text{PB}}=\left(m+\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{H}}{m}\right)u_{a}\penalty\ ,\end{array}

(7.84)

[\mathfrak{d},\bar{d}_{\alpha}]_{\text{PB}}=\frac{i}{\mu^{6}\sqrt{m}}w_{\alpha}\nu\penalty\ ,

(7.85)

[\mathfrak{d},\bar{\mathfrak{d}}]_{\text{PB}}=\mathfrak{d}^{(0)}\penalty\ ,

(7.86)

\begin{array}[]{ccc}[\bar{\mathfrak{d}},\mathfrak{d}^{(0)}-\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\mathcal{B}]_{\text{PB}}=2\bar{\mathfrak{d}}\penalty\ ,&&[\bar{\mathfrak{d}},\Phi_{a}]_{\text{PB}}=\left(m+\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{H}}{m}\right)\bar{u}_{a}\penalty\ ,\end{array}

(7.87)

[\bar{\mathfrak{d}},d_{\alpha}]_{\text{PB}}=\frac{i}{\mu^{6}\sqrt{m}}\bar{w}_{\alpha}\bar{\nu}\penalty\ ,

(7.88)

[\Phi_{a},d_{\alpha}]_{\rm{PB}}=\;\frac{2i}{m\sqrt{m}}\,u^{(0)}_{a}w_{\alpha}\,{\rm tr}({\bm{\Psi}}{\mathbb{G}})\;,

(7.89)

[\Phi_{a},\bar{d}_{\alpha}]_{\rm{PB}}=-\frac{2i}{m\sqrt{m}}\,u^{(0)}_{a}\bar{w}_{\alpha}\,{\rm tr}(\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}{\mathbb{G}})\,,

(7.90)

and

\{d_{\alpha},d_{\beta}\}_{\rm{PB}}=-\frac{8i}{m}\,w_{\alpha}w_{\beta}\,{\rm tr}(\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbb{G}})\;,

(7.91)

\{\bar{d}_{\alpha},\bar{d}_{\beta}\}_{\rm{PB}}=\;\;\frac{8i}{m}\,\bar{w}_{\alpha}\bar{w}_{\beta}\,{\rm tr}({\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{G}})\,,

(7.92)

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{split}\{d_{\alpha},\bar{d}_{\beta}\}_{\rm{PB}}&=-2ip_{a}\gamma^{a}_{\alpha\beta}+2m\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}+\frac{4i}{\mu^{6}}\,w_{\alpha}\bar{w}_{\beta}\,\frac{{\cal H}}{m}=\\
&=-2i\Phi_{a}\gamma^{a}_{\alpha\beta}-4iw_{\beta}\bar{w}_{\alpha}\,\left(m+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\,\frac{{\cal H}}{m}\right)\;.\end{split}\end{array}

(7.93)

Using this algebra, the requirement of constraint preservation under evolution, described by Eq. (
7.82
) with Hamiltonian (
7.81
), implies that the Lagrange multipliers must satisfy a system of equations solved by

\displaystyle k=0\;,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \qquad\bar{k}=0\;,

(7.94)

\displaystyle b^{a}u_{a}=0\;,\qquad b^{a}\bar{u}_{a}=0

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\qquad b^{a}=bu^{(0)a}\;,

(7.95)

\displaystyle\bar{\kappa}^{\beta}w_{\beta}=0

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\qquad\bar{\kappa}^{\beta}=\bar{\kappa}w^{\beta}\;,

(7.96)

\displaystyle\kappa^{\beta}\bar{w}_{\beta}=0

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\qquad\kappa^{\beta}=\kappa\bar{w}^{\beta}\;.

(7.97)

Thus, the total Hamiltonian reads

\displaystyle H=b\,u^{a(0)}\Phi_{a}+{\kappa}\,\bar{w}^{\alpha}d_{\alpha}+\bar{{\kappa}}\,w^{\alpha}\bar{d}_{\alpha}+ik^{(0)}\left({\mathfrak{d}}{}^{(0)}-\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\,{\cal B}\right)+\text{tr}(\mathbb{Y}\mathbb{G})\penalty\ .

(7.98)

The arbitrary Lagrange multipliers
b
,
\kappa
,
\bar{{\kappa}}
,
k^{(0)}
and
\mathbb{Y}_{j}{}^{i}
(with
\mathbb{Y}_{i}{}^{i}=0
) reflect the gauge symmetries of our model: reparametrization, local worldline supersymmetry (
\kappa
-symmetry), U
(1)
and SU
(N)
symmetries. In (
7.98
) they are multiplied by the constraints generating these symmetries,

\Phi^{(0)}:=p_{a}u^{a(0)}-\left(m+\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\,\frac{{\cal H}}{m}\right)\approx 0\penalty\ ,

(7.99)

\bar{w}^{\alpha}d_{\alpha}:=\bar{w}^{\alpha}(\bar{\Pi}_{\alpha}+ip_{a}(\gamma^{a}\bar{\theta})_{\alpha}-m\bar{\theta}_{\alpha})+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\bar{{\nu}}\approx 0\penalty\ ,

(7.100)

w^{\alpha}\bar{d}_{\alpha}:=w^{\alpha}({\Pi}_{\alpha}+ip_{a}(\gamma^{a}\theta)_{\alpha}+m\theta_{\alpha})-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}{\nu}\approx 0\penalty\ ,

(7.101)

U^{(0)}:=\mathfrak{d}^{(0)}-\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}{\cal B}\approx 0\penalty\ ,\qquad

(7.102)

with
{\cal B}
defined in (
7.73
), and secondary constraint (
7.79
).

7.2.7

Second class constraints

The remaining constraints are the second class ones. They can be collected in conjugate pairs

\mathfrak{d}\approx 0\penalty\ ,\qquad\bar{\mathfrak{d}}\approx 0\penalty\ ,

(7.103)

\Phi:=u^{a}\Phi_{a}=u^{a}p_{a}\approx 0\penalty\ ,\qquad\bar{\Phi}:=\bar{u}^{a}\Phi_{a}=\bar{u}^{a}p_{a}\approx 0\penalty\ ,

(7.104)

w^{\alpha}d_{\alpha}=w^{\alpha}(\bar{\Pi}_{\alpha}+ip_{a}(\gamma^{a}\bar{\theta})_{\alpha}-m\bar{\theta}_{\alpha})\approx 0\penalty\ ,\qquad\bar{w}^{\alpha}\bar{d}_{\alpha}=\bar{w}^{\alpha}({\Pi}_{\alpha}+ip_{a}(\gamma^{a}\theta)_{\alpha}+m\theta_{\alpha})\approx 0\penalty\ ,

(7.105)

as can be seen from their Poisson brackets, particularly

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{d},\bar{\Phi}]_{\text{PB}}=-2\Phi^{(0)}-2\left(m+\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{H}}{m}\right)\approx-2\left(m+\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{H}}{m}\right)\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\

(7.106)

\displaystyle[\bar{\mathfrak{d}},\Phi]_{\text{PB}}=-2\Phi^{(0)}-2\left(m+\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{H}}{m}\right)\approx-2\left(m+\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{H}}{m}\right)\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\

(7.107)

\displaystyle\{w^{\alpha}d_{\alpha},\bar{w}^{\alpha}\bar{d}_{\alpha}\}_{\text{PB}}=-2i\Phi^{(0)}-4i\left(m+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{H}}{m}\right)\approx-4i\left(m+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{H}}{m}\right)\penalty\ .

(7.108)

7.2.8

The algebra of first and second class constraints

Notice that there are also other nonvanishing brackets among the second class constraints:

\displaystyle{}[\mathfrak{d},\bar{\mathfrak{d}}]_{\text{PB}}=\mathfrak{d}^{(0)}=\left(\mathfrak{d}^{(0)}-\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\mathcal{B}\right)+\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\mathcal{B}\approx\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\mathcal{B}\penalty\ ,\qquad

(7.109)

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{d},\bar{w}^{\alpha}\bar{d}_{\alpha}]_{\text{PB}}=-w^{\alpha}\bar{d}_{\alpha}-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}\sqrt{m}}\nu\approx-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}\sqrt{m}}\nu\penalty\ ,\qquad

(7.110)

\displaystyle[\bar{\mathfrak{d}},w^{\alpha}d_{\alpha}]_{\text{PB}}=-\bar{w}^{\alpha}{d}_{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}\sqrt{m}}\bar{\nu}\approx\;\frac{1}{\mu^{6}\sqrt{m}}\bar{\nu}\penalty\ .\qquad\penalty\

(7.111)

The algebra of the first and second class constraints is summarized in Table
7.1
(below), where we omit the first class constraints
{\mathbb{G}}_{i}{}^{j}
(
7.78
) since they only have nonvanishing brackets with themselves, (
7.83
). We also introduce the notation

M({\cal H}):=m+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{{\cal H}}{m}\;.

(7.112)

This should not be confused with
{\cal M}({\cal H})
which was discussed in the first sections of this chapter as well as in chapters
4
-
6
.

The appearance of the positively definite additive contributions
M({\cal H})
or
M(2{\cal H})
in the r.h.s. of the Poisson brackets clearly indicates the pairing of fermionic and bosonic second class constraints.

The algebra of constraints is sufficiently complicated to hamper the use of BRST quantization with standard “abelization” scheme for second class constraints
[
175
,
176
]
. The technical problem comes from the non-canonical form of the bosonic second class constraints algebra in which, besides the nonvanishing brackets (
7.106
), (
7.107
) and (
7.108
), the other brackets (
7.109
), (
7.110
) and (
7.111
) also do not vanish in the weak sense.

Algebra of the first and second class constraints on the Poisson brackets of the simplest 3D mD0 system.
M({\cal H}):=m+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{{\cal H}}{m}
is defined in (
