).

Moreover, when attempting to apply the Gupta-Bleuler quantization scheme in its canonical forms, we found that for this algebra of constraints the corresponding system of equations (in supercoordinate representation) admits only the trivial solution. We demonstrate this explicitly for the simplest case of vanishing all the matrix and fermionic fields in Appendix
H
. This is the only example known to the authors where the Gupta-Bleuler simplified method of quantization of dynamical system fails.

Hence, the only remaining option, capable of overcoming these difficulties, is to apply the Dirac bracket procedure in the bosonic sector of the system. Notably, this procedure can be significantly simplified (in fact, reduced to explicit resolution of the bosonic second class constraints) once we pass to the so-called
analytic basis
in the center of mass sector of the configuration superspace. We will carry out this change of coordinate basis in the next section.

7.3

Hamiltonian mechanics in the analytical basis

In order to simplify the constraint structure and enable an explicit resolution of the pairs bosonic second class constraints, in this section we reformulate the Hamiltonian mechanics in the analytical (Lorentz harmonic) basis.

7.3.1

Constraints in the analytical basis

Let us begin by noticing that the center of mass sector of the configurational space of our dynamical system can be identified with the so-called Lorentz harmonic superspace, an extended enlarged superspace with coordinates
53
53

53

Here, “extended” refers to
{\cal N}=2
supersymmetry while “enlarged” reflects the presence of additional bosonic spinor (spinor moving frame or Lorentz harmonic) coordinates. The terminology goes back to
[
144
]
, which began the line of seminal papers on Harmonic superspaces
[
152
,
177
,
147
,
146
]
. See Appendix
I
for further discussion.

\Sigma^{(3+3|4)}=\{(x^{a},\theta^{\alpha},\bar{\theta}^{\alpha},w_{\alpha},\bar{w}_{\alpha})\}=:\{(z^{M},w_{\alpha},\bar{w}_{\alpha})\}\;,\qquad\bar{w}^{\alpha}{w}_{\alpha}=i\;.

(7.113)

The complete configuration superspace also includes
2(N^{2}-1)
bosonic and
(N^{2}-1)
fermionic matrix coordinates, and its coordinate basis can be chosen as

\Sigma^{(4+2N^{2}|3+N^{2})}=\{(x^{a},\theta^{\alpha},\bar{\theta}^{\alpha},w_{\alpha},\bar{w}_{\alpha};{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}},{\bm{\Psi}})\}=:\{(z^{M},w_{\alpha},\bar{w}_{\alpha};{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}},{\bm{\Psi}})\}\penalty\ .

(7.114)

This implies that
\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}
is considered to be momentum conjugate to
{{\bm{\Psi}}}
.

Clearly, other coordinate bases can be chosen. In our case, it is convenient
to perform a change of coordinate in the center of mass sector
(
7.113
) of the whole configurational superspace, choosing in it the analytic coordinate basis

\Sigma^{(3+3|4)}=\{({\rm x}{}^{(0)},{\rm x}_{A},\bar{{\rm x}}_{A}\;,\theta^{w},{\theta}^{\bar{w}},\bar{\theta}^{{w}},\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}};w_{\alpha},\bar{w}_{\alpha})\}=:\{(z^{(M)}_{An},\;w_{\alpha},\bar{w}_{\alpha}\;,{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}},{\bm{\Psi}},\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}})\}\;,\qquad

(7.115)

with

{\rm x}{}^{(0)}:=x^{a}u_{a}^{(0)}\;,

(7.116)

\begin{array}[]{lcr}{\rm x}_{A}:={\rm x}\,-2i\theta^{w}\bar{\theta}^{{w}}\;,&&{\rm x}:=x^{a}u_{a}\;,\end{array}

(7.117)

\begin{array}[]{lcr}\theta^{w}:=\theta^{\alpha}w_{\alpha}\penalty\ ,&&\theta^{\bar{w}}:=\theta^{\alpha}\bar{w}_{\alpha}\penalty\ ,\end{array}

(7.118)

\begin{array}[]{lcr}\bar{\text{x}}_{A}:=\bar{{\rm x}}+2i\theta^{\bar{w}}\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\;,&&\bar{{\rm x}}:=x^{a}\bar{u}_{a}\;,\end{array}

(7.119)

\begin{array}[]{lcr}\bar{\theta}^{w}:=\bar{\theta}^{\alpha}w_{\alpha}\penalty\ ,&&\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}:=\bar{\theta}^{\alpha}\bar{w}_{\alpha}\penalty\ ,\end{array}

(7.120)

(see Appendix
I
, particularly Eqs. (
I.5
)-(
I.7
),
cf.

[
144
,
147
,
146
]
). In this analytical basis, the Lagrangian form
{\cal L}=\text{d}\tau\mathcal{L}_{\tau}
with
\mathcal{L}_{\tau}
from (
7.43
) reads

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}\mathcal{L}&=\text{d}{\rm x}^{(0)}\left(m+\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{H}}{m}\right)-ifm\;\left[\bar{{\rm x}}_{A}\,\left(1+\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\cal H}{m^{2}}\right)-\frac{4i}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\cal H}{m^{2}}\,{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}+\frac{i}{\mu^{6}}\frac{1}{m\sqrt{m}}{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\bar{\nu}\right]+\\
&\penalty\ \\
&+i\bar{f}m\left[{\rm x}_{A}\,\left(1+\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\cal H}{m^{2}}\right)+\frac{4i}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\cal H}{m^{2}}\,\theta^{w}\bar{\theta}^{{w}}+\frac{i}{\mu^{6}}\frac{1}{m\sqrt{m}}\bar{\theta}^{w}\nu\right]-\\
&\penalty\ \\
&-2i\,\left(\text{d}\theta^{\bar{w}}-ia\theta^{\bar{w}}\right)\,\bar{\theta}^{w}\,\left(m+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{H}}{m}\right)-2i\,\left(\text{d}\bar{\theta}^{w}+ia\bar{\theta}^{w}\right)\,\theta^{\bar{w}}\,\left(m+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{H}}{m}\right)-\\
&\penalty\ \\
&-2i\,\left(\text{d}\theta^{{w}}+ia\theta^{{w}}\right)\,\left(\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{H}}{m}\,\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{1}{2\sqrt{m}}\bar{\nu}\right)-2i\,\left(\text{d}\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}-ia\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\right)\,\left(\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{H}}{m}\,{\theta}^{w}-\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{1}{2\sqrt{m}}\nu\right)+\\
&\penalty\ \\
&+{1\over\mu^{6}}{\rm tr}\left(\bar{\mathbb{P}}{\rm D}_{\tau}{\mathbb{Z}}+{\mathbb{P}}{\rm D}_{\tau}\bar{\mathbb{Z}}-{i\over 8}{\rm D}_{\tau}{{\bm{\Psi}}}\,\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}+{i\over 8}{{\bm{\Psi}}}{\rm D}_{\tau}\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}\right)\penalty\ .\end{split}\end{array}

(7.121)

It is convenient to introduce the notation

\tilde{{\cal H}}=\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{H}}{m}\;,\qquad\tilde{\nu}=\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{1}{2\sqrt{m}}\nu=(\bar{\tilde{\nu}})^{*}\qquad

(7.122)

which allows the Lagrangian to be written more compactly

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}\mathcal{L}&=\text{d}{\rm x}^{(0)}\,(m+2\tilde{\mathcal{H}})-if\left[\bar{{\rm x}}_{A}(m+2\tilde{\mathcal{H}})-{4i}\tilde{\cal H}\,{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}+2i{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\bar{\tilde{\nu}}\right]+\\
&\penalty\ \\
&+i\bar{f}\left[{{\rm x}}_{A}\,(m+2\tilde{\mathcal{H}})+{4i}\tilde{\cal H}\,{\theta}^{{w}}\bar{\theta}^{{w}}+2i\bar{\theta}^{w}\tilde{\nu}\;\right]-2i\left(\text{d}\theta^{\bar{w}}-ia\theta^{\bar{w}}\right)\,\bar{\theta}^{w}\,(m+\tilde{\mathcal{H}})-\\
&\penalty\ \\
&-2i\left(\text{d}\bar{\theta}^{w}+ia\bar{\theta}^{w}\right)\,\theta^{\bar{w}}\,(m+\tilde{\mathcal{H}})-2i\,\left(\text{d}\theta^{{w}}+ia\theta^{{w}}\right)\,\left(\tilde{\mathcal{H}}\,\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}-\bar{\tilde{\nu}}\right)-\\
&\penalty\ \\
&-2i\left(\text{d}\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}-ia\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\right)\,\left(\tilde{\mathcal{H}}\,{\theta}^{w}-\tilde{\nu}\right)+{1\over\mu^{6}}{\rm tr}\left(\bar{\mathbb{P}}{\rm D}_{\tau}{\mathbb{Z}}+{\mathbb{P}}{\rm D}_{\tau}\bar{\mathbb{Z}}-{i\over 8}{\rm D}_{\tau}{{\bm{\Psi}}}\,\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}+{i\over 8}{{\bm{\Psi}}}{\rm D}_{\tau}\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}\right)\penalty\ .\end{split}\end{array}

(7.123)

Then the canonical Hamiltonian is defined by

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}\text{d}\tau H_{0}&=\text{d}{\rm x}^{(0)}p^{(0)}+\text{d}{\rm x}_{A}\bar{p}+\text{d}\bar{\rm x}_{A}p+\text{d}\theta^{w}\Pi^{\theta}_{w}+\text{d}\theta^{\bar{w}}\Pi^{\theta}_{\bar{w}}+\text{d}\bar{\theta}^{w}\bar{\Pi}^{\bar{\theta}}_{w}+\text{d}\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\bar{\Pi}^{\bar{\theta}}_{\bar{w}}+\\
&\penalty\ \\
&+ia\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}^{(0)}+if\bar{\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}}-i\bar{f}\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\text{tr}(\text{d}\mathbb{Z}\bar{\mathbb{P}})+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\text{tr}(\text{d}\bar{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{P})-\frac{i}{8\mu^{6}}\text{tr}(\text{d}{\bm{\Psi}}\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}})-\\
&\penalty\ \\
&-\frac{i}{8\mu^{6}}\text{tr}(\text{d}\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}{\bm{\Psi}})+\text{tr}(\text{d}\mathbb{A}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{A}})-\mathcal{L}_{\text{mD}0}\penalty\ .\end{split}\end{array}

(7.124)

In (
7.124
) the momenta for the bosonic coordinate functions are related to their central basis counterparts by

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}p^{(0)}:=u^{a(0)}p_{a}\penalty\ ,&&p:=-\dfrac{1}{2}u^{a}p_{a}\penalty\ ,&&\bar{p}:=-\dfrac{1}{2}\bar{u}^{a}p_{a}\end{array}

(7.125)

and have the nonvanishing Poisson brackets

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\left[p^{(0)},{\rm x}^{(0)}\right]_{\text{PB}}=-1\penalty\ ,&&\left[p,\bar{{\rm x}}_{A}\right]_{\text{PB}}=-1\penalty\ ,&&\left[\bar{p},{\rm x}_{A}\right]_{\text{PB}}=-1\penalty\ .\end{array}

(7.126)

The momenta conjugate to the fermionic coordinate functions of the analytical basis obeying

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}{}\{{\Pi}^{\theta}_{w},\theta^{w}\}_{\text{PB}}=-1\penalty\ ,&&\{{\Pi}^{\theta}_{\bar{w}},\theta^{\bar{w}}\}_{\text{PB}}=-1\penalty\ ,\\
\\
\{\bar{\Pi}^{\bar{\theta}}_{w},\bar{\theta}^{w}\}_{\text{PB}}=-1\penalty\ ,&&\{\bar{\Pi}^{\bar{\theta}}_{\bar{w}},\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\}_{\text{PB}}=-1\end{array}

(7.127)

are related to their central basis cousins by the somewhat more complicated relations

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\Pi^{\theta}_{w}:=-i\bar{w}^{\alpha}\Pi_{\alpha}+2i\bar{\theta}^{w}\bar{p}\penalty\ ,&&\Pi^{\theta}_{\bar{w}}:=iw^{\alpha}\Pi_{\alpha}-2i\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}p\penalty\ ,\\
\\
\bar{\Pi}^{\bar{\theta}}_{w}:=-i\bar{w}^{\alpha}\bar{\Pi}_{\alpha}-2i\theta^{w}\bar{p}\penalty\ ,&&\bar{\Pi}^{\bar{\theta}}_{\bar{w}}:=iw^{\alpha}\bar{\Pi}_{\alpha}+2i\theta^{\bar{w}}p\penalty\ .\end{array}

(7.128)

The covariant momenta in the analytical basis, denoted by tilde to distinguish then from their central basis counterparts, satisfy

\begin{array}[]{lll}{}[\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}^{(0)},{z}_{An}^{(M)}]_{\text{PB}}=0\;,\qquad{}[\tilde{\mathfrak{d}},{z}_{An}^{(M)}]_{\text{PB}}=0\;,\qquad{}[\bar{\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}},{z}_{An}^{(M)}]_{\text{PB}}=0\;,\end{array}

(7.129)

while

\begin{array}[]{lll}{}[{\mathfrak{d}}^{(0)},z^{M}]_{\text{PB}}=0\;,\qquad{}[{\mathfrak{d}},z^{M}]_{\text{PB}}=0\;,\qquad{}[\bar{{\mathfrak{d}}},z^{M}]_{\text{PB}}=0\;.\end{array}

(7.130)

Eq. (
7.124
) also implicitly encodes the definitions of the canonical momenta as derivatives of the Lagrangian with respect to velocities, (
7.44
), as well as the covariant momenta as derivatives with respect to Cartan forms, (
7.59
). Calculating these for our Lagrangian (
7.123
), we obtain the primary constraints (
cf.
(
7.67
)-(
7.74
))

\displaystyle\Phi^{(0)}:=p^{(0)}-(m+2\tilde{\mathcal{H}})\approx 0\penalty\ ,\qquad

(7.131)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\ \Phi:=p\approx 0\penalty\ ,\qquad

(7.132)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\Phi}:=\bar{p}\approx 0\penalty\ ,\qquad

(7.133)

\displaystyle\tilde{{\mathfrak{d}}}+{{\rm x}}_{A}\,(m+2\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}})+{4i}\tilde{{\cal H}}\,{\theta}^{{w}}\bar{\theta}^{{w}}+2i\bar{\theta}^{w}\tilde{\nu}\approx 0\;,\qquad

(7.134)

\displaystyle\bar{\tilde{{\mathfrak{d}}}}+\bar{{\rm x}}_{A}\,(m+2\tilde{\mathcal{H}})-{4i}\tilde{\cal H}\,{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}+2i{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\bar{\tilde{\nu}}\approx 0\;,\qquad

(7.135)

\displaystyle\tilde{U}:=\tilde{{\mathfrak{d}}}^{(0)}-4i\theta^{\bar{w}}\bar{\theta}^{w}\,(m+\tilde{\mathcal{H}})+4i\theta^{{w}}\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\,\tilde{\mathcal{H}}\,-2i\theta^{{w}}\bar{\tilde{\nu}}+2i\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\tilde{{\nu}}-2\tilde{{\cal B}}\approx 0\;,\qquad

(7.136)

\displaystyle\text{where}\qquad\tilde{{\cal B}}:=\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}{\cal B}:=\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}{\rm tr}\left(\bar{{\mathbb{P}}}{\mathbb{Z}}-{\mathbb{P}}\bar{{\mathbb{Z}}}+\frac{i}{8}{{\bm{\Psi}}}\bar{{{\bm{\Psi}}}}\right),\qquad

(7.137)

\displaystyle d_{w}:=\Pi^{\theta}_{w}-2i\left(\bar{\tilde{\nu}}-\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}\right)\approx 0\penalty\ ,\qquad

(7.138)

\displaystyle\qquad d_{\bar{w}}:=\Pi^{\theta}_{\bar{w}}+2i\left(m+\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}\right)\bar{\theta}^{w}\approx 0\penalty\ ,\qquad

(7.139)

\displaystyle\bar{d}_{w}:=\bar{\Pi}^{\bar{\theta}}_{w}+2i\left(m+\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}\right)\theta^{\bar{w}}\approx 0\penalty\ ,\qquad

(7.140)

\displaystyle\bar{d}_{\bar{w}}:=\bar{\Pi}^{\bar{\theta}}_{\bar{w}}-2i\left(\tilde{{\nu}}-{\theta}^{{w}}\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}\right)\approx 0\penalty\ \qquad

(7.141)

and (
7.74
)

\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{A}}:=\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot{\mathbb{A}}_{\tau}}\approx 0\penalty\ .

(7.142)

Exactly as in the central basis analysis, the requirement of preservation of this last constraint leads to the secondary constraint (
7.79
),

\mathbb{G}=\dfrac{1}{\mu^{6}}\left([\bar{\mathbb{Z}},\mathbb{P}]+[\mathbb{Z},\bar{\mathbb{P}}]-\frac{i}{4}\{{\bm{\Psi}},\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}\}\right)\approx 0

(7.143)

and, as a result, the canonical Hamiltonian vanishes in the weak sense

H_{0}\approx 0\;.

(7.144)

That is, the total Hamiltonian is a linear combination of constraints or, more precisely, of the first class constraints.

The set of first class constraints
includes Eqs. (
7.131
), (
7.138
), (
7.141
),

\displaystyle\Phi^{(0)}\approx 0\;,\qquad d_{w}\approx 0\;,\qquad\bar{d}_{\bar{w}}\approx 0\;,\qquad

(7.145)

and the sum of (
7.136
) with a linear combination of the second class constraints (
7.132
), (
7.133
), (
7.139
) and (
7.140
),

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}\tilde{\tilde{U}}^{(0)}&:=\tilde{U}^{(0)}-2\text{x}_{A}\bar{p}+2\bar{\text{x}}_{A}p-\bar{\theta}^{w}\bar{d}_{w}+\theta^{\bar{w}}d_{\bar{w}}=\\
&\penalty\ \\
&=\tilde{{\mathfrak{d}}}^{(0)}-2\text{x}_{A}\bar{p}+2\bar{\text{x}}_{A}p-\bar{\theta}^{w}\bar{\Pi}^{\bar{\theta}}_{w}+\theta^{\bar{w}}\Pi^{\theta}_{\bar{w}}+4i\theta^{w}\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\tilde{\mathcal{H}}-2i\theta^{w}\bar{\tilde{\nu}}+2i\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\tilde{\nu}-2\tilde{{\cal B}}\approx 0\penalty\ .\end{split}\end{array}

(7.146)

It also includes (
7.142
) (reflecting the gauge nature of the 1d gauge field) and the Gauss constraint (
7.143
). The remaining constraints are second class.

In what follows, we will not need the complete Poisson bracket algebra of the constraints. Instead of calculating it directly, we can use their relations to the central basis constraints, together with the knowledge of the algebra of these latter. In the case of first class constraint (
7.99
) and second class constraints (
7.104
), this relation is just a coincidence,

\Phi^{(0)}:=p_{a}u^{(0)}-(m+2\tilde{\cal H})=p^{(0)}-(m+2\tilde{{\cal H}})\penalty\ ,

(7.147)

\Phi:=\Phi_{a}u^{a}=-2p\penalty\ ,

(7.148)

\bar{\Phi}:=\Phi_{a}\bar{u}^{a}=-2\bar{p}\penalty\ .

(7.149)

The same is true for the Gauss constraints (
7.143
) and for (
7.142
). However, for the remaining constraints, the relations are nontrivial:

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}U^{(0)}:=\mathfrak{d}^{(0)}-2\tilde{\mathcal{B}}&=\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}^{(0)}-2\text{x}_{A}\bar{p}+2\bar{\text{x}}_{A}p-\theta^{w}\Pi^{\theta}_{w}-\bar{\theta}^{w}\bar{\Pi}^{\bar{\theta}}_{w}+\theta^{\bar{w}}\Pi^{\theta}_{\bar{w}}+\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\bar{\Pi}^{\bar{\theta}}_{\bar{w}}-2\tilde{\mathcal{B}}=\\
&\penalty\ \\
&=\tilde{\tilde{U}}{}^{(0)}-\theta^{w}d_{w}+\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\bar{d}_{\bar{w}}\penalty\ ,\end{split}\end{array}

(7.150)

\bar{w}^{\alpha}d_{\alpha}:=\bar{w}^{\alpha}(\bar{\Pi}_{\alpha}+ip_{a}(\gamma^{a}\bar{\theta})_{\alpha}-m\bar{\theta}_{\alpha})+2\bar{\tilde{\nu}}=id_{w}-\Phi^{(0)}\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\penalty\ ,

(7.151)

w^{\alpha}\bar{d}_{\alpha}:=w^{\alpha}({\Pi}_{\alpha}+ip_{a}(\gamma^{a}\theta)_{\alpha}+m\theta_{\alpha})-2\tilde{\nu}=-i\bar{d}_{\bar{w}}+\Phi^{(0)}\theta^{w}\penalty\ ,

(7.152)

w^{\alpha}d_{\alpha}:=w^{\alpha}(\bar{\Pi}_{\alpha}+ip_{a}(\gamma^{a}\bar{\theta})_{\alpha}-m\bar{\theta}_{\alpha})=-id_{\bar{w}}+4p\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}+\Phi^{(0)}\bar{\theta}^{w}\penalty\ ,

(7.153)

\bar{w}^{\alpha}\bar{d}_{\alpha}:=\bar{w}^{\alpha}({\Pi}_{\alpha}+ip_{a}(\gamma^{a}\theta)_{\alpha}+m\theta_{\alpha})=i\bar{d}_{w}-4p\theta^{w}-\Phi^{(0)}\theta^{\bar{w}}\penalty\ ,

(7.154)

\mathfrak{d}=\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}+2(\text{x}^{(0)}+i\theta^{\bar{w}}\bar{\theta}^{w}+i\theta^{w}\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}})p+(\text{x}_{A}+2i\theta^{w}\bar{\theta}^{w})p^{(0)}+\bar{\theta}^{w}\bar{\Pi}^{\bar{\theta}}_{\bar{w}}+\theta^{w}\Pi^{\theta}_{\bar{w}}\penalty\ ,

(7.155)

\bar{\mathfrak{d}}=\bar{\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}}+2(\text{x}^{(0)}-i\theta^{\bar{w}}\bar{\theta}^{w}-i\theta^{w}\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}})\bar{p}+(\bar{\text{x}}_{A}-2i\theta^{\bar{w}}\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}})p^{(0)}+\theta^{\bar{w}}\Pi^{\theta}_{w}+\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\bar{\Pi}^{\bar{\theta}}_{{w}}\penalty\ .

(7.156)

Using these expressions, the algebra of constraints in the analytical basis can be read from Table
7.1
and Eqs. (
7.106
)-(
7.111
). Below we will need only part of this algebra, summarized (essentially) in Table
7.2
.

7.3.2

Resolving bosonic second class constraints

Actually, as we have already stated, we do not require the complete algebra of the constraints in detail. Instead, we can observe that the bosonic second class constraints in the analytic basis, Eqs. (
7.133
), (
7.134
) and (
7.132
), (
7.135
), are explicitly resolved (in Dirac’s terminology
[
174
]
). This means that
in these pairs of constraints, one element (namely (
7.134
) and (
7.135
)) can be rewritten to express the coordinates
{\rm x}_{A}
and
\bar{\rm x}_{A}
in terms of other variables, while the conjugate elements ((
7.133
) and (
7.132
)) imply the vanishing of the momenta conjugate to these dependent coordinates. In such cases, the procedure of changing the Poisson brackets by Dirac brackets can be replaced by just setting momenta to zero in the strong sense

\bar{p}=0\;,\qquad p=0\;,

(7.157)

and substituting
{\rm x}_{A}
and
\bar{\rm x}_{A}
in all places when these appear by their expressions obtained from solving (
7.134
) and (
7.135
),

\displaystyle{{\rm x}}_{A}=-\frac{\tilde{{\mathfrak{d}}}+{4i}\tilde{{\cal H}}\,{\theta}^{{w}}\bar{\theta}^{{w}}+2i\bar{\theta}^{w}\tilde{\nu}}{m+2\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}}\;,\qquad\bar{{\rm x}}_{A}=-\frac{\bar{\tilde{{\mathfrak{d}}}}-{4i}\tilde{\cal H}\,{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}+2i{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\bar{\tilde{\nu}}}{m+2\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}}\;.\qquad

(7.158)

Fortunately, in our case,
{\rm x}_{A}
and
\bar{{\rm x}}_{A}
do not contribute to other constraints. Therefore, the above prescription is tantamount to reducing the configuration space by omitting the directions corresponding to these coordinates. Equivalently, one can omit (
7.134
) and (
7.135
) the set of constraints, thereby converting (
7.133
) and (
7.132
) into first class constraints. Then,
{{\rm x}}_{A}
and
\bar{{\rm x}}_{A}
can be gauged away using the symmetries generated by (
7.133
) and (
7.132
), thus arriving at the same result as described above.

For the fermionic second class constraints, since the canonically conjugate constraints are also complex conjugate, they can be quantized via the Gupta-Bleuler method. At the classical level, this is equivalent to omitting one of the conjugate constraints (say,
d_{\bar{w}}
), thereby effectively promoting its conjugate
\bar{d}_{w}
to a first class constraint.

Closed algebra of the “effective first class constraints” of the mD0 system. For compactness, we have omitted the lines and columns corresponding to the Gauss constraint (

\tilde{{\mathbb{G}}}:=\frac{1}{m}{\mathbb{G}}\,\qquad\tilde{{\bm{\Psi}}}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\;{\bm{\Psi}}\,\qquad\bar{\tilde{{\bm{\Psi}}}}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\;\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}\;.\qquad

(7.159)

7.2
are proportional to the Gauss law constraint and thus vanish when this is taken into account

7.4

Quantization of multiple D0-brane system and its field theory equations

Quantization implies replacing the phase space variables with operators, whose commutators (and anticommutators) are determined by the Poisson brackets of their classical counterparts, according to Dirac’s prescription

[\ldots,\ldots\}_{\text{PB}}\quad\mapsto\quad\frac{1}{i}\,[\ldots,\ldots\}\penalty\ .

(7.160)

In the supercoordinate representation, the bosonic and fermionic configuration space variables maintain their
(quasi-)
classical description, i.e. they are ordinary numbers (
c
-number) or elements of a Grassmann algebra (
a
-number), respectively. Their momenta are represented by differential operators such that the Dirac prescription (
7.160
) holds.

In particular, for the bosonic and fermionic center of mass coordinate functions and their momenta, quantization proceeds by replacing them with the corresponding coordinates and differential operators as

\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\hat{\text{x}}^{(0)}=\text{x}^{(0)}\penalty\ ,&&\hat{\text{x}}_{A}=\text{x}_{A}\penalty\ ,&&\hat{\bar{\text{x}}}_{A}=\bar{\text{x}}_{A}\penalty\ ,\\
\\
\hat{p}^{(0)}=-i\partial_{\text{x}^{(0)}}\penalty\ ,&&\hat{\bar{p}}_{A}=-i\partial_{\text{x}_{A}}\penalty\ ,&&\hat{p}_{A}=-i\partial_{\bar{\text{x}}_{A}}\penalty\ ,\end{array}

(7.161)

and

\begin{array}[]{rcrclcl}\hat{\theta}^{w}=\theta^{w}\penalty\ ,&&\hat{\theta}^{\bar{w}}=\theta^{\bar{w}}\penalty\ ,&&\hat{\bar{\theta}}^{w}=\bar{\theta}^{w}\penalty\ ,&&\hat{\bar{\theta}}^{\bar{w}}=\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\penalty\ ,\\
\\
\hat{\Pi}^{\theta}_{w}=-i\partial_{\theta^{w}}\penalty\ ,&&\hat{\Pi}^{\theta}_{\bar{w}}=-i\partial_{\theta^{\bar{w}}}\penalty\ ,&&\hat{\bar{\Pi}}^{\bar{\theta}}_{w}=-i\partial_{\bar{\theta}^{w}}\penalty\ ,&&\hat{\bar{\Pi}}^{\bar{w}}_{\bar{\theta}}=-i\partial_{\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}}\penalty\ ,\end{array}

(7.162)

where the notation

\begin{array}[]{ccrcrcr}&&\partial_{\text{x}^{(0)}}=\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\text{x}^{(0)}}\penalty\ ,&&\partial_{\text{x}_{A}}=\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\text{x}_{A}}\penalty\ ,&&\partial_{\bar{\text{x}}_{A}}=\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\text{x}}_{A}}\penalty\ ,\\
\\
\partial_{\theta^{w}}=\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{w}}\penalty\ ,&&\partial_{\theta^{\bar{w}}}=\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{\bar{w}}}\penalty\ ,&&\partial_{\bar{\theta}^{w}}=\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\theta}^{w}}\penalty\ ,&&\partial_{\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}}=\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}}\end{array}

(7.163)

has been used. For the complex spinors frame variables, the covariant momenta are expressed in terms of covariant derivatives

\mathbb{D}^{(0)}=\bar{w}_{\alpha}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\bar{w}_{\alpha}}-{w}_{\alpha}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial{w}_{\alpha}}\penalty\ ,\qquad\mathbb{D}=w_{\alpha}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\bar{w}_{\alpha}}\penalty\ ,\qquad\bar{\mathbb{D}}=\bar{w}_{\alpha}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial{w}_{\alpha}}\penalty\ ,

(7.164)

so that

\begin{array}[]{lcrcr}&&\hat{w}_{\alpha}=w_{\alpha}\penalty\ ,&&\hat{\bar{w}}_{\alpha}=\bar{w}_{\alpha}\penalty\ ,\\
\\
\hat{\mathfrak{d}}{}^{(0)}=-i\mathbb{D}^{(0)}\penalty\ ,&&\hat{\mathfrak{d}}=-i\mathbb{D}\penalty\ ,&&\hat{\bar{\mathfrak{d}}}=-i\bar{\mathbb{D}}\penalty\ .\end{array}

(7.165)

For the bosonic matrix fields, quantization in coordinate representation is standard

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{i}{}^{j}=\mathbb{Z}_{i}{}^{j}\penalty\ ,&&\hat{\bar{\mathbb{Z}}}_{i}{}^{j}=\bar{\mathbb{Z}}_{i}{}^{j}\penalty\ ,\\
\\
\hat{\bar{\mathbb{P}}}_{i}{}^{j}=-i\mu^{6}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\mathbb{Z}_{j}{}^{i}}\penalty\ ,&&\hat{\mathbb{P}}_{i}{}^{j}=-i\mu^{6}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\mathbb{Z}}_{j}{}^{i}}\penalty\ ,\end{array}

(7.166)

while for the fermionic matrix variables obeying

\left\{\hat{{\bm{\Psi}}}_{i}{}^{j},\hat{\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}}_{k}{}^{l}\right\}=\;4\mu^{6}(\delta^{l}_{i}\delta_{k}^{j}-\dfrac{1}{N}\delta^{j}_{i}\delta^{l}_{k})\penalty\ ,\qquad\left\{\hat{{\bm{\Psi}}}^{j}_{i},\hat{{{\bm{\Psi}}}}_{k}^{l}\right\}=0\;,\qquad\left\{\hat{\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}}^{j}_{i},\hat{\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}}_{k}^{l}\right\}=0\;\qquad

(7.167)

one employs the holomorphic representation (analogous to that for the Heisenberg algebra), in which the creation operator is represented by differentiation with respect to classical counterpart of the annihilation operator,

\hat{{\bm{\Psi}}}^{j}_{i}={\bm{\Psi}}^{j}_{i}\penalty\ ,\qquad\hat{\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}}^{j}_{i}=\;4\mu^{6}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial{\bm{\Psi}}^{i}_{j}}\penalty\ .

(7.168)

Then, the quantum counterparts of the SYM supercurrents and Hamiltonian are represented by the following differential operators

\hat{\tilde{\nu}}=\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{1}{2\sqrt{m}}\hat{\nu}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{m}}\text{tr}\left(-i{\bm{\Psi}}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\overline{\mathbb{Z}}}+4[\mathbb{Z},\bar{\mathbb{Z}}]\dfrac{\partial}{\partial{\bm{\Psi}}}\right)\penalty\ ,\qquad

(7.169)

\hat{\bar{\tilde{\nu}}}=\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{1}{2\sqrt{m}}\hat{\bar{\nu}}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{m}}\text{tr}\left(-4i{\mu^{6}}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\mathbb{Z}}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial{\bm{\Psi}}}+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}{\bm{\Psi}}[\mathbb{Z},\bar{\mathbb{Z}}]\right)\penalty\ ,\qquad

(7.170)

\hat{\tilde{{\cal H}}}=\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}{m}=\frac{1}{m}{\rm tr}\left(-\mu^{6}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\mathbb{Z}}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\mathbb{Z}}}+8i\mu^{6}\bar{\mathbb{Z}}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial{\bm{\Psi}}}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial{\bm{\Psi}}}+\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}[{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{\mathbb{Z}}]^{2}-{i\over 2}\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}{\mathbb{Z}}{{\bm{\Psi}}}{{\bm{\Psi}}}\right)\penalty\ ,

(7.171)

and the U
(1)
generator acting on the matrix variables is

\hat{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}}=\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\hat{\mathcal{B}}=i\text{tr}\left(\bar{\mathbb{Z}}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\mathbb{Z}}}-\mathbb{Z}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\mathbb{Z}}+\frac{1}{2}{\bm{\Psi}}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial{\bm{\Psi}}}\right)\penalty\ .

(7.172)

The state vector is represented by a function dependent on the configuration space coordinates
54
54

54

To be precise, we should also say that
\Xi
depends on the 1d gauge field
{\mathbb{A}}_{\tau}
, but then we should subject it to the quantum constraint (
7.74
) which reads

\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\dot{\mathbb{A}}_{\tau}}\Xi=0
and implies just independence on
{\mathbb{A}}_{\tau}
. We allowed ourselves to “straighten” the presentation by omitting this stage.
,

\Xi=\Xi\left({\rm x}^{(0)},\bar{w},w;\theta^{w},\theta^{\bar{w}},\bar{\theta}{}^{w},\bar{\theta}{}^{\bar{w}};{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{\mathbb{Z}};{\bm{\Psi}}\right)\penalty\ .

(7.173)

The physical states are represented by the state vectors obeying equations obtained as quantum version of the effective first class constraints which read

\left[-i\partial_{\text{x}^{(0)}}-\left(m+2\hat{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}}\right)\right]\Xi=0\penalty\ ,

(7.174)

i\hat{\tilde{\tilde{U}}}^{(0)}\Xi=\left(\mathbb{D}^{(0)}-\bar{\theta}^{w}\partial_{\bar{\theta}^{w}}+\theta^{\bar{w}}\partial_{\theta^{\bar{w}}}-4\theta^{w}\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\hat{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}}+2\theta^{w}\hat{\bar{\tilde{\nu}}}-2\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\hat{\tilde{\nu}}-2i\hat{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}}-q\right)\Xi=0\penalty\ ,

(7.175)

\left[\partial_{\theta^{w}}+2\left(\hat{\bar{\tilde{\nu}}}-\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\hat{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}}\right)\right]\Xi=0\penalty\ ,

(7.176)

\left[\partial_{\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}}+2\left(\hat{\tilde{\nu}}-\theta^{w}\hat{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}}\right)\right]\Xi=0\penalty\ ,

(7.177)

\left[\partial_{\bar{\theta}^{w}}-2\left(m+\hat{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}}\right)\theta^{\bar{w}}\right]\Xi=0\penalty\ ,

(7.178)

where
\hat{\tilde{\nu}}
,
\hat{\bar{\tilde{\nu}}}
and
\hat{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}}
are given in (
7.169
), (
7.170
) and (
7.171
), and the quantum SU
(N)
Gauss law constraint

i\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{j}_{i}\Xi=\left(\bar{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}_{i}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}_{j}}-\bar{\mathbb{Z}}^{j}_{k}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\mathbb{Z}}_{k}^{i}}+\mathbb{Z}^{k}_{i}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\mathbb{Z}^{k}_{j}}-\mathbb{Z}^{j}_{k}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\mathbb{Z}_{k}^{i}}+{\bm{\Psi}}^{k}_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bm{\Psi}}_{j}^{k}}-{\bm{\Psi}}^{j}_{k}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial{\bm{\Psi}}_{k}^{i}}\right)\Xi=0\penalty\ ,

(7.179)

Notice that, when calculating the quantum generator of SU
(N)
transformations
{\mathbb{G}}
in (
7.179
) we have to use the
“qp” ordering
for the commutators and anticommutators of matrices operators. Specially, we have

\begin{array}[]{l}[{\bar{\mathbb{Z}}},{\mathbb{P}}]{}_{i}{}^{j}\mapsto-i\mu^{6}\left(\bar{\mathbb{Z}}_{i}{}^{k}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\mathbb{Z}}_{j}{}^{k}}-\bar{\mathbb{Z}}_{k}{}^{j}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\mathbb{Z}}_{k}^{i}}\right)\penalty\ ,\qquad[{\mathbb{Z}},{\bar{\mathbb{P}}}]{}_{i}{}^{j}\mapsto-i\mu^{6}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{i}{}^{k}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\mathbb{Z}_{j}{}^{k}}-\mathbb{Z}_{k}{}^{j}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\mathbb{Z}_{k}{}^{i}}\right)\penalty\ ,\\
\\
\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \{{{\bm{\Psi}}},{\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}}\}{}_{i}{}^{j}\mapsto 4\mu^{6}\left({\bm{\Psi}}_{i}^{\,k}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial{\bm{\Psi}}_{j}^{\,k}}-{\bm{\Psi}}_{k}^{\,j}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial{\bm{\Psi}}_{k}^{\,i}}\right)\penalty\ .\end{array}

(7.180)

Without this ordering, the resulting expression for
{\mathbb{G}}
would fail to be traceless as it should be. Thus, Eqs. (
7.174
), (
7.175
), (
7.176
), (
7.177
), (
7.178
) and (
7.179
) define the (super)field theory corresponding to the simplest 3D counterpart of multiple D
0
-brane system in the configuration superspace (
7.173
).

It is convenient to allow the state vector to depend also on additional complex bosonic coordinates
{\rm x}_{A}
and its c.c.
\bar{\rm x}_{A}
,

\Xi=\Xi\left({\rm x}^{(0)},{\rm x}_{A},\bar{\rm x}_{A},\bar{w},w;\theta^{w},\theta^{\bar{w}},\bar{\theta}{}^{w},\bar{\theta}{}^{\bar{w}};{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{\mathbb{Z}};{\bm{\Psi}}\right)\penalty\ ,

(7.181)

but we impose the additional constraint removing this dependence:

\dfrac{\partial}{\partial{\rm x}_{A}}\Xi=0\;,\qquad\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\rm x}_{A}}\Xi=0\;.

(7.182)

This formulation is equivalent to the original one, but it makes explicit that the state vector can be viewed as dependent on the central basis coordinates,

\Xi=\tilde{\Xi}\left(x^{a},\bar{w},w;\theta^{\alpha},\bar{\theta}{}^{\alpha};{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{\mathbb{Z}};{\bm{\Psi}}\right)\penalty\ .

(7.183)

This perspective is useful for interpreting the resulting (super)field theory equations and write this in a more conventional form, at least in some particular cases such as
N=1
case discussed in the next subsection. In particular, in this simplest case for
N=1
, the equations can be reformulated in terms of derivatives
55
55

55

Here, we have introduced the notation

\partial_{\alpha}=\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{\alpha}}\penalty\ ,\qquad\bar{\partial}_{\alpha}=\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\theta}^{\alpha}\penalty\ .}

(7.184)

and covariant derivatives in the central basis

\displaystyle\partial_{a}=u_{a}^{(0)}\partial_{{\rm x}^{(0)}}+u_{a}\partial_{{\rm x}_{A}}+\bar{u}_{a}\partial_{\bar{{\rm x}}_{A}}\;,\qquad

(7.185)

\displaystyle\text{D}_{\alpha}=\partial_{\alpha}+i(\gamma^{a}\bar{\theta})_{\alpha}\partial_{a}=w_{\alpha}\left(\partial_{\theta^{w}}+i\bar{\theta}{}^{\bar{w}}\partial_{{\rm x}^{(0)}}\right)+\bar{w}_{\alpha}\left(\partial_{\theta^{\bar{w}}}+4i\bar{\theta}{}^{\bar{w}}\partial_{\bar{\rm x}_{A}}+i\bar{\theta}{}^{{w}}\partial_{{\rm x}^{(0)}}\right)\;,

(7.186)

\displaystyle\bar{\text{D}}_{\alpha}=\bar{\partial}_{\alpha}+i(\gamma^{a}{\theta})_{\alpha}\partial_{a}=w_{\alpha}\left(\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\theta}{}^{w}}+4i{\theta}{}^{w}\partial_{{\rm x}_{A}}+i{\theta}{}^{\bar{w}}\partial_{{\rm x}^{(0)}}\right)+\bar{w}_{\alpha}\left(\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\theta}}{}^{\bar{w}}+i{\theta}{}^{w}\partial_{{\rm x}^{(0)}}\right)\;,

(7.187)

and the spinor frame variables can be integrated out, thus arriving to a more conventional description of field theory in terms of usual (central basis) spacetime coordinates.

But before considering the
N=1
case, let us note that, in the general case of arbitrary
N>1
, it is possible to change the variables in the relative motion sector by decomposing the matrix fields

\begin{array}[]{lcccr}\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{i}^{j}=\sqrt{2}\hat{{Z}}^{I}T_{Ii}^{\penalty\ j}\penalty\ ,&&\hat{\bar{\mathbb{Z}}}_{i}^{j}=\sqrt{2}\hat{\bar{{Z}}}^{I}T_{Ii}^{\penalty\ j}\penalty\ ,&&\hat{{\bm{\Psi}}}_{i}^{j}=\sqrt{2}\hat{{\Psi}}^{I}T_{Ii}^{\penalty\ j}\penalty\ ,\\
\\
\hat{\bar{\mathbb{P}}}_{i}^{j}=\sqrt{2}\hat{\bar{{P}}}^{I}T_{Ii}^{\penalty\ j}\penalty\ ,&&\hat{\mathbb{P}}_{i}^{j}=\sqrt{2}\hat{{P}}^{I}T_{Ii}^{\penalty\ j}\penalty\ ,&&\hat{\bar{{\bm{\Psi}}}}_{i}^{j}=\sqrt{2}\hat{\bar{{\Psi}}}^{I}T_{Ii}^{\penalty\ j}\penalty\ ,\end{array}

(7.188)

on the SU
(N)
generators
T_{Ii}^{\penalty\ j}
, with
I,J,K=1,\ldots N^{2}-1\;
, which satisfy

\begin{array}[]{c}[T_{I},T_{J}]=if_{IJK}T_{K}\;,\qquad f_{IJK}=f_{[IJK]}\;,\qquad f_{IJK}f_{I^{\prime}JK}=N\delta_{II^{\prime}}\;,\\
\\
{\rm tr}(T_{I}T_{J})=\dfrac{1}{2}\delta_{IJ}\;,\qquad T_{I}T_{J}=\dfrac{1}{2}\left(\dfrac{1}{N}\delta_{IJ}{\mathbb{1}}+\left(d_{IJK}+if_{IJK}\right)T_{K}\right)\;,\qquad d_{IJK}=d_{(IJK)}\;,\\
\\
T_{I}{}_{i}{}^{j}T_{I}{}_{k}{}^{l}=\dfrac{1}{2}\left(\delta_{i}{}^{l}\delta{}_{k}{}^{j}-\frac{1}{N}\delta{}_{i}{}^{j}\delta{}_{k}{}^{l}\right)\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad(T_{I}T_{I})_{i}{}^{j}=C_{F}\delta{}_{i}{}^{j}\;,\qquad C_{F}=\dfrac{N^{2}-1}{N}\end{array}

(7.189)

and some other relations which can be found, e.g., in
[
178
]
. In the simplest case of SU
(2)
(for
N=2
),

\text{SU}(2):\qquad T_{I}{}_{i}{}^{j}=\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{I}{}_{i}{}^{j}\;,\qquad f_{IJK}=\epsilon_{IJK}\;,\qquad d_{IJK}=0\;,\qquad I,J,K=1,2,3.

(7.190)

The coefficients in (
7.188
) are chosen in such a way that the nontrivial commutation relations of the
basic operators have (almost) standard form

\begin{array}[]{rcccl}[\hat{{P}}^{I},\hat{\bar{Z}}^{J}]=-i\mu^{6}\delta^{IJ}\penalty\ ,&&[\hat{\bar{P}}^{I},\hat{Z}^{J}]=-i\mu^{6}\delta^{IJ}\penalty\ ,&&\{\hat{{\Psi}}{}^{I},\hat{\bar{{\Psi}}}{}^{J}\}=\;4\mu^{6}\delta^{IJ}\penalty\ .\end{array}

(7.191)

Thus, in coordinate representation, the operators are realized by

\begin{array}[]{lcrcclcl}\hat{{Z}}^{I}={Z}^{I}\penalty\ ,&&\hat{\bar{{P}}}^{I}=-i\mu^{6}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial{Z}^{I}}\penalty\ ,&&&\hat{\bar{{Z}}}^{I}=\bar{{Z}}^{I}\penalty\ ,&&\hat{{P}}^{I}=-i\mu^{6}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\bar{{Z}}^{I}}\penalty\ ,\\
\\
&&\hat{{\Psi}}^{I}={\Psi}^{I}\penalty\ ,&&&\hat{\bar{{\Psi}}}^{I}=\;4\mu^{6}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial{\Psi}^{I}}\penalty\ ,&&\end{array}

(7.192)

where
{Z}^{I}=({Z}^{1},{Z}^{2},\ldots,{Z}^{N^{2}-1})
are complex bosonic (
c
-number) coordinates and
{\Psi}^{I}=(\Psi^{1},\Psi^{2},\ldots,\Psi^{N^{2}-1})
are fermionic (
a
-number) coordinates,

{\Psi}^{I}{\Psi}^{J}=-{\Psi}^{J}{\Psi}^{I}\;.

(7.193)

In terms of these relative motion coordinates, the quantum generator of SU
(N)
transformations (
7.179
) reads

\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{j}_{i}=:2\hat{{G}}^{I}T^{\penalty\ j}_{Ii}=2f_{IJK}\left(\bar{{Z}}^{I}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\bar{{Z}}^{J}}+{Z}^{I}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial{Z}^{J}}+{\Psi}^{I}\frac{\partial}{\partial{\Psi}^{J}}\right)T_{Ki}{}^{j}\penalty\ ,

(7.194)

while the part of the U
(1)
generator acting on the matrix field (
7.172
) is

\hat{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}}=\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\hat{\mathcal{B}}=i\left(\bar{{Z}}^{I}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\bar{{Z}}^{I}}-{Z}^{I}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial{Z}^{I}}+\frac{1}{2}{\Psi}^{I}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial{\Psi}^{I}}\right)\penalty\ .

(7.195)

Similarly, the SYM supercharges (
7.169
), (
7.170
) take the form

\hat{\tilde{\nu}}=\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{1}{2\sqrt{m}}\hat{\nu}=-\frac{i}{2\sqrt{m}}\left({\Psi}^{I}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\bar{{Z}}^{I}}-4\sqrt{2}f_{IJK}{Z}^{I}\bar{{Z}}^{J}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial{\Psi}^{K}}\right)\penalty\ ,\qquad

(7.196)

\hat{\bar{\tilde{\nu}}}=\frac{1}{\mu^{6}}\frac{1}{2\sqrt{m}}\hat{\bar{\nu}}=-\frac{i}{2\sqrt{m}}\left(4{\mu^{6}}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial{Z}^{I}}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial{\Psi}^{I}}-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\mu^{6}}f_{IJK}{Z}^{I}\bar{{Z}}^{J}{\Psi}^{K}\right)\penalty\ ,\qquad

(7.197)

and the SYM Hamiltonian (
7.171
)

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}\hat{\tilde{{\cal H}}}&=\frac{1}{m}\left(-\mu^{6}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial{Z}^{I}}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\bar{{Z}}^{I}}-4\sqrt{2}\mu^{6}\bar{Z}^{I}f_{IJK}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial{\Psi}^{J}}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial{\Psi}^{K}}\right.-\\
&\penalty\ \\
&\left.-\dfrac{2}{\mu^{6}}f_{IJM}f_{KLM}{Z}^{I}\bar{{Z}}^{J}{Z}^{K}\bar{\mathbb{Z}}^{L}+{\sqrt{2}\over 4\mu^{6}}f_{IJK}{Z}^{I}{\Psi}^{J}{\Psi}^{K}\right)\penalty\ .\end{split}\end{array}

(7.198)

These expressions can be used in the equations (
7.174
)-(
7.178
) imposed on the state vector superfield (“wavefunction”)

\Xi=\Xi\left({\rm x}^{(0)},{\rm x}_{A},\bar{{\rm x}}_{A},\bar{w},w;\theta^{w},\theta^{\bar{w}},\bar{\theta}{}^{w},\bar{\theta}{}^{\bar{w}};{{Z}}{}^{I},\bar{{Z}}{}^{I};{\Psi}^{I}\right)\penalty\ ,

(7.199)

which also satisfies (
7.182
). This is manifestly a superfield on superspace with
5+N^{2}
bosonic and
3+N^{2}
fermionic directions (specifically,
9
bosonic and
7
fermionic directions in the simplest case
N=2
).

7.5

Some properties of 3D mD0 field theory equations

7.5.1

N
=1: Field theory of single D0-brane

If we set the number
N
of D
0
-branes to unity,
N=1
, the matrix fields disappear so that the state vector becomes superfield in a center of mass superspace. This superspace is the usual superspace enlarged by spinor frame variables (Lorentz harmonic superspace in terminology of
[
144
]
)

\Xi_{0}:=\Xi|_{N=1}=\tilde{\Xi}_{0}\left(x^{a};\theta^{\alpha},\bar{\theta}{}^{\alpha};\bar{w},w\right)=\Xi_{0}\left({\rm x}^{(0)},{\rm x}_{A},\bar{{\rm x}}_{A},\bar{w},w;\theta^{w},\theta^{\bar{w}},\bar{\theta}{}^{w},\bar{\theta}{}^{\bar{w}}\right)\penalty\ .

(7.200)

The above system of equations for this states vector then reduces to

\partial_{\theta^{w}}\Xi_{0}=0\penalty\ ,\qquad\partial_{\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}}\Xi_{0}=0\penalty\ ,

(7.201)

\left[\partial_{\bar{\theta}^{w}}-2m\theta^{\bar{w}}\right]\Xi_{0}=0\penalty\ ,

(7.202)

\partial_{{\rm x}_{A}}\Xi_{0}=0\;,\qquad\partial_{\bar{\rm x}_{A}}\Xi_{0}=0\;,\qquad

(7.203)

\left(-i\partial_{\text{x}^{(0)}}-m\right)\Xi_{0}=0\penalty\ ,

(7.204)

\left(\mathbb{D}^{(0)}-\bar{\theta}^{w}\partial_{\bar{\theta}^{w}}+\theta^{\bar{w}}\partial_{\theta^{\bar{w}}}-q\right)\Xi_{0}=0\penalty\ .

(7.205)

This system of equations describes the free (super)field theory of single 3D counterpart of D
0
brane, i.e. the massive
{\cal N}=2
superparticle in
\text{D}=3
. In Appendix
I
, we show how this system is obtained directly by quantizing the massive superparticle in its spinor moving frame formulation. Of course, the quantization of such a simple system in the standard formulation described by the de Azcárraga-Lukierski action (
7.8
) is much simpler; however, in our case, the more complicated quantization makes sense as it describes
N=1
limit of our simplest counterpart for multiple D
0
-brane system, which is known presently in its spinor moving frame formulation only.

Eqs. (
7.201
) and (
7.203
) imply that the state vector superfield
\Xi
is independent of
{\rm x}_{A},\bar{{\rm x}}_{A};\theta^{w},\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}
coordinates,

\Xi_{0}=\Xi_{0}\left({\rm x}^{(0)},\bar{w},w;\theta^{\bar{w}},\bar{\theta}{}^{w}\right)\;.

(7.206)

Eqs. (
7.202
) and
(
7.204
) are easily solved by

\Xi_{0}=e^{im{\rm x}^{(0)}-2m{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\bar{\theta}^{{w}}}\,\chi({\theta}^{\bar{w}},\bar{w}_{\alpha},w_{\alpha})\;\equiv e^{im{\rm x}^{(0)}}\,(1-2m{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\bar{\theta}^{{w}})\,\chi({\theta}^{\bar{w}},\bar{w}_{\alpha},w_{\alpha})\;\qquad

(7.207)

and then (
7.205
) implies

\left({\mathbb{D}}^{(0)}+{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\partial_{{\theta}^{\bar{w}}}-q\right)\chi({\theta}^{\bar{w}},\bar{w}_{\alpha},w_{\alpha})=0\;.\qquad

(7.208)

Decomposing
\chi
in the powers of (fermionic and hence nilpotent)
{\theta}^{\bar{w}}
,

\chi({\theta}^{\bar{w}},\bar{w}_{\alpha},w_{\alpha})=\phi(\bar{w}_{\alpha},w_{\alpha})+i{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\,\xi(\bar{w}_{\alpha},w_{\alpha})

(7.209)

we find from (
7.208
) that the components of these superfields are bosonic and fermionic functions of complex normalized bosonic spinors (functions on SU
(1,1)\approx\text{SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
) with definite U
(1)
charges
q
and
(q-1)
,

\displaystyle\phi=\phi^{q}(\bar{w}_{\alpha},w_{\alpha})\;,\qquad\left({\mathbb{D}}^{(0)}-q\right)\phi^{q}(\bar{w}_{\alpha},w_{\alpha})=0\;.\qquad

(7.210)

\displaystyle\xi=\xi^{(q-1)}(\bar{w}_{\alpha},w_{\alpha})\;,\qquad\left({\mathbb{D}}^{(0)}-(q-1)\right)\xi^{(q-1)}(\bar{w}_{\alpha},w_{\alpha})=0\;.\qquad

(7.211)

These definite U
(1)
charges imply that the fields are functions on the coset SU
(1,1)/\text{U}(1)
56
56

56

More precisely, they are sections of a fiber bundle
\pi:\text{SU}(2)\mapsto\text{SU}(2)/\text{U}(1)
with structure group U
(1)
, which can be identified with Hopf fibration of
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}=\text{SU}(2)
over
{\mathbb{S}}^{2}=\text{SU}(2)/\text{U}(1)
with fiber
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}=\text{U}(1)
. However, such mathematical subtleties are beyond the scope of this thesis.
. As far as
\theta^{\bar{w}}
carries charge one with respect to U
(1)
symmetry generated by the operator in (
7.208
), the latter equation implies that the superfield
\chi
itself has definite charge
q
with respect to this U
(1)
symmetry,

\chi=\chi^{q}(\bar{w}_{\alpha},w_{\alpha};{\theta}^{\bar{w}})\;.

(7.212)

Eqs. (
7.210
) and (
7.211
) imply the following homogeneity properties of the bosonic and fermionic components under U
(1)
gauge transformations

\begin{array}[]{c}\phi^{q}(e^{-i\vartheta}\bar{w}_{\alpha},e^{i\vartheta}w_{\alpha})=e^{-iq\vartheta}\phi^{q}(\bar{w}_{\alpha},w_{\alpha})\;,\\
\\
\quad\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \xi^{q-1}(e^{-i\vartheta}\bar{w}_{\alpha},e^{i\vartheta}w_{\alpha})=e^{-i(q-1)\vartheta}\xi^{q-1}(\bar{w}_{\alpha},w_{\alpha})\;.\end{array}

(7.213)

Spacetime interpretation I

To clarify the physical meaning of these functions, let us observe that, as a result of the constraints ( Eqs. (
7.13
) and (
7.131
)-(
7.133
)), which imply
p_{a}=mu_{a}^{(0)}
and

p_{a}\gamma^{a}_{\alpha\beta}=mu_{a}^{(0)}\gamma^{a}_{\alpha\beta}=2m\bar{w}_{(\alpha}w_{\beta)}\penalty\ ,

(7.214)

we can write

\bar{w}_{\alpha}w_{\beta}=\frac{1}{2m}p_{a}\gamma^{a}_{\alpha\beta}+\frac{i}{2}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\;,\qquad p_{a}p^{a}=m^{2}\;.

(7.215)

As a result, the dependence on the product
\bar{w}_{\alpha}w_{\beta}
can be identified with a dependence on the on-shell momentum of a massive particle. For integer
q\geq 1
we can then write the following solution of Eqs. (
7.210
) and (
7.211
)

\begin{array}[]{lcr}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \phi^{q}(\bar{w},w)=\bar{w}^{\alpha_{1}}\ldots\bar{w}^{\alpha_{q}}\phi_{\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{q}}(p)|_{p^{2}=m^{2}}\;,\\
&&\qquad q\geq 1\;.\\
\xi^{q-1}(\bar{w},w)=\bar{w}^{\alpha_{1}}\ldots\bar{w}^{\alpha_{q-1}}\xi_{\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{q-1}}(p)|_{p^{2}=m^{2}}\;.\end{array}

(7.216)

According to the general spin-statistics theorem
57
57

57

Notice that in 3D, the set of possible statistics is not exhausted by the Bose-Einstein and Fermi-Dirac options and includes also anyons
[
179
,
180
,
181
]
, such as quartions
[
182
]
, which can be collected in exotic supermultiplets
[
183
]
. We will not address these possibilities in the present thesis.
, for even
q
the field
\phi^{q}(\bar{w},w)
should be bosonic and
\xi^{q-1}(\bar{w},w)
fermionic, and vice versa for odd
q
.

For
q\leq 0
, a similar solution is

\begin{array}[]{lcr}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \phi^{q}(\bar{w},w)={w}^{\alpha_{1}}\ldots{w}^{\alpha_{-q}}\phi_{\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{-q}}(p)|_{p^{2}=m^{2}}\;,\\
&&\qquad q\leq 0\;.\\
\xi^{q-1}(\bar{w},w)={w}^{\alpha_{1}}\ldots{w}^{\alpha_{-q-1}}\xi_{\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{-q-1}}(p)|_{p^{2}=m^{2}}\;.\end{array}

(7.217)

Finally, for
q=1
we have

\phi^{(1)}(\bar{w},w)=\bar{w}^{\alpha}\phi_{\alpha}(p)|_{p^{2}=m^{2}}\;,\qquad\xi^{(0)}(\bar{w},w)=\xi(p)|_{p^{2}=m^{2}}\;.

(7.218)

with fermionic
\phi_{\alpha}(p)
and bosonic
\xi(p)
.

However, since it is not manifestly clear how/whether these analytic solutions can be normalized, we will next describe the solution in terms of a different type of functions.

Spacetime interpretation II

To pass to a more standard description in terms of central basis coordinates, let us summarize the preceding discussion: the state vector superfield for a single massive 3D
{\cal N}=2
superparticle takes the form

\Xi_{0}=\Xi_{0}^{q}=e^{im{\rm x}^{(0)}}\left(\phi^{q}(\bar{w}_{\alpha},w_{\alpha})+i{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\xi^{q-1}(\bar{w}_{\alpha},w_{\alpha})-2m{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\bar{\theta}^{{w}}{\phi}{}^{q}(\bar{w}_{\alpha},w_{\alpha})\right)\;,

(7.219)

where
\phi^{q}(\bar{w}_{\alpha},w_{\alpha})
and
\xi^{q-1}(\bar{w}_{\alpha},w_{\alpha})
satisfy (
7.210
) and (
7.211
). Using (
7.116
), (
7.118
), (
7.120
) (or (
7.185
)-(
7.187
)) and acting with a covariant derivative in central basis on this superfield, we obtain

(\bar{\text{D}}_{\alpha}+im\theta_{\alpha})\Xi_{0}^{q}=0\;,

(7.220)

which can be obtained from a (generalized) dimensional reduction of the chirality constraint in
\text{D}=4
,
58
58

58

The fermionic operator on the l.h.s. of (
7.220
) is the result of the dimensional reduction of the 4D fermionic covariant derivative
\bar{\text{D}}_{\dot{\alpha}}=\bar{\partial}_{\dot{\alpha}}+i(\theta\sigma^{\underline{a}})_{\dot{\alpha}}{\partial}_{\underline{a}}
with
{\partial}_{x^{\perp}}:={\partial}_{x^{2}}=im
.
. We also have

(\partial_{a}-imu_{a}^{(0)})\Xi_{0}^{q}=0

(7.221)

which implies the Klein-Gordon equation

(\partial_{a}\partial^{a}+m^{2})\Xi_{0}^{q}=0\;.

(7.222)

Consider the leading component of the superfield (
7.219
), given by
e^{im{\rm x}^{(0)}}\phi^{q}(\bar{w}_{\alpha},w_{\alpha})
. Roughly speaking (that is, following the structure of (
7.216
)), for the case
q=0
this can be written as

\Xi_{0}^{(0)}|_{{}_{\theta=0}}=e^{im{\rm x}^{(0)}}\phi^{(0)}(\bar{w}_{\alpha},w_{\alpha})=e^{ip_{a}{x}^{a}}\phi(p)|_{p^{2}=m^{2}}\;,\qquad

(7.223)

where we used (
7.131
)-(
7.133
). In this form it becomes evident that the state vector depends on both momentum and coordinates. To obtain a conventional state vector (a “wavefunction”) in coordinate representation, we should integrate over the on-shell momentum.

In the standard textbook notation this reads

\phi(x)=\left.\int\frac{d^{2}\vec{p}}{(2\pi)^{2}p^{0}}\left(e^{ip_{a}{x}^{a}}\phi(p)+e^{-ip_{a}{x}^{a}}\phi^{*}(p)\right)\right|_{p^{0}=\sqrt{\vec{p}^{2}+m^{2}}}\;.

(7.224)

Our twistor-like representation describes only one of two terms in the integrand of the above equation, since the sign of
p^{0}
is fixed by (
7.99
). This relation also allows us to determine the expression for integrating measure in terms of moving frame and spinor moving frame variables. Indeed, it implies (see (
7.26
))

\text{d}p_{a}=imf\bar{u}_{a}-im\bar{f}u_{a}\;,\qquad

(7.225)

and, using (
7.24
),

\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{abc}\text{d}p_{b}\wedge\text{d}p_{c}=-2iu^{a(0)}\bar{f}\wedge f\;.

(7.226)

Therefore, the natural correspondence is

{\text{d}^{2}\vec{p}}\;\longleftrightarrow\;\bar{f}\wedge f

(7.227)

and the standard coordinate representation of the state vector field with
q=0
is

\phi(x)=\int\bar{f}\wedge f\,e^{imx^{a}u_{a}^{(0)}}\phi^{(0)}(\bar{w},w)\;,\qquad q=0\;,\qquad

(7.228)

where (let us recall)

u_{a}^{(0)}=\bar{w}\gamma_{a}w\;,\qquad f=w^{\alpha}\text{d}w_{\alpha}=(\bar{f})^{*}\;,\qquad\bar{w}^{\alpha}w_{\alpha}=i\;.

(7.229)

This construction can be easily generalized to the case of nonzero
q
giving

\phi_{\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{q}}(x)=\int\bar{f}\wedge f\,w_{\alpha_{1}}\ldots w_{\alpha_{q}}e^{imx^{a}u_{a}^{(0)}}\phi^{q}(\bar{w},w)\;,\qquad q>0\;,\qquad

(7.230)

and

\phi_{\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{-q}}(x)=\int\bar{f}\wedge f\,\bar{w}_{\alpha_{1}}\ldots\bar{w}_{\alpha_{-q}}e^{imx^{a}u_{a}^{(0)}}\phi^{q}(\bar{w},w)\;,\qquad q<0\;.\qquad

(7.231)

It is important to emphasize that when using expressions involving
\phi^{q}(\bar{w},w)
, we have to be careful about the class of functions we work with. In particular, we should not substitute Eqs. (
7.216
)-(
7.218
) for
\phi^{q}(\bar{w},w)
since the corresponding integrals would diverge. We will not work out the precise mathematical formulation of the problem here (see
[
144
]
and
[
184
,
185
]
for a more rigorous treatment of
\text{D}=4
case), but rather state that Eqs. (
7.230
) and (
7.231
) describe the solutions of the wave equation, assuming
\phi^{q}(\bar{w},w)
are chosen such that the integrals converge.

Similarly, the standard superfield representation of the state vector can be written as

\displaystyle\Xi_{\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{|q|}}(x,\theta,\bar{\theta})=\begin{cases}\mathop{\text{\LARGE$\int$}}\nolimits\bar{f}\wedge f\,w_{\alpha_{1}}\ldots w_{\alpha_{q}}\;e^{imx^{a}u_{a}^{(0)}-2m\theta{\bar{w}}\,\bar{\theta}w}\;\chi^{q}(\bar{w},w,\theta\bar{w})\;,\qquad q\geq 0\;,\qquad\cr\cr\mathop{\text{\LARGE$\int$}}\nolimits\bar{f}\wedge f\,\bar{w}_{\alpha_{1}}\ldots\bar{w}_{\alpha_{-q}}e^{imx^{a}u_{a}^{(0)}-2m\theta{\bar{w}}\,\bar{\theta}^{w}}\;\chi^{q}(\bar{w},w,\theta{\bar{w}})\;,\qquad q<0\;,\qquad\end{cases}

(7.232)

where
\theta\bar{w}=\theta^{\alpha}\bar{w}_{\alpha}=(\bar{\theta}w)^{*}
and
u_{a}^{(0)}=\bar{w}\gamma_{a}w
. These superfields obey

\displaystyle(\bar{\text{D}}_{\alpha}+im\theta_{\alpha})\;\Xi_{\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{|q|}}(x,\theta,\bar{\theta})=0\;,\qquad

(7.233)

(\text{D}^{\alpha}\text{D}_{\alpha}-2im\bar{\theta}^{\alpha}\text{D}_{\alpha}-m^{2}\bar{\theta}^{\alpha}\bar{\theta}_{\alpha})\;\Xi_{\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{|q|}}(x,\theta,\bar{\theta})=0\;

(7.234)

and Eq. (
7.222
)

(\partial_{a}\partial^{a}+m^{2})\;\Xi_{\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{|q|}}(x,\theta,\bar{\theta})=0\;.

(7.235)

Moreover, one can easily check that for
q\not=0
the superfield (
7.232
) obeys the following Dirac equation

\partial^{\alpha\alpha_{1}}\Xi_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\ldots\alpha_{|q|}}=-m\,\frac{q}{|q|}\Xi^{\alpha}{}_{\alpha_{2}\ldots\alpha_{|q|}}\,

(7.236)

and, for
q<0
,

(\text{D}^{\alpha}-im\bar{\theta}^{\alpha})\;\Xi_{\alpha\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{-q}}=0\penalty\ .

(7.237)

For
q>0
we do not have equation of such type, indicating that the corresponding supermultiplet is reducible.

In the case
q<0
, Eq. (
7.236
) can be derived using
\bar{\text{D}}_{\alpha}
to (
7.237
), while Eq. (
7.235
) can be obtained in a similar way from (
7.236
) or by applying
\bar{\text{D}}^{\alpha}\bar{\text{D}}_{\alpha}
to (
7.234
).

The irreducible multiplet for
q=0
contains massive complex scalar and spinor fields. Its equations can be obtained via dimensional reduction from those of massless scalar multiplet in
\text{D}=4
. Eqs. (
7.233
)-(
7.237
) describe a massive vector multiplet
(\frac{1}{2},1)
for
q=-1
, a massive supermultiplet with
3\text{d}
gravitino
(1,\frac{3}{2})
for
q-2
, a massive supergravity multiplet
(\frac{3}{2},2)
for
q=-3
, and higher spin supermultiplets for
q\leq-4
.

Thus the quantum state vector of
\text{D}=3

{\cal N}=2
superparticle can be identified with superfield in the standard
\text{D}=3

{\cal N}=2
superspace obeying Eqs. (
7.233
)-(
7.236
). This description is equivalent to that in terms of a function defined on the analytic basis Lorentz harmonic superspace (
7.200
) obeying (
7.201
)-(
7.205
).

Unfortunately, in the general case of
N>1
, the first type of description is not available (or, perhaps more precisely, is not practical). This is because the bosonic and fermionic matrix coordinates of the 3D mD
0
configurational superspace are not inert under the U
(1)
symmetry. Therefore, we have to work with the description of the second type, in terms of superfield depending on the coordinates of the analytical basis of center of mass superspace (
7.181
), satisfying the set of equations
(
7.174
)-(
7.179
).

7.5.2

Dependence on Grassmann center of mass coordinates for generic case
N>
1.

The set of fermionic equations for the state vector (
7.176
)-(
7.178
) can be solved in the general case of
N>1
. To this end, let us first observe that the commutators and anticommutators of the SYM currents simplify significantly when acting on the state vector obeying Eq. (
7.179
),

\displaystyle\hat{\tilde{\nu}}^{2}\,\Xi=0\;,\qquad\hat{\bar{\tilde{\nu}}}^{2}\,\Xi=0\;,\qquad\{\hat{\tilde{\nu}},\hat{\bar{\tilde{\nu}}}\}\,\Xi=\hat{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}}\,\Xi\penalty\ ,

(7.238)

\displaystyle{}[\hat{\tilde{\nu}},\hat{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}}]\,\Xi=0\;,\qquad{}[\hat{\bar{\tilde{\nu}}},\hat{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}}]\,\Xi=0\;,

(7.239)

which, in particular, leads to

e^{-2\theta^{w}\hat{\bar{\tilde{\nu}}}}\hat{{\tilde{\nu}}}=\hat{{\tilde{\nu}}}e^{-2\theta^{w}\hat{\bar{\tilde{\nu}}}}-2\theta^{w}\hat{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}}\penalty\ .

(7.240)

It is then straightforward to verify that Eqs. (
7.176
)-(
7.178
) admit the (formal) solution

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{split}\Xi&=e^{-2\theta^{w}\hat{\bar{\tilde{\nu}}}}e^{-2\bar{\theta}{}^{\bar{w}}\hat{{\tilde{\nu}}}}e^{+2\theta^{w}\bar{\theta}{}^{\bar{w}}\hat{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}}}e^{-2\theta^{\bar{w}}\bar{\theta}{}^{{w}}(m+\hat{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}})}\;\left({\mathfrak{B}}+\theta^{\bar{w}}{\mathfrak{F}}\right)=\\
&\penalty\ \\
&=\left(1-2\theta^{w}\hat{\bar{\tilde{\nu}}}-2\bar{\theta}{}^{\bar{w}}\hat{{\tilde{\nu}}}+2\theta^{w}\bar{\theta}{}^{\bar{w}}(\hat{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}}-2\hat{\bar{\tilde{\nu}}}\hat{{\tilde{\nu}}})-2\theta^{\bar{w}}\bar{\theta}{}^{{w}}(m+\hat{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}})+\right.\\
&\penalty\ \\
&+4\theta^{w}\theta^{\bar{w}}\bar{\theta}{}^{{w}}\hat{\bar{\tilde{\nu}}}(m+\hat{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}})+4\theta^{\bar{w}}\bar{\theta}{}^{{w}}\bar{\theta}{}^{\bar{w}}\hat{{\tilde{\nu}}}(m+\hat{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}})-\\
&\penalty\ \\
&\left.-4\theta^{w}\bar{\theta}^{\bar{w}}\theta^{\bar{w}}\bar{\theta}^{w}(\hat{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}}-2\hat{\bar{\tilde{\nu}}}\hat{\tilde{\nu}})(m+\hat{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}})\right)\;\left({\mathfrak{B}}+\theta^{\bar{w}}{\mathfrak{F}}\right)\penalty\ ,\end{split}\end{array}

(7.241)

where

{\mathfrak{B}}={\mathfrak{B}}\left(\text{x}^{(0)},\bar{w},w;{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{\mathbb{Z}};{\bm{\Psi}}\right)\;,\qquad{\mathfrak{F}}={\mathfrak{F}}\left(\text{x}^{(0)},\bar{w},w;{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{\mathbb{Z}};{\bm{\Psi}}\right)

(7.242)

are superfields of opposite statistics (one bosonic, one fermionic), depending on the spinor moving frame variables and the matrix coordinates of our enlarged superspace. They should obey

\hat{{\mathbb{G}}}_{j}{}^{i}{\mathfrak{B}}=0\;,\qquad\hat{{\mathbb{G}}}_{j}{}^{i}{\mathfrak{F}}=0\;\qquad

(7.243)

with
\hat{\mathbb{G}}_{j}{}^{i}
defined in (
7.179
) (or (
7.194
)), implying that
\mathfrak{B}
and
\mathfrak{F}
are constructed from SU
(N)
invariant combinations of the matrix fields. Moreover, they obey

\left(\mathbb{D}^{(0)}-2i\hat{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}}-q\right){\mathfrak{B}}=0\;,\qquad\left(\mathbb{D}^{(0)}-2i\hat{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}}-(q-1)\right){\mathfrak{F}}=0\;,\qquad

(7.244)

which fix their U
(1)
charges under transformations acting on the spinor frame and matrix fields

{\mathfrak{B}}={\mathfrak{B}}^{(q)}\left(\text{x}^{(0)},\bar{w},w;{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{\mathbb{Z}};{\bm{\Psi}}\right)\;,\qquad{\mathfrak{F}}={\mathfrak{F}}^{(q-1)}\left(\text{x}^{(0)},\bar{w},w;{\mathbb{Z}},\bar{\mathbb{Z}};{\bm{\Psi}}\right)\;.\qquad

\underline{\Xi}{}^{q^{\prime}}(Z^{I},\bar{Z}^{I},\Psi^{I})=\mathfrak{f}{}^{q^{\prime}}(Z,\bar{Z})+\Psi^{I}\mathfrak{f}{}_{I}^{q^{\prime}-1}(Z,\bar{Z})+\frac{1}{2}\Psi^{I}\Psi^{J}\mathfrak{f}{}_{IJ}{}^{q^{\prime}-2}(Z,\bar{Z})+\Psi^{\wedge 3}\mathfrak{f}{}{}^{q^{\prime}-3}(Z,\bar{Z})

Thus, in this chapter we have carried out the covariant quantization of the simplest 3-dimensional counterpart of the 10D mD
0
system. Through this process, we derived the equations of the 3D mD
0
field theory, which is defined on the superspace extended by additional matrix bosonic and fermionic coordinates. We have also studied some particular solutions of these equations and have discussed their properties.

These results represent an initial step toward the development of the (super)field theory of 10D mD
0
-brane system which, as we expect, will shed a new light on structure and properties of String Theory

Conclusions and Outlook

Ho sfilato via la mia vita miei desideri. Se tu potessi

risalire il mio cammino, li troveresti uno dopo l’altro,

incantati, immobili, fermati lì per sempre a segnare

la rotta di questo viaggio strano che a nessuno

mai ho raccontato se non a te.

Novecento: Un monologo

by Alessandro Baricco.

While a complete supersymmetric description of the
N
nearly coincident D
p
-branes system is still far from being achieved, this thesis takes some steps toward such a description, both in classical and quantum domains, beginning with the system of multiple D
0
-branes and laying the groundwork to guide possible future progress in this direction.

Motivated by the result presented in
[
94
]
, where it was shown that the dimensional reduction of the 11D single M
0
-brane reproduces the action for a 10D single D
0
-brane, it was natural to expect that the action for the 10D mD
0
-brane (mD
0
) should arise as the dimensional reduction of some 11D action; namely, a non-Abelian generalization of the single M
0
-brane system. This idea was pursued by the supervisor of this thesis, who constructed such a non-Abelian 11D action in flat superspace
[
126
]
by extending to the M
0
-brane action in its spinor moving frame formulation (which is described in chapter
2
) with an additional sector of matrix fields and interacting terms involving center of energy variables.

However, a previous attempt to derive such 10D action for mD
0
system from this 11D mM
0
system was not successful
[
130
]
; instead a candidate action for such 10D mD
0
system was constructed in
[
130
]
by coupling of 1d reduction of the 10D SU
(N)
SYM to the supergravity induced by the embedding of the center of mass worldline into the 10D type IIA superspace.

This unsuccessful attempt of reduction of mM
0
action (along other arguments previously discussed in chapter
1
) served as the starting point for the investigation presented in this thesis. Basing on the idea that the 10D mD
0
action should have an 11D origin, and wishing first to approach the problem on a simpler example, in chapter
3
we have constructed the action (
3.26
), describing the 4D nAmW counterpart of the 11D mM
0
system in flat superspace. This toy model, which was explicitly constructed from first principles, is doubly supersymmetric (that is, it is invariant under both target superspace supersymmetry and local worldline supersymmetry), a characteristic property expected for
p
-branes and multiple
p
-brane systems in String/M-theory. As such, it provides a simplified but concrete framework for approaching the problem of the 11D origin of the 10D mD
0
action.

In chapter
4
, the dimensional reduction of the 4D nAmW action (
3.26
) was performed and thus a 3D counterpart of mD
0
action was obtained. But moreover, the study there showed (following
[
3
]
) the existence of a set of
\text{D}=3

\mathcal{N}=2
candidate actions (
4.72
) for describing the lower dimensional counterpart of the 10D mD
0
system. All the member of this set exhibit the properties expected for such a 3D counterpart: it includes all bosonic and fermionic fields that one anticipates from considering the very low energy gauge fixed description given by the U
(N)
SYM action
[
103
]
and the known actions for single D
p
-branes
[
99
,
100
,
92
,
93
,
94
,
104
]
. Moreover, it is doubly supersymmetric, i.e. invariant under both
\text{D}=3

\mathcal{N}=2
target superspace supersymmetry and under local worldline supersymmetry, generalizing the
\kappa
-symmetry of the single D
0
-brane (in the irreducible form characteristic for the spinor moving frame formulation
[
168
]
). The generic form of the action of this set includes an arbitrary positive definite function
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H}/\mu^{6})
of the SYM Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
, constructed from the bosonic and fermionic matrix fields describing the relative motion of the constituents of the 3D mD
0
system (we roughly call it “relative motion Hamiltonian”). A key feature is that the system remains doubly supersymmetric regardless of the specific choice of this positive definite function
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H}/\mu^{6})
. A representative of this set of actions (
4.72
), which can be obtained directly via the dimensional reduction of the 4D nAmW action (
3.26
), corresponds to a particular choice of
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H}/\mu^{6})
given in (
4.71
).

Following the line developed in chapter
4
, we have obtained in chapter
5
a (set of) nonlinear action(s) for the dynamical system of 10D multiple D
0
-branes (
5.27
). Notably, this action possesses all the properties expected for the description of mD
0
system in flat target superspace. Specifically, it is doubly supersymmetric which means that it possesses both target space supersymmetry and worldline supersymmetry, the latter generalizing the
\kappa
-symmetry of the action of single D
0
-brane. This property ensures that the ground state of the dynamical system preserves half of the target space supersymmetry, and thus is a stable
1/2
BPS state, the property expected for mD
0
-brane system. Moreover, as in the 3D counterpart, the generic candidate 10D mD
0
action (
5.27
) includes a positive definite function
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H}/\mu^{6})
, and remarkably, the system remains doubly supersymmetric regardless of the choice of this
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H}/\mu^{6})
. Curiously enough, the simplest candidate action proposed in
[
130
]
can be recovered by choosing
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H}/\mu^{6})=m
, with
m
being the center of mass parameter which coincides with the mass parameter in a single D
0
-brane action use to describe the center of mass motion of mD
0
system.

We have derived the complete set of equations of motion from this action for any
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H}/\mu^{6})
, showing that the center of mass dynamics formally coincides with the dynamics for single D
0
-brane, but with an effective mass

\mathfrak{M}=m+\frac{2}{\mu^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{M}}

expressed in terms of
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H}/\mu^{6})
. The gauge invariant relative motion equations are complicated, but we have shown that they imply
\text{d}\mathcal{H}=0
, which is the Noether identity manifesting the reparametrization symmetry (1d general coordinate invariance), ensuring
\mathfrak{M}
is conserved,
\text{d}\mathfrak{M}=0
.

Another interesting result obtained in this chapter
5
is that, in a suitable gauge (fixed on the fields from center of mass sector and on 1d SU
(N)
gauge field), the relative motion equations simplify and reveal an interesting relation with the equations of
\mathcal{N}=16

\text{d}=1
SU
(N)
SYM theory. This relation, valid for any positive
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H}/\mu^{6})
is not a gauge equivalence; it links (equations of relative motion of the) models with different
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H}/\mu^{6})
, and thus different mass
\mathfrak{M}
, via Eq. (
5.73
).

Through this relation, we find that all supersymmetric bosonic solutions of the relative motion sector are in one-to-one correspondence with SUSY solutions of the SYM model. The associated BPS conditions reduce to a single equation
\mathcal{H}=0
, implying
\mathfrak{M}=m
, which means that the effective mass
\mathfrak{M}
of the configurations described by these supersymmetric solutions are given by center of mass parameter
m
. In other words, for any positive function
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H}/\mu^{6})
, the BPS spectrum of our mD
0
model essentially coincides (in the relative motion sector) with the vacuum set of the maximally supersymmetric
\mathcal{N}=16

\text{d}=1
SU
(N)
SYM theory.

The above correspondence also allows to study the non-supersymmetric solutions of the mD
0
equations using known SYM solutions (see Appendix
E
for an example).

The origin of the unexpected multiplicity of massive
p=0
supersymmetric objects founds in our study remains as an open question
60
60

60

Notice that the zoo of theses objects includes, besides our mD
0
models with positive definite function
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H}/\mu^{6})
, the elegant
0
-brane model of
[
114
]
with arbitrary function of relative motion variables
\bar{\mathcal{M}}=\bar{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbb{X}^{i},{\bm{\Psi}}_{q})
.
. However, our study establishes a notable link between the gauge fixed equations of the mD
0
model (
5.27
) for any
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H}/\mu^{6})
and the maximally supersymmetric SYM equations, suggesting a possible reason behind this multiplicity.

In chapter
6
, we revealed the
\text{D}=11
origin of one representative of the family of candidates actions (
5.27
) for the 10D mD
0
-brane system. Specifically, we showed that the member of (
5.27
) with the particular form of the positive definite function

\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H}/\mu^{6})=\dfrac{m}{2}+\sqrt{\dfrac{m^{2}}{4}+\dfrac{\mathcal{H}}{\mu^{6}}}

can be derived by the dimensional reduction of the 11D multiple M
0
-brane system.

Finally, in chapter
7
, we have initiated the program of quantum description of the 10D mD
0
-brane system beginning by its quantization which should result in a superfield theory in superspace extended by bosonic and fermionic matrix coordinates. Since the quantization of the 10D system turned out to be quite complicated, we have begun by quantization of a toy model given by the simplest 3D counterpart of mD
0
system, described by the action (
4.72
) with the choice of positive definite function
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H}/\mu^{6})=m
.

First we have developed the Hamiltonian formulation of the model. The calculation of canonical momenta for both the center of mass variables (including spinor moving frame) and the SU
(N)
gauge field led to the identification of primary constraints. The SU
(N)
gauge field, while carrying no physical degrees of freedom, serves as a Lagrange multiplier for the non-Abelian Gauss law, which appears as the only secondary constraint in this system. As expected from reparametrization invariance, the canonical Hamiltonian vanishes in the weak sense (i.e. when constraints are imposed). Then, we separated the constraints into first class (generating gauge symmetries) and second class ones (split on canonically conjugate pairs). However, the nontrivial Poisson brackets algebra of these first and second class constraints made powerful modern methods, like BRST quantization, difficult to apply. Surprisingly, even the Gupta-Bleuler approach failed for the bosonic sector, leaving the Dirac brackets method as the only way for quantizing this simplest 3D mD
0
system.

In order to simplify the quantization, we moved to the so-called analytical coordinate basis
of the center of mass superspace, where the bosonic second class constraints are effectively resolved, allowing us to remove the “unphysical” coordinates from the phase space
without passing (explicitly) to the Dirac brackets. To deal with the fermionic second class constraints, we applied the Gupta-Bleuler (or more precisely, generalized conversion) method, reducing the system to a set of effective first class constraints that, upon quantization, can be imposed on the quantum state vector.

By imposing the quantum constraints on the state vector
\Xi
in the (generalized) coordinate representation, we derived the field theory equations describing the simplest 3D counterpart of the quantum mD
0
-brane system. This theory is defined on the superspace extended by traceless
N\times N
bosonic
\mathbb{Z}=(\bar{\mathbb{Z}})^{\dagger}
and fermionic
{\bm{\Psi}}
,
\bar{\bm{\Psi}}
matrices, together with the center of mass sector which can be identified with coordinates (coordinate functions) of Lorentz harmonic
\text{D}=3

\mathcal{N}=2
superspace. This superspace is parametrized by spinor moving frame variables and the usual coordinates
x^{a},\penalty\ \theta^{\alpha},\penalty\ \bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}
, taken in the analytical basis (e.g.,
{\rm x}^{(0)}=x^{a}\bar{w}\gamma_{a}w\,
,
\,\bar{\theta}{}^{w}=\bar{\theta}{}^{\alpha}w_{\alpha}
, etc.). Even for
N=1
, corresponding to the single D
0
-brane case, the resulting field theory has an unusual structure. We have discussed this case in detail, showing how the equations for the state vector superfield looks like in the central basis of Lorentz harmonic superspace,
\Xi=\Xi(x^{a},\theta,\bar{\theta};\bar{w},w)
, and how one can pass to the standard 3D field theory equations and solutions in spacetime. This central basis description is not available (not practical) for
N>1
.

For the general
N>1
case, we have presented the formal solution of this system of
equations describing the dependence of the state vector on the fermionic center of mass coordinates. We have also investigated BPS-like solutions in the relative motion sector, where this sector decouples from the center of mass, describing configurations similar to the ground states of the BFSS matrix model at finite
N
, as studied in
[
190
,
191
,
187
,
192
,
193
,
188
,
189
]
. For
N=2
case with SU
(2)
gauge symmetry, we obtained field theory equations describing the ground state of the
\text{D}=3

\mathcal{N}=2
SU
(2)
SYM theory in Schrödinger-type representation
61
61

61

See
[
186
]
and refs. therein for studies of 3D YM model in Schrödinger representation.
. Following the approach of
[
187
]
, we also discussed the asymptotic behaviour of these solutions.

The results obtained in this thesis open the door to many directions for further research in the quest for a complete description of multiple D
p
-branes system. In particular, let us mention

•

the construction of the Hamiltonian mechanics and quantization of the 10D mD
0
-brane system, starting from the single D
0
-brane case as well as a search for generalization of our mD
0
action for the case of mD
p
-branes with
p\geq 1
, beginning from
p=1
.

•

This (relatively) simplest case is intriguing in particular because it could help to identify which candidate in the 10D type IIA
0
-brane zoo truly describes the mD
0
system, given that it should be related to mD
1
by T-duality transformations. Such a study could also deepen our understanding of the nature and implications of T-duality.

In summary, the results and research directions outlined in this thesis aim to enhance the understanding of multiple D
p
-brane systems and help establish a foundation for uncovering deeper connections within the structure of String/M-theory.

The Road goes ever on and on down from the door

where it began. Now far ahead the Road has gone,

and I must follow, if I can, pursuing it with eager feet,

until it joins some larger way where

many paths and errands meet. And whither then?

I cannot say.

The Lord of the Rings

by J. R. R. Tolkien.
